Static Bank

Permanent static knowledge for bank PO, regulatory body and government exams: polity, RBI, tax, regulators, committees, schemes, days and older events.

★ must-learn   ☆ good-to-know   | card numbers restart in each section

1. Constitution, Polity and Amendments

Basics for beginners: how to read this section

The Constitution is India's supreme law. It is divided into Parts (themes), Parts contain numbered Articles (the rules), and Schedules at the end hold long lists.

Fundamental Rights (Part III) can be enforced in court; Directive Principles (Part IV) and Fundamental Duties (Part IVA) guide the State and citizens but are not enforceable in court.

Government has three organs: Executive runs, Legislature (Parliament: Lok Sabha, Rajya Sabha and the President) makes laws, Judiciary interprets them.

The Constitution can be changed only by Amendment under Article 368; the Supreme Court has said the Basic Structure cannot be changed (Kesavananda Bharati, 1973).

Reading order: Basics (Parts, Schedules) → Rights, Principles, Duties → Union and State machinery → Emergency and Amendment → cases and amendments.

1.1 Constitution at a glance

★ Basic facts

The Constitution is the supreme law that sets up India's government and fixes citizens' rights; these are the dates, bodies and counts about how it was made.

  • Adopted 26 Nov 1949 → in force 26 Jan 1950 (Constitution Day = 26 Nov)
  • Drafting Committee chair → Dr B R Ambedkar | Constituent Assembly president → Dr Rajendra Prasad
  • Original: 395 Articles, 22 Parts, 8 Schedules → now 25 Parts (Part VII repealed), 12 Schedules, about 448 numbered Articles
  • Preamble amended once → 42nd Amendment 1976: added Socialist, Secular, Integrity

Which amendment changed the Preamble?

One Preamble change = 42nd, the "mini-Constitution".

1.2 All Parts

★ Parts and Article ranges

The Constitution is divided into Parts, and each Part is a group of numbered Articles on one theme; this table is the map showing which Articles sit in which Part.

PartSubjectArticles
IUnion and its territory1–4
IICitizenship5–11
IIIFundamental Rights12–35
IVDirective Principles (DPSP)36–51
IVAFundamental Duties (42nd)51A
VThe Union52–151
VIThe States152–237
VIIRepealed (7th Amendment)–
VIIIUnion Territories239–242
IXPanchayats (73rd)243–243O
IXAMunicipalities (74th)243P–243ZG
IXBCooperative Societies (97th)243ZH–243ZT
XScheduled and Tribal Areas244–244A
XIUnion–State relations245–263
XIIFinance, Property, Contracts, Suits264–300A
XIIITrade, Commerce, Intercourse301–307
XIVServices under Union and States308–323
XIVATribunals (42nd)323A–323B
XVElections324–329A
XVISpecial provisions: SC/ST/classes330–342A
XVIIOfficial Language343–351
XVIIIEmergency provisions352–360
XIXMiscellaneous361–367
XXAmendment of the Constitution368
XXITemporary, Transitional, Special provisions369–392
XXIIShort title, commencement, Hindi text, repeals393–395

XVIII = Emergency | XX = Amendment (Art 368) | XII = money.

Memory tricks Master story of the Constitution (read top to bottom)
  • Nation: Part I (1–4) Union and territory = "What is India?" → Part II (5–11) Citizenship = "Who belongs?"
  • People: III (12–35) Rights = what people get → IV (36–51) DPSP = what government should do → IVA (51A) Duties = what people should do. Order: Rights → Principles → Duties
  • Government: V (52–151) Union = President, PM, Parliament, SC → VI (152–237) States = Governor, CM, Legislature, HC. V = Delhi, VI = States
  • Local: VIII (239–242) UT → IX (243–243O) Panchayat → IXA (243P–243ZG) Municipality → IXB (243ZH–243ZT) Co-op. UT → Village → City → Society
  • Special areas: X (244–244A) Scheduled and Tribal
  • Centre–State: XI (245–263) Legislative/administrative → XII (264–300A) Finance → XIII (301–307) Trade. Power → Money → Business
  • Administration: XIV (308–323) Services → XIVA (323A–323B) Tribunals. Jobs → Disputes
  • Democracy and justice: XV (324–329A) Elections → XVI (330–342A) Special classes → XVII (343–351) Language
  • Emergency: XVIII (352–360) → 352–360 = Emergency zone
  • The machinery at the end: XIX Misc (361–367) → XX Amendment (368) → XXI Transition (369–392) → XXII End (393–395). Misc → Change → Transition → End

Super-chain (one breath): Country → Citizen → Rights → Policy → Duties → Union → State → UT → Panchayat → Municipality → Co-op → Tribal → Relations → Finance → Trade → Services → Tribunal → Election → Special Classes → Language → Emergency → Misc → Amendment → Transition → End.

Part VII is missing from the chain because it was repealed (7th Amendment), so the chain jumps from State (VI) to UT (VIII).

Parts I–VI: "Union Citizens Rights Direct Duties; Union States" = Union, Citizenship, Rights, Directives, Duties (IVA), Union (V), States (VI).

Part IX family: 73 → 74 → 97 = Panchayat → Municipality → Cooperative = IX, IXA, IXB.

Parts XIV–XX: "Services Tribunals Elect Special Language, Emergency Misc. Amend" = XIV Services, XIVA Tribunals, XV Elections, XVI Special, XVII Language, XVIII Emergency, XIX Misc, XX Amendment.

1.3 All 12 Schedules

★ Schedules

Schedules are lists attached at the end of the Constitution for details too long for the Articles, such as states, oaths and languages.

No.Content
1States and Union Territories
2Salaries and emoluments: President, Governors, Speakers, judges, CAG
3Forms of oaths and affirmations
4Rajya Sabha seat allocation by State/UT
5Administration of Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes
6Tribal Areas in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram
7Union / State / Concurrent Lists (98 / 59 / 52)
8Languages (22)
9Land reform and other laws protected from judicial review (1st Amendment)
10Anti-defection (52nd)
11Panchayat powers, 29 subjects (73rd)
12Municipality powers, 18 subjects (74th)

11th = Panchayat = 29 | 12th = Municipality = 18 (the bigger number goes with the earlier amendment, 73rd).

  • 8th Schedule growth → 14 original; Sindhi (21st, 1967); Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali (71st, 1992); Bodo, Dogri, Maithili, Santhali (92nd, 2003)
Memory tricks

1–6 "SEO-RAT": States, Emoluments, Oaths, Rajya Sabha, Areas (Scheduled), Tribal areas.

7–12 "Lazy Lions Leave Dogs; Pandas Mourn": Lists, Languages, Land, Defection, Panchayat, Municipality.

1.4 Part III: Fundamental Rights (Arts 12–35)

★ Six rights, Article ladder

Fundamental Rights are freedoms guaranteed against the State and enforceable in court; they come in six groups, each with its own Articles.

RightArtWhat
General12, 1312 = "State" defined; 13 = laws violating FRs are void
Equality14Equality before law, equal protection
15No discrimination (religion, race, caste, sex, place of birth)
16Equal opportunity in public employment
17Untouchability abolished
18Titles abolished
Freedom19Six freedoms: speech; assembly; association, unions, cooperative societies; movement; residence; profession
20Protection in conviction (no double jeopardy, no self-incrimination)
21Life and personal liberty
21AEducation, age 6–14 (86th)
22Protection against arrest and detention
Against Exploitation23No trafficking, no forced labour
24No child labour (under 14) in hazardous work
Religion25Freedom of conscience, practise, propagate
26Manage religious affairs
27No tax to promote a religion
28Religious instruction in educational institutions
Cultural and Educational29Protection of minorities' language and culture
30Minorities can set up educational institutions
Remedies32Move Supreme Court directly ("heart and soul", Ambedkar)
Misc33, 34, 35Armed forces; martial law; Parliament's power to legislate on FRs
  • Art 31 (property) repealed by 44th Amendment 1978 → now Art 300A (legal right)
Memory tricks

Equality (14–18) = "L-D-J-U-T": 14 Law, 15 Discrimination, 16 Jobs, 17 Untouchability, 18 Titles.

Freedom (19–22): 19 speaks (six freedoms), 20 convicts (protection after conviction), 21 lives (life and liberty), 22 arrests. 21A is the school gate (age 6–14).

Exploitation (23–24): 23 = adults (trafficking, forced labour), 24 = children (under 14, hazardous work).

Religion (25–28): 25 Pray, 26 Manage, 27 No tax, 28 School.

Culture (29–30): 29 Culture is protected, 30 Colleges can be set up by minorities. 32 = the only remedy door (go straight to the Supreme Court).

Ladder 5-4-2-4-2-1: Equality 5 Arts (14–18), Freedom 4 (19–22), Exploitation 2 (23–24), Religion 4 (25–28), Cultural-Educational 2 (29–30), Remedies 1 (32).

Six freedoms of 19(1) = "SAAMRP": Speak, Assemble, Associate, Move, Reside, Profess.

★ Writs (Art 32 SC, Art 226 High Courts)

Writs are court orders used to enforce Fundamental Rights; you can go straight to the Supreme Court (Art 32) or a High Court (Art 226).

WritMeaningUse
Habeas Corpus"produce the body"Illegal detention
Mandamus"we command"Public officer to do duty
ProhibitionstopHigher court tells lower court to stop a proceeding
Certiorari"to be certified"Quash a lower court's order
Quo Warranto"by what authority"Challenge right to hold public office

Art 32 = SC, Fundamental Rights only. Art 226 = HC, FRs and other legal rights, so wider.

Memory tricks

"Ha Ma Pro Cer Quo". Prohibition prevents (during), Certiorari cancels (after).

1.5 Part IV: DPSP (Arts 36–51) and Part IVA

★ DPSP, article by article

Directive Principles are goals the State should follow when making policy; unlike Fundamental Rights, you cannot take the State to court for not meeting them.

  • Art 36 = "State" defined | Art 37 = not enforceable in court, yet fundamental in governance
ArtContentGroup
38Social order for welfare, reduce inequalitySocialist
39(a) livelihood; (b) resources for common good; (c) no wealth concentration; (d) equal pay for equal workSocialist
39AFree legal aid (42nd)Socialist
40Village panchayatsGandhian
41Work, education, public assistanceSocialist
42Just and humane work, maternity reliefSocialist
43Living wage; cottage industriesGandhian
43AWorkers' participation in management (42nd)Socialist
43BCooperative societies (97th)Gandhian
44Uniform Civil CodeLiberal
45Early childhood care, under 6 (86th)Liberal
46SC/ST and weaker sectionsGandhian
47Nutrition, public health, prohibitionGandhian
48Agriculture, animal husbandry, cow slaughterGandhian
48AEnvironment, forests, wildlife (42nd)Liberal
49Protect monumentsLiberal
50Separate judiciary from executiveLiberal
51International peaceLiberal

42nd added 39A, 43A, 48A ("A-A-A": legal Aid, workers' participation, environment). Group split follows the standard textbook classification.

Memory tricks

Gandhian = 40, 43, 43B, 46, 47, 48: "Village, Cottage, Cooperative, Weaker, Prohibition, Cow".

Liberal = 44, 45, 48A, 49, 50, 51: "UCC, Early, Environment, Monuments, Judiciary, Peace".

★ Fundamental Duties (Art 51A, Part IVA)

Fundamental Duties are things the Constitution expects citizens to do; like the Directive Principles they are not enforced by courts.

  • 42nd Amendment 1976 (Swaran Singh Committee) → 10 duties
  • 11th added by 86th Amendment 2002 → parent/guardian provides education to child aged 6–14
  • Idea borrowed from the USSR constitution

1.6 Part V: The Union (Arts 52–151)

★ Executive, Parliament, judiciary

The Union government has three organs: the Executive runs the country, Parliament makes laws, and the judiciary interprets them; the table gives the Articles for each.

BlockArtKey points
President52–6254 election; 56 term (5 years); 61 impeachment; 62 filling vacancy; 72 pardon; 123 ordinance
Vice-President63–7064 ex-officio Chair of Rajya Sabha
Council of Ministers74, 7574 aid and advise; 75 PM appointed, ministers ≤ 15% of House (91st)
Attorney General76Union's top law officer
Parliament79–12279 composition; 80 Rajya Sabha (max 250 = 238 + 12 nominated); 81 Lok Sabha (max 550 = 530 states + 20 UTs)
Terms83RS permanent (1/3 retire every 2 years); LS 5 years
Speaker93Chosen by Lok Sabha
Quorum / disqualification / privileges100 / 102 / 105Quorum = 1/10th of House
Joint sitting108Speaker presides
Money Bill110Lok Sabha only
Budget to Financial Bills112–117See mnemonic
Supreme Court124–147124 establishment; 129 court of record; 136 SLP; 141 binding law; 143 advisory
CAG148–151148 appointment; 149 duties; 151 audit reports

72 = pardon | 74 = aid and advise | 76 = Attorney General | 123 = President's ordinance.

Memory tricks

112 (like the emergency number) → Budget, then count up: 112 Budget → 113 Demands for grants → 114 Appropriation Bill → 115 Supplementary grants → 116 Vote on Account → 117 Financial Bills.

President: 52-53-54 = Exists, Executive power, Elected. 61 = impeachment.

★ Money Bill vs Financial Bill

Bills about tax and Government spending are treated differently from ordinary Bills, with a weaker role for the Rajya Sabha; the table shows where Money Bills and the two types of Financial Bills differ.

Money Bill (Art 110)Financial Bill I (117(1))Financial Bill II (117(3))
Introduced inLS onlyLS onlyEither House
President's recommendationYesYesNeeded only for consideration
Certified bySpeaker––
Rajya Sabha roleRecommend only within 14 days; LS may rejectFull powersFull powers
Joint sittingNoYes, on deadlockYes, on deadlock
★ Parliamentary committees and sessions

Parliament does much of its detailed checking through small committees; this card lists the main ones and how Parliament's sessions run.

CommitteeMembersSinceKey points
PAC (Public Accounts Committee)22 (15 LS + 7 RS)1921Examines CAG audit reports and appropriation accounts; chair from the opposition by convention since 1967; a Minister cannot be a member; one-year term
Estimates Committee30 (Lok Sabha only)1950Called the "continuous economy committee"; examines estimates, suggests economies; chair from the ruling party
COPU (Committee on Public Undertakings)22 (15 LS + 7 RS)1964Examines public sector undertakings (PSUs); set up on the Krishna Menon Committee recommendation
DRSCs (Departmentally Related Standing Committees)24 committees, 31 members each (21 LS + 10 RS)1993Scrutinise demands for grants and Bills of each ministry; one-year term; Ministers cannot be members
Government Assurances15 in each House1953Tracks promises made by Ministers on the floor
Subordinate Legislation15 in each House1953Checks rules and regulations framed under Acts
Privileges15 in each House1952Examines breaches of privilege
Business Advisory15 in each House1952Allots time for business; chaired by Speaker / Chairman
EthicsLS: 15; RS: 10LS 2000; RS 1997Examines ethics complaints against members
  • Sessions: summoned by the President (Art 85); gap between two sittings must not exceed 6 months → at least two sessions a year
  • Three usual sessions: Budget (longest), Monsoon, Winter
  • Quorum = one-tenth of total members (Art 100); joint sitting on deadlock → Art 108, presided by the Lok Sabha Speaker (not for Money Bills or Constitution Amendment Bills)

Which financial committee has only Lok Sabha members, and how many?

Memory tricks

Numbers: PAC 22, COPU 22, Estimates 30 (LS only); DRSC 31 × 24. "PAC-COPU twins at 22; Estimates alone at 30."

1.8 Parts VIII–XIII: UTs, local bodies, relations, finance

★ UTs, Panchayat, Municipality, Cooperatives

Besides the Union and States, India has Union Territories and elected local bodies (panchayats, municipalities and cooperatives), each added by a constitutional amendment.

  • Part VIII (239–242): UTs; 239AA = Delhi (69th Amendment, NCT)
  • Part IX: Panchayat (73rd; in force 24 Apr 1993) → Gram Sabha, 3 tiers, 5-year term, State Election Commission (243K), State Finance Commission (243I)
  • Part IXA: Municipality (74th; in force 1 Jun 1993)
  • Part IXB: Cooperatives (97th) → after SC (Rajendra N Shah, 2021) valid only for multi-state cooperative societies

Panchayati Raj Day = 24 April. Panchayat has its own State EC and State FC.

Memory tricks

Chain: Part VIII = UT → IX = Village → IXA = Town → IXB = Society. Amendments in the same order: 73 Village, 74 Town, 97 Society.

Rural before urban: Panchayat came into force 24 Apr 1993 (Panchayati Raj Day), Municipality 1 Jun 1993.

☆ Centre–State relations (Part XI, Arts 245–263)
  • 246 = distribution of legislative power (Seventh Schedule); 248 = residuary power → Union
  • 249 = Rajya Sabha (2/3 present and voting) can empower Parliament to legislate on a State List subject
  • 253 = implementing treaties; 256–257 = executive control
  • 262 = inter-state water disputes; 263 = Inter-State Council
Memory tricks

246 Divide (who legislates) → 248 Residue (leftover → Union) → 249 Rajya Sabha helps Parliament (2/3 present and voting) → 253 Treaties → 256–257 Orders to States → 262 Water disputes → 263 Council.

Read the numbers as one rising ladder: 246 – 248 – 249 – 253 – 256 – 262 – 263.

★ Finance (Part XII, Arts 264–300A)

This Part decides who can collect taxes and spend money, how the Centre and States share them, and who checks the accounts.

ArtSubject
265No tax except by law
266Consolidated Fund of India / States
267Contingency Fund
268–272Taxes: levy, collection, sharing
275Grants-in-aid
246A / 269A / 279AGST power / inter-state GST / GST Council (101st)
280Finance Commission (every 5 years)
282Discretionary grants
292, 293Borrowing: Union, States
300ARight to property (legal right)
Memory tricks

265 tax by Law → 266 Consolidated → 267 Contingency (5-6-7 climb). 280 = Finance Commission.

1.9 Parts XIV–XXII

☆ Services and tribunals
  • 308–314 Union/State services; 311 protection against dismissal; 312 All India Services
  • 315–323 UPSC / State PSC; 320 PSC functions; 323A/B tribunals (42nd)
★ Elections (Part XV, Arts 324–329A)

This Part sets up the Election Commission and the basic rules for elections, including the right of every adult citizen to vote.

  • 324 Election Commission | 325 no exclusion from electoral roll | 326 adult suffrage, age 18 (61st) | 327, 328 law-making | 329 bar on court interference in electoral matters
Memory tricks

324 Commission → 325 No one is left out of the roll → 326 Vote at 18 → 327–328 Make laws → 329 Courts stay out.

★ Special provisions (Part XVI, Arts 330–342A)

These Articles give reserved seats and special protection to Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes and other groups, with the bodies that watch over them.

ArtSubject
330 / 332SC/ST reservation in Lok Sabha / State Assemblies
334Reservation sunset, extended to 2030 (104th)
335Claims of SC/ST in services
338 / 338A / 338BNCSC / NCST / NCBC (89th, 102nd)
340Commission to investigate backward classes
341 / 342Scheduled Castes / Scheduled Tribes lists
342ASEBCs (105th)
Memory tricks

338 → A → B = SC, ST, BC (alphabetical).

☆ Official Language (Part XVII, Arts 343–351)
  • 343 Hindi (Devanagari) = Union language | 344 Commission | 345–347 State languages | 348 English in courts | 351 development of Hindi
Memory tricks

343 Hindi is the Union language → 344 Commission → 345–347 States (three articles) → 348 English in courts → 351 Hindi to be developed.

★ Emergency (Part XVIII, Arts 352–360)

Emergency provisions let the Union take extra powers in a national crisis or when a State government cannot function; the table shows the three types and the Articles.

ArtSubjectUsed?
352National Emergency (war, external aggression, armed rebellion)1962, 1971, 1975
355Union's duty to protect states–
356President's Rule (state machinery failure)Many times
358, 359Effect on Fundamental Rights–
360Financial EmergencyNever
Memory tricks

352 National (3-5-2), 356 State (3-5-6), 360 Finance (3-6-0). 44th replaced "internal disturbance" with "armed rebellion".

★ Amendment procedure (Part XX, Art 368)

Article 368 says how the Constitution can be changed; the more a change affects the federal structure, the bigger the majority and approval needed.

TypeUsed forMajority
SimpleNew states (Art 3), UT laws, citizenship (outside Art 368)Simple majority
SpecialMost amendments, FRs, DPSPMajority of total membership and 2/3 of present and voting
Special + statesFederal features: President's election, SC, HC, Seventh Schedule, states' representation, Art 368Special majority + half of state legislatures
Memory tricks

Things touching states need states: "President, Executive powers, SC/HC, Seventh Schedule, Representation, Art 368".

☆ Special provisions for states (Part XXI, Arts 369–392)
  • 370 → J&K special status, abrogated 5 Aug 2019
ArtState
371Maharashtra, Gujarat
371ANagaland
371BAssam
371CManipur
371D / 371EAndhra Pradesh and Telangana / central university in AP

371K (proposed, not enacted)Ladakh: Home Ministry proposal of 9 Sep 2026 for a directly elected UT-level body on land, culture, language, forests and environment, linked to Art 240
Memory tricks

"Many Naga Assamese Marry Andhra Sikh Mizo Aruna Goan Kar": Maharashtra-Gujarat, Nagaland, Assam, Manipur, AP, Sikkim, Mizoram, Arunachal, Goa, Karnataka.

★ Important Constitutional Amendments

Each Constitutional Amendment changed one part of the Constitution; this table lists the ones most often asked, with the year and what changed.

No.YearWhat changedArticle / Schedule

Which amendment introduced GST, anti-defection or the EWS quota?

73 = Panchayat = 11th Sch (29) | 74 = Municipality = 12th Sch (18). GST trio: 101 → 246A (power) → 279A (Council). 42nd added 39A, 43A, 48A. The tens: 10% EWS (103rd), 10th Schedule (52nd), 15% ministers (91st).

Memory tricks

Group by theme, not by number:

  • Rights and property: 1st (Ninth Schedule), 24th (FRs can be amended), 42nd (Mini-Constitution), 44th (property out of FRs)
  • Parliament and politics: 31st (545 seats), 52nd (anti-defection), 61st (vote at 18), 91st (ministers ≤ 15%), 106th (women's quota)
  • Map changes: 7th (States), 36th (Sikkim), 69th (Delhi), 100th (land boundary)
  • Local government: 73rd Village, 74th Town, 97th Society
  • Languages in the Eighth Schedule: 21st Sindhi, 71st Konkani–Manipuri–Nepali, 92nd Bodo–Dogri–Maithili–Santhali
  • Money and social justice: 101st GST, 102nd NCBC, 103rd EWS, 104th seats to 2030, 105th SEBC list

Odd numbers 69 and 91 and 101: 69 Delhi, 91 Ministers, 101 GST.

1.11 Landmark cases

★ Judgments and doctrines

Court judgments have set the limits of what Parliament and the Government can do; these cases are the ones that shaped Fundamental Rights and the Basic Structure idea.

CaseYearHeld
Champakam Dorairajan1951Communal reservation struck → led to 1st Amendment
Golaknath1967Parliament cannot amend FRs (later overruled)
Kesavananda Bharati1973Basic Structure doctrine (13-judge bench)
Maneka Gandhi1978Art 21 procedure must be fair, just, reasonable
Minerva Mills1980Basic structure reaffirmed; harmony of FRs and DPSP
Kihoto Hollohan1992Anti-defection law valid
Indra Sawhney199250% reservation cap; creamy layer
S R Bommai1994Art 356 subject to judicial review; floor test
Vishaka1997Guidelines on sexual harassment at workplace
I R Coelho20079th Schedule laws after 24 Apr 1973 are reviewable
Shreya Singhal2015Sec 66A, IT Act struck down
NJAC case201599th Amendment struck down
Puttaswamy2017Privacy a Fundamental Right under Art 21 (9 judges)
Navtej Johar2018Sec 377 partly decriminalised
Rajendra N Shah2021Part IXB valid only for multi-state cooperative societies
ADR (Electoral Bonds)2024Electoral bonds scheme struck down
Memory tricks

Basic structure chain: Golaknath says No (1967) → Kesavananda says Basic Structure (1973) → Minerva Mills confirms (1980).

1.12 Citizenship, census, tribal areas, bodies and civilian honours

★ Citizenship (Part II, Arts 5–11)

Part II says who is a citizen of India and how citizenship begins, with the rules for people who came from Pakistan at Partition.

ArtProvision
5Citizen at commencement: domiciled in India and born here, or a parent born here, or ordinarily resident for 5 years
6Migrants from Pakistan: before 19 Jul 1948 (citizen on arrival); after → registration
7Migrants to Pakistan after 1 Mar 1947 who returned on a permit
8Persons of Indian origin living abroad: register via consulate
9Voluntarily acquiring foreign citizenship → loses Indian citizenship
10Continuance of citizenship
11Parliament may regulate citizenship by law → Citizenship Act, 1955
Citizenship Act 1955Detail
Acquisition (5 modes)Birth, Descent, Registration, Naturalisation, Incorporation of territory
Loss (3 ways)Renunciation, Termination (voluntarily taking another citizenship), Deprivation (by Centre: fraud, disloyalty, etc.)
Birth26 Jan 1950 – 30 Jun 1987: born in India = citizen; from 1 Jul 1987 one parent must be a citizen; from 3 Dec 2004 the other parent must not be an illegal migrant
Descent (abroad)Father a citizen (to 10 Dec 1992); either parent from 10 Dec 1992; register at consulate within 1 year
Naturalisation12 months continuous residence + 11 of preceding 14 years; 5 years for CAA 2019 groups
Amendments1986 (birth rules); 2003 (defined "illegal migrant"; barred them); 2005 (OCI category); 2015 (PIO and OCI merged into OCI cardholder); 2019 CAA
CAA 2019Hindus, Sikhs, Buddhists, Jains, Parsis, Christians from Afghanistan, Bangladesh, Pakistan who entered by 31 Dec 2014; residence needed 11 → 5 years
  • Single citizenship (unlike the US federal model); dual citizenship not allowed; illegal migrants cannot become citizens
  • Citizenship is a Union List subject → only Parliament legislates
Memory tricks

Acquire = BDRNI (Birth, Descent, Registration, Naturalisation, Incorporation). Lose = RTD (Renunciation, Termination, Deprivation).

☆ Part X: Scheduled and Tribal Areas (Fifth vs Sixth Schedule)
Fifth ScheduleSixth Schedule
Article244(1)244(2) and 275(1)
AreaScheduled Areas in 10 states: Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Chhattisgarh, Gujarat, Himachal Pradesh, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Odisha, RajasthanTribal Areas in 4 states: Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram
Who declaresPresident declares Scheduled Areas (after consulting the Governor)Governor organises and reorganises autonomous districts and regions
BodyTribes Advisory Council (TAC): up to 20 members, three-fourths ST MLAs; advisory only10 Autonomous District Councils (plus Regional Councils): up to 30 members (26 elected + 4 nominated), term 5 years
PowersGovernor can modify or stop laws for the area; lower self-ruleCouncils have legislative, executive and judicial powers; higher self-rule
Panchayati RajExtended by PESA 1996 (Panchayats Extension to Scheduled Areas Act; Bhuria Committee, 1995)Part IX does not apply; Councils perform those functions
  • Part X = Arts 244–244A (Scheduled and Tribal Areas); Art 244A allows an autonomous state within Assam (added 1969)
Memory tricks

Fifth → 10 states, President, advisory TAC. Sixth → 4 NE states, Governor, Councils with real power (26 + 4 = 30).

☆ Constitutional, statutory and executive bodies
TypeBody (source)
ConstitutionalElection Commission (Art 324); UPSC and State PSCs (Arts 315–323); Finance Commission (Art 280); CAG (Art 148); Attorney General (Art 76); Advocate General (Art 165); GST Council (Art 279A); NCSC (Art 338); NCST (Art 338A); NCBC (Art 338B, 102nd Amendment 2018); Special Officer for linguistic minorities (Art 350B)
Statutory (created by an Act)NHRC (Protection of Human Rights Act 1993; set up 12 Oct 1993); National Commission for Women (Act 1990; set up 1992); CIC (RTI Act 2005); CVC (statutory since 2003); Lokpal (Act 2013); NGT (Act 2010); CCI (Competition Act 2002); TRAI (1997); SEBI; NFRA (Companies Act 2013, s.132)

  • NHRC chairperson: Justice V. Ramasubramanian (took charge 30 Dec 2024)
  • UPSC: first Public Service Commission 1 Oct 1926; named UPSC on 26 Jan 1950; Art 316 appointment, Art 317 removal only after Supreme Court inquiry

Constitutional = has its own Article. Statutory = has its own Act. NITI Aayog has neither.

★ Census and Census 2027

A census is the count of the whole population done by the Union Government; this card gives the law, the history and the next one.

  • Legal base: Census Act 1948 and Census Rules 1990; run by the Registrar General and Census Commissioner (Ministry of Home Affairs); census is a Union subject (Art 246, Entry 69, Seventh Schedule)
  • First census 1872 (non-synchronous); every 10 years since 1881
  • Census 2027: two phases → houselisting from 1 Apr 2026 (about 5–6 months; buildings and households) → population enumeration in Feb 2027
  • Reference date 1 Mar 2027; 1 Oct 2026 for snow-bound areas (Ladakh, J&K, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand)
  • First digital census; caste data beyond SC/ST to be collected for the first time since Independence (last complete caste count: 1931)
  • Home Secretary asked States not to change administrative boundaries till 31 Mar 2027
  • 16th census in the series and the 8th since Independence (last census 2011); outlay ₹11,718.24 crore; caste enumeration approved by the Cabinet Committee on Political Affairs on 30 Apr 2025; houselisting helpline 1855; app and portal in 16 languages
  • Section 15 of the Census Act keeps individual data confidential (not open to RTI or as evidence in court)

Under which Act is the Census conducted, and which Entry of the Union List covers it?

Memory tricks

1872 first → 1881 every decade → 1931 last caste count → 2027 digital + caste.

☆ Bharat Ratna, Padma awards and gallantry honours

Padma BhushanDistinguished service of a high order
Padma ShriDistinguished service
  • Padma cap: 120 a year (excluding posthumous and foreign / NRI awardees); Padma Awards Committee headed by the Cabinet Secretary
  • Announced on the eve of Republic Day; conferred by the President; no cash prize (certificate and medal)
  • Art 18 abolishes titles; the Supreme Court held (Balaji Raghavan, judgment of 15 Dec 1995) that these awards are honours, not titles
  • Gallantry ladder: wartime → Param Vir Chakra, Maha Vir Chakra, Vir Chakra; peacetime → Ashoka Chakra, Kirti Chakra, Shaurya Chakra
Memory tricks

Padma rank = V-B-S: Vibhushan > Bhushan > Shri; all below Bharat Ratna.

1.13 Disaster management: Centre, States and process

★ How India's disaster law came about

India's disaster law was built step by step, each major disaster leading to a committee and then a new body; this timeline shows that chain up to the Disaster Management Act, 2005.

WhenEvent
Aug 1999High Powered Committee (J C Pant) set up shortly before the Odisha cyclone; recommended a separate disaster management ministry (not implemented as proposed)
Feb 2001After the Gujarat earthquake, an All Party National Committee chaired by the PM recommended an NDMA under the Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA)
Jun 2002Responsibility for disaster management moved to the MHA
2004Indian Ocean tsunami → government decides on a law
23 Dec 2005Disaster Management Act, 2005 enacted (notified 26 Dec 2005)
200913th Finance Commission aligns the national and State funds (NDRF, SDRF) with the Act
1 Aug 2024Amendment Bill introduced in Lok Sabha; passed by Lok Sabha in Dec 2024
25 Mar 2025Rajya Sabha passes it by voice vote → Disaster Management (Amendment) Act, 2025
  • Definition (Section 2(d)) → a catastrophe or grave occurrence from natural or man-made causes, accident or negligence, causing substantial loss of life, damage or harm that is beyond the coping capacity of the affected community
  • The Act does not declare any calamity a "National Calamity"; it uses "calamity of a severe nature" for central help
  • 2025 amendment → statutory status to the NCMC and the High Level Committee; NDMA and SDMAs (not executive committees) prepare national and State plans; national and State disaster databases; Urban Disaster Management Authorities (Section 41A); States may raise a State Disaster Response Force; "disaster risk reduction" added to the definition; law-and-order situations excluded from "man-made causes"

When was the Disaster Management Act enacted, and which events led to it?

Memory tricks

1999 Pant → 2001 Gujarat → 2002 MHA → 2004 tsunami → 2005 Act → 2025 Amendment.

★ Three tiers: NDMA, SDMA, DDMA (and UDMA)

India manages disasters at three levels: national, State and district, each with its own authority; the card shows who chairs each one.

TierAuthority (section)ChairMembers and executive body
NationalNDMA (Sec 3)Prime MinisterUp to 9 other members nominated by the chair, one as Vice-Chairperson; executive body = National Executive Committee (NEC), Sec 8, chaired by the Union Home Secretary
StateSDMA (Sec 14)Chief Minister (Lieutenant Governor for a UT)Up to 8 other members; State Executive Committee chaired by the Chief Secretary, who is CEO of the SDMA
DistrictDDMA (Sec 25)District Collector / Magistrate / Deputy Commissioner; co-chair an elected representative of the local authorityCEO, Superintendent of Police, Chief Medical Officer and up to two district officers
City (2025)UDMA (Sec 41A)Municipal Commissioner (District Collector as vice-chair)State capitals and municipal-corporation cities, excluding Delhi and Chandigarh; Karnataka first (for BBMP)

Who chairs the NDMA, the SDMA and the DDMA?

Memory tricks

PM → CM → Collector → Commissioner. NDMA 9, SDMA 8.

★ Central bodies and forces: NEC, NCMC, HLC, NDRF, NIDM

Alongside the three authorities, the Centre has crisis-management committees, a dedicated response force (NDRF) and a training institute; this card lists each with its role.

BodyKey facts
NCMC (National Crisis Management Committee)Chaired by the Cabinet Secretary; nodal body for major disasters with serious or national consequences; statutory status from 2025
HLC (High Level Committee)Chaired by the Union Home Minister; approves central financial aid from the national response fund and assistance from the National Disaster Mitigation Fund; members include the Finance and Agriculture Ministers and NITI Aayog's Vice Chairman; statutory status from 2025
NDRF (National Disaster Response Force, Sec 44)Specialised response force; 16 battalions with personnel from BSF, CRPF, CISF, ITBP and SSB; HQ New Delhi; constituted 2006; Raising Day 19 Jan
NIDM (National Institute of Disaster Management, Sec 42)Training, research, policy help and awareness; HQ New Delhi
SDRF forceStates may constitute their own State Disaster Response Force, modelled on the NDRF (2025 amendment)

NDRF is a force; NDRF (fund) is money. Same letters, different things: always check the full form.

★ Disaster funds: NDRF, SDRF, NDMF, SDMF

Funds for disaster relief are kept separately at the national and State level, for response and for mitigation; the table shows who contributes to each.

FundLegal basis and key facts
NDRF (National Disaster Response Fund)Sec 46; held in the Public Account; 100% central grant
SDRF (State Disaster Response Fund)Sec 48(1)(a); first line of State relief; Centre : State = 75 : 25 (general States), 90 : 10 (North-East and hill States)
NDMF / SDMF (Mitigation Funds)SDMF guidelines 14 Jan 2022; NDMF guidelines 28 Feb 2022; Centre releases its share in two instalments (June and December)
Release ruleNDRF help is adjusted against 50% of the State's SDRF balance on 1 April; SDRF instalments can be released in advance for severe calamities
Finance CommissionWhat it did
9thCalamity Relief Fund (CRF) for each State: 75% Centre, 25% State
10thRules for CRF; National Fund for Calamity Relief (NFCR) for severe calamities
11thNational Calamity Contingency Fund (NCCF), initial corpus ₹500 crore
13th (2009)Merged NCCF and CRF into NDRF and SDRF, aligned with the 2005 Act
14thDedicated chapter on disaster management
15thSDRF and SDMF split 80 : 20 (response : mitigation); mitigation windows for drought-prone States (12), hill States (10), seismic and landslide risk, urban flooding in the 7 most populous cities
16th (2026-27 to 2030-31)SDRF + SDMF total ₹2,04,401 crore (Centre ₹1,55,915.85 crore; States ₹48,485.15 crore); 80 : 20 → SDRF ₹1,63,521 crore, SDMF ₹40,880 crore; 75 : 25 and 90 : 10 kept

Which Finance Commission merged CRF and NCCF into SDRF and NDRF? What is the Centre's share for North-East and hill States?

Memory tricks

CRF → NFCR → NCCF → NDRF/SDRF (13th FC). 9th 75:25; 15th 80:20.

★ The process: from disaster to central relief

This card follows what happens after a disaster strikes: how it is notified, who assesses the damage and how central help reaches the State.

  • Notified calamities → cyclone, drought, earthquake, fire, flood, tsunami, hailstorm, landslide, avalanche, cloud burst, pest attack, cold wave and frost; the Ministry of Agriculture handles drought, hailstorm, pest attack, cold wave and frost; the MHA handles the rest
  • 16th Finance Commission → recommended adding heatwaves and lightning to the notified list; States may keep spending up to 10% of their SDRF on locally severe disasters notified by them
  • RBI calamity relief for borrowers (from 1 Jul 2026) → banks may implement resolution on their own on SLBC recommendation for standard loans not over 30 days overdue; invoke within 45 days, implement within 135 days

Which committee approves central relief, and which ministry releases the money?

Memory tricks

How a disaster becomes central help: disaster strikes → State and district authorities respond, spending from the SDRF → State sends a memorandum (sector-wise damage and relief needs) to the MHA (Ministry of Agriculture for drought, hailstorm, pest attack, cold wave and frost) → ministry checks whether the SDRF balance is enough → if not, a Central Team assesses on the spot → Sub-Committee of the NEC reviews the report and recommends the amount → High Level Committee approves → Ministry of Finance releases the money → MHA monitors use.

Why it exists

Natural calamities destroy farmer and business cash flows, requiring immediate institutional debt relief without automatically categorizing stressed borrowers as defaulting non-performing accounts.

How it works

Following district or state disaster declarations, lenders restructure standard loans not beyond thirty days overdue, offering repayment extensions and restructuring credit facilities.

More fixed facts

  • Principle-based regime; accounts standard and not beyond SMA-0 (up to 30 days overdue) on the event date are eligible
  • Relief: rescheduling, conversion of interest into another facility, moratorium; considers SLBC and DCC decisions
  • Effective 1 Apr 2026

Traps

Only standard accounts not overdue past thirty days on the calamity date qualify for relief; older non-performing loans are ineligible.

In the news

  • RBI: resolution framework for loans hit by natural calamities
☆ Sendai Framework and global links
  • Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015–2030 → adopted 18 Mar 2015 at the Third UN World Conference on Disaster Risk Reduction, Sendai, Japan; 7 global targets and 4 priorities; successor to the Hyogo Framework for Action (2005); UN body: UNDRR (UN Office for Disaster Risk Reduction, earlier UNISDR)
  • Four priorities → understanding disaster risk; strengthening risk governance; investing in resilience; enhancing preparedness and "Build Back Better"
  • International Day for Disaster Risk Reduction → 13 Oct
Memory tricks

Hyogo 2005 → Sendai 2015 (7 targets, 4 priorities).

☆ Warning, monitoring and recognition
  • IMD (India Meteorological Department): 150th anniversary 14 Jan 2025; Mission Mausam (Ministry of Earth Sciences; Cabinet Sept 2024; launched 14 Jan 2025; ₹2,000 crore; seven-day forecasts for cyclones and floods); MHEW-DSS (Multi-Hazard Early Warning Decision Support System, Jan 2024); Mausamgram hyper-local forecasts
  • IMD: 2025 was India's 8th warmest year since 1901; 11 cyclonic depressions (usual 6)
  • ISRO EOS-05 (GSLV-F17) → first imaging satellite in geosynchronous orbit, for agriculture and disaster management
  • Sikkim → 40 high-hazard glacial lakes, 16 in Category A; GLOF (Glacial Lake Outburst Flood); South Lhonak Lake burst Oct 2023 (Chungthang dam destroyed)
  • NDMA issued its first SOP on Disaster Victim Identification; Subhash Chandra Bose Aapda Prabandhan Puraskar announced yearly on 23 Jan (2026: Sikkim SDMA and Lt Col Seeta Ashok Shelke)
  • Operation Sagar Bandhu (28 Nov 2025) → humanitarian aid to Sri Lanka after Cyclone Ditwah
★ Fast Track Special Courts

In plain words: Dedicated judicial tribunals expedite the trial and disposal of pending rape cases and child sexual abuse offenses under specialized child protection statutes.

Why it exists

Prolonged judicial delays in sensitive sexual offense cases cause severe distress to victims and weaken criminal deterrence, requiring dedicated fast-track trial infrastructure.

How it works

Centrally funded schemes support dedicated courts staffed by specialized judicial officers and public prosecutors to conclude trials within statutory expedition timelines.

Fixed facts

  • Each court → 1 judicial officer + 1 special public prosecutor + 6 support staff

Traps

Fast track special courts focus specifically on sexual offenses and child protection cases, operating as dedicated courts rather than general jurisdiction benches.

In the news

  • Cabinet: Fast Track Special Courts scheme extended, ₹3,788 crore

2. RBI, Monetary Policy and Banking Basics

Basics for beginners: how to read this section

The RBI is the central bank: it issues currency, controls interest rates and money supply, regulates banks and is the banker to the Government.

Repo rate is what the RBI charges banks for short loans. When it rises, loans become costlier and spending and inflation tend to slow; when it falls, the opposite.

CRR and SLR are shares of a bank's deposits that it must keep aside (as cash with the RBI, and in safe securities). A higher ratio leaves less money for lending.

An NPA is a loan on which the borrower has stopped paying for over 90 days; banks must set money aside for such loans, and the Basel norms make sure banks hold enough capital.

Chain to remember: RBI changes repo / CRR → banks' cost of money changes → loan and deposit rates change → spending and prices change.

2.1 RBI at a glance

★ Foundation facts

The RBI is India's central bank; it came from a 1926 commission, was set up by the RBI Act 1934 and started work in 1935.

  • Idea → Hilton Young Commission 1926 (Royal Commission on Indian Currency and Finance) | Ambedkar's The Problem of the Rupee shaped its thinking
  • Act → RBI Act 1934 | began work 1 Apr 1935 | HQ Kolkata → Mumbai (1937)
  • Private shareholders' bank → nationalised 1 Jan 1949
  • Statutory body for central banking: issuer of notes, banker to government, banker's bank, regulator, forex manager
  • First Governor → Sir Osborne Smith | first Indian Governor → C D Deshmukh (1943)
  • Logo → Lion and palm tree (from the East India Company double mohur; lion replaced the tiger)
  • Accounting year → July–June, changed to April–March from 2020-21 (nine-month transition)

Which commission led to RBI? Which Act governs it? When was it nationalised?

1926 idea → 1934 Act → 1935 start → 1937 Mumbai → 1949 nationalised. Say it as "26-34-35-37-49".

☆ Governors since 1997
GovernorTerm
Bimal Jalan1997–2003
Y V Reddy2003–2008
D Subbarao2008–2013
Raghuram Rajan (23rd)2013–2016
Urjit Patel (24th)2016–2018
Shaktikanta Das (25th)2018–2024
Sanjay Malhotra (26th)from 11 Dec 2024 (3-year term)
Memory tricks

"Just Read Some Really Useful Dictionaries, Man": Jalan, Reddy, Subbarao, Rajan, Urjit, Das, Malhotra.

2.2 Structure, functions and Acts

★ Central Board and local boards

The RBI is run by a Central Board of up to 21 members, with four regional boards advising it.

  • Central Board → up to 21 members = Governor + up to 4 Deputy Governors + 10 nominated directors (Centre) + 4 directors from local boards + 2 government officials
  • Four local boards → Mumbai, Kolkata, Chennai, New Delhi
  • Governor and DGs appointed by the Centre; Governor's term fixed by appointment (usually 3 years)
  • Subsidiaries → DICGC, BRBNMPL (note printing), RBI Innovation Hub (RBIH, 2021), IFTAS
  • Training institutions → IDRBT (Hyderabad), NIBM (Pune), CAB (Pune)
Memory tricks

21 = 1 (Gov) + 4 (DG) + 10 + 4 + 2.

★ Key laws and sections

The RBI's powers come from a few laws, mainly the RBI Act 1934; the table gives the sections you are asked about.

Law / SectionWhat
RBI Act 1934RBI's parent law
RBI Act s.7Centre can issue directions to RBI (after consulting the Governor)
RBI Act s.22RBI's sole right to issue notes
RBI Act s.42Cash Reserve Ratio
RBI Act s.45ZBMonetary Policy Committee
RBI Act s.45IA / 45LNBFC registration / RBI's power to regulate NBFCs
RBI Act s.47Allocation of surplus to Centre
Banking Regulation Act 1949Bank licensing and regulation (earlier Banking Companies Act 1949; extended to cooperative banks in 1966)
BR Act s.5(b) / s.22Definition of "banking" / licence
BR Act s.24Statutory Liquidity Ratio (max 40%)
BR Act s.35A / s.45RBI's directions to banks / amalgamation and moratorium
FEMA 1999Forex management (replaced FERA 1973; effective 1 Jun 2000)
Payment and Settlement Systems Act 2007RBI regulates payment systems
Credit Information Companies Act 2005Regulates credit bureaus (CIBIL, Equifax, Experian, CRIF High Mark)
Government Securities Act 2006Issue and management of G-secs
SARFAESI Act 2002Recovery without court
DICGC Act 1961Deposit insurance
IBC 2016Insolvency and bankruptcy
Negotiable Instruments Act 1881Cheques, bills, promissory notes

CRR sits in the RBI Act (s.42); SLR sits in the Banking Regulation Act (s.24).

Why it exists

  • Strict criminal regulations under earlier statutory regimes hindered economic growth and cross-border commercial engagement.
  • A modern civil regulatory system facilitates external trade and payments while orderly developing foreign exchange markets.

How it works

  • Current account transactions are generally permitted unless explicitly restricted or prohibited by central rules.
  • Capital account transactions altering overseas assets or liabilities are prohibited unless specifically permitted under delegated directions.
  • Authorised dealers execute cross-border remittance workflows and verify regulatory compliance before settling claims.

More fixed facts

  • FEMA 1999 (civil law) replaced FERA 1973 (criminal) | effective 1 Jun 2000
  • Current account convertibility → Aug 1994 (IMF Article VIII) | capital account → partial

How it changed

  • October 2026: Foreign Exchange Management (Export and Import of Goods and Services) Regulations, 2026; effective 1 Oct 2026.
  • May 2026: Notification dated 30 April 2026; in force from Gazette publication on 6 May 2026; applications through PRAVAAH portal to the RBI regional office
  • April 2026: No resident may be principal debtor, surety or creditor in a guarantee with a non-resident unless permitted; exempt: guarantees by AD bank branches abroad or in IFSC; IPC by custodian banks for FPIs to central counterparties

Traps

  • The statute is a civil code with monetary penalties, unlike the criminal detention provisions of the predecessor regime.
  • Current account transactions are fully convertible under international treaty obligations, whereas capital account flows remain partially regulated.

In the news

  • October 2026: New FEMA rules for freelancers and influencers
  • May 2026: FEMA (Authorised Persons) Regulations, 2026
  • April 2026: FEMA (Guarantees) Regulations, 2026
  • January–March 2026: RBI: FEMA (Guarantees) Regulations, 2026
  • January–March 2026: RBI: ECB framework eased (Borrowing and Lending First Amendment Regulations 2026)
★ RBI functions in one view

The RBI does four main jobs: it issues currency, acts as banker to the government and banks, regulates banks and payments, and manages foreign exchange.

  • Currency → sole issuer of notes (except ₹1 note, issued by the Finance Ministry; coins also from the government)
  • Banker → to Centre and states, and "banker's bank" (lender of last resort)
  • Regulator → banks, NBFCs, payment systems, cooperative banks, credit bureaus
  • Monetary authority → inflation targeting through the MPC
  • Forex manager → FEMA and reserves
  • Debt manager → G-secs, T-bills, SDLs (State Development Loans)
  • Development → financial inclusion and literacy
Memory tricks

"CBR MFD D": Currency, Banker, Regulator, Monetary, Forex, Debt, Development.

2.3 Monetary Policy Committee and inflation targeting

★ MPC at a glance

The Monetary Policy Committee is the six-member group that decides the repo rate, so interest-rate decisions are taken by a committee, not one person.

  • Source → RBI Act s.45ZB (inserted by Finance Act 2016) | suggested by Urjit Patel Committee 2014
  • Constituted 29 Sep 2016 | first meeting 3–4 Oct 2016
  • Members → 6 = 3 from RBI (Governor as ex-officio chair; DG in charge of monetary policy; one officer nominated by the Board) + 3 external (appointed by the Centre on a search committee's recommendation; 4-year term, no reappointment)
  • Meets → at least 4 times a year (in practice 6 times, bimonthly) | quorum → 4, with the Governor (or in his absence the DG) present
  • Decision by majority; Governor has a casting vote only in a tie | minutes published after 14 days
  • Before 2016 → Governor decided, with a Technical Advisory Committee only advising
Memory tricks

3 + 3 = 6, chair casts the tiebreak. "4-4-14": 4-year term, 4 minimum meetings, 4 quorum, 14 days for minutes.

★ Flexible Inflation Targeting (FIT)

Inflation targeting means the RBI aims to keep prices rising at about 4%, and the Government sets that target.

  • Legal basis → RBI Act (amended 2016) + Monetary Policy Framework Agreement (Feb 2015)
  • Target → 4% CPI (Combined) with tolerance band ±2% (2% to 6%)
  • Target is set by the Centre, in consultation with RBI, every 5 years
  • Failure → inflation outside the band for 3 consecutive quarters → RBI must report to the Centre with reasons and remedial steps
  • Index used → CPI-Combined (rural + urban), not WPI
  • Sections → 45ZA (Centre sets the target every 5 years, in consultation with RBI) | 45ZN (failure report)
  • Same 4% ± 2% retained in March 2021 and again by a Department of Economic Affairs Gazette notification of 25 Mar 2026 → valid 1 Apr 2026 to 31 Mar 2031

What is the target and band? What counts as failure?

4 ± 2 | failure = 3 quarters in a row outside 2–6%.

2.4 Instruments of monetary policy

★ Rate corridor (Liquidity Adjustment Facility)

The rate corridor is the band of RBI rates within which banks borrow or park money for a short time; the repo rate sits in the middle.

InstrumentWhatPosition vs repo
Repo rateRBI lends to banks against G-secs (policy rate)Anchor
SDF (Standing Deposit Facility, Apr 2022)Banks park surplus with RBI, no collateralRepo − 0.25% (floor)
MSF (Marginal Standing Facility, 2011)Overnight emergency borrowing, can dip into SLRRepo + 0.25% (ceiling)
Bank RateLong-term lending rate; penalty basisSame as MSF
Reverse repoRBI absorbs liquidity against G-secs; separate fixed-rate windowBelow SDF; not the floor since 2022
  • LAF introduced June 2000 (Narasimham Committee II) | corridor width = 50 bps
  • SDF replaced the fixed-rate reverse repo as the floor

Repo = money comes to the bank. Reverse repo = money goes to RBI. SDF needs no collateral, that is the difference.

Memory tricks

Floor-Anchor-Ceiling: SDF → Repo → MSF (−25, 0, +25). Bank Rate = MSF.

★ CRR, SLR and other tools

CRR and SLR force banks to hold part of their deposits in safe form, which limits how much they can lend; the RBI raises or lowers them to control money in the system.

ToolKey points
CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio)Share of NDTL (Net Demand and Time Liabilities) held as cash with RBI; no interest; s.42 RBI Act; statutory floor and ceiling removed by 2006 amendment
SLR (Statutory Liquidity Ratio)Held by banks themselves in cash, gold or G-secs; s.24 BR Act; statutory ceiling 40%
OMO (Open Market Operations)RBI buys G-secs → injects liquidity; sells → absorbs
VRR / VRRRVariable Rate Repo / Reverse Repo (auctions; durations beyond overnight)
MSS (Market Stabilisation Scheme, 2004)Sterilisation of forex inflows through special bills
Operation TwistSimultaneous buy of long-term and sale of short-term G-secs
Selective credit controlMargin requirements, caps on sector credit
Moral suasionInformal guidance

CRR → cash with RBI, earns nothing. SLR → kept with the bank, in liquid assets.

Memory tricks

Quantitative = CRR, SLR, repo, reverse repo, OMO, MSF. Qualitative = margin, rationing, moral suasion.

★ Money supply measures

Money supply is the total money in the economy; M0, M1, M2 and M3 count it in wider and wider ways.

MeasureComposition
M0 (Reserve / High-powered money)Currency in circulation + bankers' deposits with RBI + other RBI deposits
M1 (Narrow money)Currency with public + demand deposits + other RBI deposits
M2M1 + savings deposits with post office
M3 (Broad money)M1 + time deposits with banks
M4M3 + all post office deposits (no longer reported)
  • M3 = main aggregate | Money multiplier = M3 ÷ M0
  • Higher CRR/SLR → lower multiplier
  • RBI also publishes newer measures (NM1, NM2, NM3) with liquidity aggregates L1 to L3, adopted after a 2020 review
Memory tricks

"Narrow to Broad, 1-2-3-4": each step adds a bigger, less liquid layer. M3 = M1 + time deposits.

2.5 Lending-rate regimes

★ BPLR → Base Rate → MCLR → EBLR

Banks' lending-rate benchmarks changed over time to make loan rates fairer and quicker to follow the RBI's rates; the table shows each regime in order.

RegimeStartIdea
BPLR (Benchmark Prime Lending Rate)2003Each bank's own benchmark; sub-BPLR lending allowed, so opaque
Base Rate1 Jul 2010Floor; no lending below it
MCLR (Marginal Cost of funds based Lending Rate)1 Apr 2016Based on marginal cost of funds; better transmission
EBLR (External Benchmark-linked Lending Rate)1 Oct 2019Linked to an external benchmark (repo, T-bill, etc.); mandatory for new floating-rate retail and MSE loans
  • EBLR spread over benchmark is fixed by the bank; reset at least once every 3 months
Memory tricks

"Big Boys Make Everything": BPLR, Base, MCLR, EBLR (2003, 2010, 2016, 2019).

"14 in 69, 6 in 80". RRB = 50-15-35.

Four anchors: PNB, Canara, Union, Indian = "P-C-U-I".

★ Types of banks

Banks in India are grouped by type, each with a different role and rule; this card lists the types and their main rules.

TypeKey facts
Scheduled banksIn the Second Schedule of RBI Act 1934
Public Sector Banks (PSBs)Government owned (12 after 2020)
Small Finance Banks (SFBs)Serve small borrowers; 60% priority sector (75% till FY 2024-25); capital ₹200 crore
Payments BanksNo lending; deposit cap ₹2 lakh per customer; Nachiket Mor Committee
Cooperative banksUCBs (Urban Cooperative Banks), State and District Central Cooperative Banks; dual regulation (RBI + state/registrar)
Local Area BanksSmall, district-level; 1996 scheme
Foreign banksBranches or wholly owned subsidiaries

Payments banks accept deposits but cannot lend. SFBs lend, with 60% to priority sectors.

Memory tricks

Payments = pay but never lend (₹2 lakh deposit cap). Small Finance = lend small (60% priority sector, ₹200 crore capital). Cooperative = two bosses (RBI + State).

★ NBFC classification and Scale-Based Regulation

NBFCs lend like banks but do not hold a banking licence; the RBI sorts them into layers by size, with tighter rules as the layer rises.

LayerWho sits here
Base (NBFC-BL)Non-deposit-taking NBFCs with assets below ₹1,000 crore; plus NBFC-P2P, NBFC-AA, NOFHC and NBFCs with no public funds or customer interface
Middle (NBFC-ML)All deposit-taking NBFCs (NBFC-D) of any size; non-deposit-taking with assets ₹1,000 crore or more; plus SPD, IDF-NBFC, CIC, HFC, NBFC-IFC
Upper (NBFC-UL)Identified by RBI through a scoring method; the top 10 NBFCs by asset size are always here
Top (NBFC-TL)Kept empty; filled only if RBI sees much higher systemic risk in an Upper-layer NBFC
  • Scale-Based Regulation (SBR) from 1 Oct 2022 (RBI circular of 22 Oct 2021); replaced the old deposit-taking / systemically-important split
  • Always Base: P2P, AA, NOFHC. Never Base: NBFC-D, CIC, IFC, HFC. Always Middle: SPD, IDF-NBFC. Government-owned NBFCs: Base or Middle, not Upper
  • Activity types: ICC (Investment and Credit Company), IFC (Infrastructure Finance Company), CIC (Core Investment Company), IDF-NBFC (Infrastructure Debt Fund), MFI (Microfinance Institution), Factors, MGC (Mortgage Guarantee Company), AA (Account Aggregator), P2P (Peer-to-Peer), HFC (Housing Finance Company)
  • NBFC vs bank: no demand deposits, no cheque issue, not in the payment and settlement system as a bank, no DICGC cover

From which date does Scale-Based Regulation apply, and which layer is kept empty?

Memory tricks

Layers = BMUT: Base, Middle, Upper, Top (empty). Base cut-off = ₹1,000 crore.

★ Banking Laws (Amendment) Act, 2025

This 2025 Act changed several banking laws together, mainly on nominees and director rules in banks.

LayerDetail
PassedLok Sabha Dec 2024; Rajya Sabha Mar 2025
Acts amendedReserve Bank of India Act 1934; Banking Regulation Act 1949; State Bank of India Act 1955; Banking Companies (Acquisition and Transfer of Undertakings) Acts 1970 and 1980 → 19 amendments
NomineesUp to 4 (was 1) for accounts, deposits and lockers; simultaneous (share in %, totalling 100%) or successive (order of precedence); in force 1 Nov 2025
Substantial interestDirector's interest threshold ₹5 lakh → ₹2 crore (unchanged since 1968)
Cooperative banksDirector tenure 8 → 10 years (excluding chairperson and whole-time directors)
AuditorsPublic sector bank boards now set statutory auditors' remuneration (earlier fixed by RBI with the Centre)
Unclaimed moneyPublic sector banks may transfer unclaimed shares, interest and bond money to the IEPF (Investor Education and Protection Fund)
Reporting datesTied to the 15th and the last day of the month (earlier last Friday / alternate Fridays)
In force1 Aug 2025 for Sections 3–5 and 15–20

Substantial interest: ₹5 lakh → ₹2 crore. Nominees: 1 → 4. Co-op director: 8 → 10 years.

★ Bankers' Books Evidence Act, 2026

This Act lets electronic bank records be accepted as evidence in court, replacing a 1891 law written for paper books.

  • Replaces the Bankers' Books Evidence Act, 1891; in force 1 Oct 2026
  • Electronic or digital bank records are admissible if a true copy, no unauthorised change, no tampering; certification by manual, digital or electronic signature
  • A certified copy proves the entry → no need to produce the original book
  • Bank officers cannot be made to produce books or testify (where the bank is not a party) unless a written court order records a "special cause"
  • Applies to courts, arbitration, inquiries and investigations under the BNSS (Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita) 2023; "bank" includes banking companies, post office savings banks and money order offices; Centre can extend it to other financial entities
☆ Regional Rural Banks (RRBs)
ItemDetail
OriginNarasimham Working Group (1975) → ordinance 26 Sep 1975 → five RRBs on 2 Oct 1975; RRB Act 1976 (Act No. 21 of 1976)
First fivePrathama Bank (Moradabad, UP; Syndicate Bank, the first RRB); Gorakhpur Kshetriya Gramin Bank (SBI); Haryana Kshetriya Gramin Bank (PNB); Gaur Gramin Bank (UCO); Jaipur-Nagaur Anchalik Gramin Bank (UCO)
OwnershipCentre 50% : State 15% : Sponsor bank 35%
Count82 (2010) → 67 (2013) → 56 (2016) → 43 (2020) → 28 (1 May 2025, "One State One RRB"; 22,966 branches)
RecapitalisationGovernment support to lift CRAR to at least 9%
Memory tricks

Ownership 50-15-35 = Centre-State-Sponsor. Born on 2 Oct 1975 (Gandhi Jayanti).

2.7 Prudential norms

★ Licence cancellation: Section 22 and Paytm Payments Bank

The RBI can refuse or cancel a bank's licence under Section 22 of the Banking Regulation Act when its working harms depositors; Paytm Payments Bank is the example.

Section (Banking Regulation Act 1949)Meaning
22(1)Licence needed to carry on banking business (e.g. SMBC subsidiary approval)
22(3)Grounds for refusing or cancelling: (b) affairs detrimental to depositors, (c) management prejudicial to public interest, (e) no useful purpose in continuing
22(4)RBI may cancel the licence
5(b)Defines "banking": accepting deposits for lending or investment, repayable on demand
  • Paytm Payments Bank → licence cancelled by RBI under Section 22(4), effective 24 Apr 2026; barred from "banking" (Section 5(b)) and Section 6 business; bank had enough liquidity to repay all deposits
  • History → onboarding of new customers stopped 11 Mar 2022; business curbs on 31 Jan and 16 Feb 2024

Under which Section can RBI cancel a bank's licence? (22(4), Banking Regulation Act 1949)

☆ Payments banks and SFB-to-universal bank conversion
TopicKey facts
Payments banksSet up on the Nachiket Mor Committee advice; public limited company; minimum paid-up capital ₹100 crore; deposit cap ₹2 lakh per customer; cannot lend
First and newestAirtel Payments Bank was first to launch (2017); India Post Payments Bank (IPPB) launched 1 Sep 2018, the first fully government-owned
Five payments banksAirtel, IPPB, Fino, Jio, NSDL
SFB → universal bankScheduled status with 5-year record, listed, net worth ₹1,000 crore, CRAR met, GNPA ≤ 3% and NNPA ≤ 1% in the last two years
Who convertedOnly AU Small Finance Bank so far; Ujjivan SFB's application was returned (to diversify its loan book)
Memory tricks

SFB conversion: 5 – 1000 – 3 – 1 (years, ₹ crore, GNPA %, NNPA %).

★ Basel norms

The Basel norms are global rules on how much capital a bank must hold against its risks, so that banks can absorb losses and not fail.

Basel IBasel IIBasel III
Year198820042010 (after the 2008 crisis)
FocusCredit riskThree pillars: capital, supervisory review, market disciplineCapital quality, buffers, liquidity, leverage
Min capital8%8%8% (India: 9% CRAR)
  • BCBS (Basel Committee on Banking Supervision) → set up 1974 under the BIS (Bank for International Settlements, 1930, Basel, Switzerland)
  • India's Basel III from 1 Apr 2013:
CapitalIndia minimum
CET1 (Common Equity Tier 1)5.5%
Tier 1 (CET1 + AT1)7%
Total capital (CRAR)9%
Capital Conservation Buffer (CCB)2.5% → total with CCB = 11.5%
Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR) / NSFR100% / 100%
Leverage ratio4% for D-SIBs, 3.5% for others
  • D-SIBs (Domestic Systemically Important Banks) → SBI, HDFC Bank, ICICI Bank

India (9%) is stricter than the Basel floor (8%).

Memory tricks

"5.5, 7, 9, +2.5 = 11.5". Basel I-II-III = 88, 04, 10.

Why it exists

  • Under-capitalised commercial banking institutions can fail during severe macroeconomic stress, spreading contagion across the global economy.
  • Standardised capital adequacy ratios and liquid asset requirements ensure banks absorb losses and meet depositor demands independently.

How it works

  • Banking assets are risk-weighted according to credit, market, and operational risk metrics under supervisory rules.
  • Banks must hold core equity tier one and tier two capital matching or exceeding statutory risk-weighted asset ratios.
  • Leverage metrics establish unweighted exposure backstops, while buffer requirements enforce capital conservation during economic stress.

More fixed facts

  • Framework set by RBI guidelines in February 2015
  • Two aims: build extra capital in expansion to draw down in stress; curb indiscriminate lending in credit booms

How it changed

  • May 2026: RBI Amendment Directions, 8 May 2026, immediate effect: commercial banks (Fifth Amendment), SFBs (Fourth Amendment), payments banks (Second Amendment)
  • April 2026: From 1 April 2027; 150% risk weight on unrated corporate/NBFC exposures only above ₹500 cr (₹200 cr was proposed)
  • April 2026: Unrated bank exposures: long-term 100%, short-term 50%; foreign bank branches may use parent ratings

Traps

  • Domestic capital requirements are stricter than international baseline standards.
  • Leverage ratio calculations evaluate total exposure without applying risk weight reductions.

In the news

  • October 2026: RBI: CVA Framework Directions, 2026
  • October 2026: RBI: new operational-risk and leverage-ratio norms from 1 April 2027
  • May 2026: Quarterly profits in CET1 capital: condition removed
  • May 2026: Countercyclical capital buffer (CCyB) not activated
  • May 2026: RBI draft: Pillar 3 disclosures by banks
  • April 2026: RBI: Basel III credit-risk (standardised approach) final directions
  • April 2026: RBI capital, card and classification directions
☆ Basel III market-risk capital directions (2026)
  • RBI (Commercial Banks - Minimum Capital Requirements for Market Risk) Directions, 2026: final directions issued 21 Sep 2026; effective 1 Apr 2027
  • Aligns India with the revised Basel III framework for market risk (capital banks must hold against losses from price and rate movements in their trading positions); interim transition scalars have applied since 1 Apr 2024
  • Changes from the Feb 2023 draft: trading-book definition now refers to the Investment Directions; revised net open position and forex risk charges; revised specific-risk tables for interest-rate risk; revised treatment of debt mutual funds and ETFs in the trading book; total return swaps covered for hedged positions

Basel III market-risk directions: issued 21 Sep 2026, effective 1 Apr 2027 (not 2026).

From which date do the revised market-risk capital norms apply to commercial banks?

★ Basel III credit-risk (standardised approach) directions

These directions change how banks calculate the risk of their loans for capital purposes, in line with Basel III.

  • RBI final directions on Basel III credit risk (Standardised Approach) apply from 1 Apr 2027, the same day as the ECL norms (they sit on top of the Basel norms above)
  • Unrated corporate and NBFC exposures → 150% risk weight only above ₹500 crore (₹200 crore was proposed)
  • Unrated bank exposures → long-term 100%, short-term 50%; foreign bank branches may use parent ratings
  • Regulatory retail → all small businesses (turnover up to ₹500 crore); per-borrower limit ₹7.5 crore → ₹10 crore
  • Real estate AD&C (Acquisition, Development and Construction) → minimum borrower contribution 25% of project cost
  • Tier 2 capital → general provisions on standard assets (Stage 1/2) plus excess provisions from NPA sales count up to 1.25% of credit risk-weighted assets

Unrated corporate: 150% only above ₹500 cr. Retail limit ₹10 cr.

★ NPA (Non-Performing Asset) classification

A loan becomes an NPA when payments stop for more than 90 days; before that it moves through SMA stages as an early warning.

  • NPA → loan overdue for more than 90 days (crop loans: 2 crop seasons for short-duration crops, 1 for long-duration)
StageOverdue
SMA-0 (Special Mention Account)1–30 days
SMA-131–60 days
SMA-261–90 days
Sub-standardNPA for up to 12 months
DoubtfulNPA for more than 12 months
LossLoss identified, mostly uncollectable
ProvisioningRate
Standard assets (general)0.40% (farm credit, individual housing loans, small and micro enterprises 0.25%; commercial real estate 1%; CRE-residential housing 0.75%)
Sub-standard (secured / unsecured)15% / 25%
Doubtful secured: up to 1 yr / 1–3 yrs / over 3 yrs25% / 40% / 100%
Doubtful unsecured portion, and Loss100%

From 1 Apr 2027 the incurred-loss ladder gives way to ECL stages (see the next card).

Memory tricks

SMA = 30-60-90 days, then NPA. Provision climbs 15 → 25 → 40 → 100.

Why it exists

Financial systems depend on transparent asset classification to ensure lenders reflect true loan impairments on balance sheets and hold adequate capital reserves against defaults.

How it works

Accounts overdue beyond ninety days are categorized as non-performing assets, transitioning through sub-standard, doubtful, and loss classifications with escalating provisioning requirements.

More fixed facts

  • Gross NPA 1.93%, net NPA 0.39% (31 March 2026): lowest on record
  • DFS: Viability Plan 2.0 for RRBs, 2025-26 to 2027-28, 30 performance parameters (first plan FY22 to FY25)

Traps

Do not confuse gross non-performing assets with net non-performing assets; net ratios reflect the actual remaining loss exposure after deducting dedicated provisions.

In the news

  • Public sector banks FY26: record profit, lowest NPAs
★ RBI Expected Credit Loss (ECL) norms and the IRAC replacement

Under ECL norms, banks set aside money for expected loan losses in advance rather than waiting until a loan actually turns bad.

  • RBI released the final Expected Credit Loss (ECL) directions with a package on 27 Apr 2026; they apply from 1 Apr 2027 to commercial banks (directions for All India Financial Institutions to follow separately)
  • Shift → from incurred loss (provide after a loan turns bad) to expected loss (provide in advance, forward-looking) using PD (Probability of Default), LGD (Loss Given Default) and EAD (Exposure at Default)
  • A Repeal Directions, 2026 ends the earlier income recognition, asset classification and provisioning (IRAC) framework for commercial banks (see the NPA classification card for the old SMA/NPA ladder); 13 amendment directions were issued alongside (stressed assets, credit risk, capital adequacy, investments, disclosures)
StageMeaningMinimum provision
Stage 1Normal loans0.4% (12-month expected loss)
Stage 2Risky loans (e.g. 60–90 days overdue)5% (lifetime expected loss)
Stage 3Credit-impaired (NPA)Full lifetime loss, highest provision
  • Transition → extra provision from the switch can be spread over 4 years, to 31 Mar 2031, with CET1 add-back during that period
  • Existing loans at 31 Mar 2027 move to the Effective Interest Rate (EIR) method by 31 Mar 2030 (three-year window)
  • Lower floors reported for some products → Stage 1 for individual housing loans 0.25%; Stage 2 for direct lending to State governments 2.5%
  • Wilful defaulter → from 1 Apr 2027 the bank must classify within 6 months of the account turning NPA

Old rule: provision after default. New rule: provision before default. Start date 1 Apr 2027, spread ends 31 Mar 2031.

What are the minimum Stage 1 and Stage 2 provisions under RBI's ECL norms, and from when do they apply?

Memory tricks

Stages 1-2-3 = 0.4 – 5 – Full. "ECL = PD × LGD × EAD".

Why it exists

  • The earlier incurred loss model recognised impairment only after a default occurred, leading to sudden provisioning shocks during downturns.
  • Forward-looking provisioning forces banks to build capital buffers gradually as credit risk increases, protecting the stability of the financial system.

How it works

  • A loan starts in stage one as a performing asset, requiring twelve-month expected loss provisioning.
  • When credit risk rises significantly without default, the exposure moves to stage two, requiring full lifetime expected credit loss provisioning.
  • Upon default beyond ninety days overdue, the asset enters stage three as non-performing, requiring comprehensive lifetime loss provisioning.
  • Expected credit loss equals probability of default multiplied by loss given default multiplied by exposure at default.

More fixed facts

  • Stage 1 Normal loans 0.4% minimum (12-month loss)
  • Stage 2 Risky loans (e.g. 60-90 days overdue) 5% minimum
  • Stage 3 NPAs Full lifetime loss
  • 1 April 2027 RBI ECL and Basel III credit-risk norms start

How it changed

  • 27 Apr 2026: Transition → extra provision from the switch can be spread over 4 years, to 31 Mar 2031 , with CET1 add-back during that period
  • 27 Apr 2026: All loans at 31 March 2027 under the effective interest rate (EIR) regime by 31 March 2030

Traps

  • Incurred loss accounting waits for actual delinquency events; expected credit loss accounting assesses probabilities in advance.
  • Stage one covers twelve months of expected defaults, whereas stage two and stage three mandate provisions across the entire remaining lifetime of the facility.

In the news

  • October 2026: Statement on Developmental and Regulatory Policies (7 Oct 2026)
  • April 2026: RBI: ECL norms (final)
★ Recovery channels

When a loan goes bad, banks and lenders have several legal routes to recover the money; the table lists each route with its law.

RouteLaw / bodyKey point
Lok AdalatLegal Services Authorities Act 1987Compromise, no court fee
DRT (Debt Recovery Tribunal)RDDBFI Act 1993Claims of ₹20 lakh and above (s.1(4) text says ₹10 lakh; raised by Finance Ministry notification S.O. 4312(E), 6 Sep 2018); also applies to SARFAESI s.13(10) applications
SARFAESI2002Seize and sell security without court; 60-day notice; ARCs (Asset Reconstruction Companies; first: ARCIL)
IBC (Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code)2016; NCLT (National Company Law Tribunal)Default of ₹1 crore and above (raised from ₹1 lakh by MCA notification S.O. 1205(E), 24 Mar 2020); 330 days time limit (180 + 90 + litigation extension)
NARCLAnnounced Budget 2021"Bad bank" for stressed assets, with IDRCL
Memory tricks

Smallest to biggest: Lok Adalat → DRT → SARFAESI → IBC.

Why it exists

  • Deliberate non-repayment by capable borrowers drains financial capital and encourages moral hazard across credit markets.
  • Distinguishing deliberate evasion from distress caused by natural disasters ensures punitive deterrence while supporting genuine recovery.

How it works

  • When an account defaults, identification committees evaluate borrower repayment capacity and fund diversion evidence.
  • Review committees verify findings and issue formal classification within statutory timelines from non-performing asset classification.
  • For borrowers struck by natural calamities, institutional committees approve accelerated debt restructuring on state-level recommendations.

More fixed facts

  • From 1 April 2027: bank must classify a wilful defaulter within 6 months of the account turning NPA
  • Wilful defaulter → from 1 Apr 2027 the bank must classify within 6 months of the account turning NPA

How it changed

  • July 2026: Calamity relief (from 1 July 2026): banks may implement resolution on their own on SLBC recommendation, for standard loans not over 30 days overdue; invoke within 45 days, implement within 135 days; one-time 30-day extension via RBI regional director

Traps

  • Wilful default requires deliberate capacity to repay or diversion of borrowed funds, not mere business failure.
  • Calamity relief applies to accounts experiencing sudden natural disaster stress under formal state committee recommendations.

In the news

  • April 2026: RBI: wilful defaulters and calamity relief
★ Priority Sector Lending (PSL)

Priority sector lending means banks must give a fixed share of their loans to sectors such as agriculture and small businesses.

  • Target → 40% of ANBC (Adjusted Net Bank Credit) or CEOBE (Credit Equivalent of Off-Balance Sheet Exposure), whichever is higher
Sub-targetShare
Agriculture18%
Small and marginal farmers (within agriculture)10%
Micro enterprises7.5%
Weaker sections12%
  • RRBs → 75% | Small Finance Banks → 60% (cut from 75% from FY 2025-26)
  • PSLC (Priority Sector Lending Certificates) → trading of PSL surplus, from April 2016
Memory tricks

Sub-targets read "18, 10, 7.5, 12": Agriculture, Small farmers, Micro, Weaker. Remember "AS-MW".

2.8 Payment systems and currency

★ Bank dividend cap and lending limits (2026)

These 2026 rules limit how much dividend a bank can pay out of its profit, and how much it can lend against shares and securities.

  • Dividend rules (2026 Directions, from FY27) → payout depends on capital strength in 10 buckets; adjusted PAT = PAT − 50% of net NPAs; no dividend out of exceptional income, unrealised fair-value gains or reversal of excess provisions
Bank typeMaximum dividend payout
Commercial banks (CET1 buckets), payments banks, small finance banks75% of PAT
Local Area Banks, Regional Rural Banks80% of PAT
  • Earlier cap was 40% of PAT (draft proposed raising it to 75%)
Loans against securities (from 1 Apr 2026)Maximum LTV
Listed shares60%
Equity mutual funds, ETFs, REITs, InvITs75%
Debt mutual funds; AAA listed debt85%
  • Cap ₹1 crore per individual; secondary-market purchase and IPO/FPO/ESOP loans ₹25 lakh each; breach to be cured in 7 working days; all such loans count as Capital Market Exposure (CME)
  • MSE loans → no collateral up to ₹20 lakh (also for all PMEGP units); banks may raise it to ₹25 lakh for good track record; effective 1 Apr 2026

What is the maximum dividend payout for RRBs and LABs? What is the maximum LTV for loans against listed shares?

Memory tricks

Dividend: 75 for banks, 80 for LAB/RRB. LTV: 60 – 75 – 85.

★ Payment systems timeline
