★ Currency management

Currency management covers who prints notes and coins, how they are backed and how the RBI controls them.

  • Minimum Reserve System (from 1956) → ₹200 crore of assets, of which ₹115 crore gold + ₹85 crore foreign securities
  • ₹1 note → issued by the Ministry of Finance, signed by the Finance Secretary; other notes → RBI, signed by the Governor
  • Printing → BRBNMPL (Mysuru, Salboni); SPMCIL (Nashik, Dewas)
  • Mints (coins) → Mumbai, Kolkata, Hyderabad, Noida
  • 8 Nov 2016 → ₹500 and ₹1,000 demonetised | ₹2,000 introduced then | 19 May 2023 → ₹2,000 withdrawn from circulation
Memory tricks

Mysuru and Salboni = RBI's BRBNMPL. Nashik and Dewas = SPMCIL (government).

Why it exists

Sovereign monetary authority must maintain public trust in circulating physical currency by ensuring counterfeit-free supply, clean note standards, and systematic withdrawal of soiled paper.

How it works

Regulated currency chests and mechanized sorting machinery classify banknotes into fit and unfit categories, recycling acceptable notes while shredding and retiring damaged paper.

More fixed facts

  • Issued by RBI under Sections 35A and 56, Banking Regulation Act, 1949; consolidates rules on note authentication and fitness sorting.
  • Fit note = genuine, clean enough to recycle; unfit = physical condition poor or phased-out series (always unfit).

Traps

Phased-out series of banknotes are classified as unfit notes automatically, regardless of their pristine physical condition.

In the news

  • RBI Master Direction on Note Sorting and Authentication
  • Polymer currency notes: trial approval
☆ MDR and the economics of UPI
  • MDR (Merchant Discount Rate) = fee a merchant pays its bank to accept a digital payment (shared among issuer bank, acquirer bank and network)
  • Section 10A, Payment and Settlement Systems Act 2007: lets the Government bar charges on specified payment modes; zero MDR on RuPay debit and UPI from 1 Jan 2020
  • Sept 2026: Finance Ministry notified that banks cannot levy charges on UPI and RuPay debit card transactions up to ₹2,000; MDR on higher-value payments to apply only to select transactions
  • NPCI (National Payments Corporation of India): set up 2008; runs UPI (Unified Payments Interface), RuPay and IMPS

Up to what amount are UPI transactions free of charge?

Free ≤ ₹2,000; legal hook = Sec 10A of the PSS Act 2007.

Why it exists

  • Traditional electronic interbank fund transfers required cumbersome account details and incurred significant processing delays.
  • A single interoperable protocol with virtual payment addresses dramatically increases digital transaction adoption and financial inclusion.

How it works

  • A customer links bank accounts to a mobile application using an authenticated virtual address or phone number.
  • The retail payments corporation routes instructions between remitting and receiving institutions in real time.
  • Immediate settlement occurs across commercial bank accounts using secure two-factor device and biometric authentication.

How it changed

  • September 2026: Sept 2026: Finance Ministry notified that banks cannot levy charges on UPI and RuPay debit card transactions up to ₹2,000; MDR on higher-value payments to apply only to select transactions
  • September 2026: UPI Tap and Pay: tap an NFC-enabled phone on an NFC POS terminal, no QR scan; can work without phone internet using merchant terminal connectivity
  • October 2026: MDR on select UPI merchant payments deferred until after Diwali 2026.

Traps

  • Merchant discount rates remain legally barred on small retail transactions under statutory mandates.
  • Transactions execute directly between underlying bank accounts rather than stored digital balances.

In the news

  • November 2026: UPI in October 2026: records in volume and value
  • October 2026: UPI MDR deferred
  • October 2026: UPI data, September 2026
  • September 2026: UPI record: 24.51 bn transactions in August 2026
  • September 2026: Govt notifies zero charges on UPI transactions up to ₹2,000
  • September 2026: UPI Tap and Pay and MyUPI announced at GFF 2026
  • May 2026: UPI record and payment firsts, May 2026
  • April 2026: UPI at 10 years and 2025 data
  • May 2026: RBI Payment Systems Report (December 2025)
☆ Payments Regulatory Board (PRB)
  • PRB (Payments Regulatory Board): under Section 3, Payment and Settlement Systems Act 2007; Section 3 amendments (Finance Act 2017) took effect 9 May 2025
  • Replaced the BPSS (Board for Regulation and Supervision of Payment and Settlement Systems), which was a committee of the RBI Central Board
  • Chair: RBI Governor; members include the Deputy Governor and Executive Director in charge of payment systems, Secretary of the Department of Financial Services, Secretary of the Ministry of Electronics and IT, and an external member
  • Payment aggregators need RBI authorisation under Section 7, PSS Act 2007

PRB = chaired by the Governor and includes two Union Secretaries; BPSS was RBI-only.

2.9 Depositor protection, forex and publications

★ DICGC, Ombudsman and credit bureaus

Deposit insurance protects small depositors if a bank fails; the Ombudsman and credit bureaus handle customer complaints and credit records.

  • DICGC (Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation) → RBI subsidiary; formed 1978 by merging the Deposit Insurance Corporation (1962) and Credit Guarantee Corporation (1971); Act 1961
  • Cover → ₹5 lakh per depositor per bank (since 4 Feb 2020); payout within 90 days of restrictions (DICGC Amendment Act 2021); banks pay the premium
  • RBI Integrated Ombudsman Scheme → launched 12 Nov 2021 ("One Nation One Ombudsman"); replaced the 1995 Banking Ombudsman Scheme and two others
  • From 1 Apr 2026 → flat premium of 12 paise per ₹100 replaced by Risk-Based Premium (RBP) under Section 15(1), DICGC Act 1961; Tier 1 = commercial banks (not RRBs), Tier 2 = RRBs and cooperative banks; risk categories A–D card rates 8, 10, 11, 12 paise; vintage incentive up to 25%; reviewed at least every 3 years; Local Area Banks and Payments Banks stay at the old rate
  • First credit bureau → TransUnion CIBIL (2000); four licensed under CICRA 2005

₹5 lakh per depositor per bank, not per account.

★ Customer protection: ombudsman, e-mandate and 2FA

These rules protect bank customers through the internal ombudsman, standing instructions on cards and two-factor checks on payments.

ItemKey facts
Internal Ombudsman (IO) Directions 2026Issued 14 Jan 2026, in force at once; a few clauses due by 30 Jun 2026; issued separately for each type of entity; covers commercial banks, SFBs, payments banks, NBFCs, non-bank PPI issuers, credit information companies; every bank needs at least one IO; replaces the 2023 Directions
IO rank and tenureServing or retired officer of rank equal to General Manager, 7 years' experience, age up to 70; Deputy IO at DGM level with 5 years; tenure at least 3 years, total up to 5; final decision to the complainant within 30 days
RBI Ombudsman compensationNo limit on amount in dispute; up to ₹30 lakh for consequential loss plus up to ₹3 lakh for time lost, expenses, harassment or mental anguish; complaints on the CMS portal (cms.rbi.org.in)
e-mandate (recurring digital payments)No OTP up to ₹15,000 per transaction after one-time registration with Additional Factor of Authentication (AFA); ₹1 lakh for insurance premiums, mutual fund SIPs and credit card bills; pre-debit notification at least 24 hours before
Two-factor authenticationCompulsory for all digital payments from 1 Apr 2026 (one factor dynamic); cross-border card-not-present deadline 1 Oct 2026

Ombudsman: ₹30 lakh (loss) + ₹3 lakh (harassment). e-mandate: ₹15,000 normal, ₹1 lakh for insurance/SIP/card bills.

What is the no-OTP limit for e-mandates, and the higher limit for insurance, SIPs and card bills?

☆ Forex basics
  • FEMA 1999 (civil law) replaced FERA 1973 (criminal) | effective 1 Jun 2000
  • Current account convertibility → Aug 1994 (IMF Article VIII) | capital account → partial
  • Tarapore Committees on Capital Account Convertibility → 1997, 2006
  • Exchange rate regime → managed float
  • LRS (Liberalised Remittance Scheme) → $250,000 per person per financial year
Memory tricks

FERA = Fear (criminal) → FEMA = Manage (civil).

Why it exists

  • Cross-border trade and capital flows generate sudden supply-demand mismatches in domestic currency markets.
  • Strategic foreign currency reserves and bilateral currency swaps insulate the domestic economy from international external shocks.

How it works

  • The central bank accumulates foreign currencies, gold, special drawing rights, and reserve positions through market purchases and operations.
  • In a buy/sell swap, the authority absorbs foreign currency from dealer banks against domestic currency, agreeing to reverse the transaction later.
  • To stabilise volatile currency swings, designated foreign exchange windows provide direct foreign exchange access to critical importers.

More fixed facts

  • Reserves = foreign currency assets + gold + SDRs + reserve position in IMF; foreign currency assets are the largest part

How it changed

  • October 2026: No rebooking of a rupee FX derivative (deliverable or non-deliverable) once cancelled; rollovers at maturity still allowed .
  • October 2026: Hedging without proving underlying exposure: $100 mn → $5 mn (all AD banks together); same cut for exchange-traded currency derivatives (all exchanges together).
  • May 2026: Buy/sell swap → RBI buys dollars now, rupee liquidity rises; sell/buy swap → RBI sells dollars now, rupee liquidity falls
  • May 2026: Auction on 26 May 2026; tenor 3 years; near leg 29 May 2026, far leg 29 May 2029; minimum bid $10 million, then multiples of $1 million

Traps

  • A buy/sell swap injects domestic currency into the banking system, whereas a sell/buy swap absorbs domestic liquidity.
  • Reserves consist of four separate components rather than foreign banknotes alone.

In the news

  • October 2026: Forex derivatives tightened; dollar window for oil firms
  • October 2026: RBI special USD-INR forex swap window
  • September 2026: RBI FCNR(B) swap window: $136.4 bn mobilised
  • September 2026: India’s forex reserves hit record $785.7 bn
  • May 2026: RBI gold holdings and deployment of reserves (end-March 2026)
  • May 2026: Forex reserves, FDI and RBI dollar sales
  • May 2026: USD/INR buy/sell swap auction of $5 billion
  • April 2026: Trade relief and FX derivative rules (rolled back)
  • April 2026: Forex reserves, week ended 17 April 2026
☆ RBI publications and calendar
  • Annual Report → by end Aug | Report on Trend and Progress of Banking in India → annual | Financial Stability Report → half-yearly (June, Dec)
  • Monetary Policy Report → half-yearly (Apr, Oct)
  • FSDC (Financial Stability and Development Council) → set up 2010, chaired by the Finance Minister

Why it exists

Regulators and market participants require periodic assessments of systemic banking vulnerabilities, bad loan ratios, and capital adequacy to prevent financial fragility.

How it works

Bi-annual macro-stress tests and financial system surveys assess capital to risk-weighted assets ratios, non-performing assets, and credit trends under hypothetical macroeconomic downturn scenarios.

More fixed facts

  • FSR June 2026: GNPA 1.8% (multi-decade low), CRAR 17.7%, CET1 15.3%; FSR biannual since 2010 (FSDC Sub-Committee)

Traps

Do not confuse the Financial Stability Report with the annual Trend and Progress report, as the stability survey focuses specifically on systemic resilience and bi-annual stress testing.

In the news

  • Bank credit and deposit data (BSR, gold loans, Financial Stability Report)

2.10 Recent RBI directions and rules (2025–26)

★ Integrated Ombudsman Scheme, 2026 (RB-IOS)

One ombudsman for every kind of RBI-regulated entity; the customer first complains to the entity, then to the Ombudsman.

ItemRule
In force1 Jul 2026 ("One Nation One Ombudsman"); supersedes the 2021 scheme
CoversCommercial banks, RRBs, State and Central co-operative banks, scheduled UCBs, non-scheduled UCBs with deposits ₹50 crore and above; NBFCs that take deposits or have a customer interface with assets ₹100 crore and above (HFCs, CIC, IDF-NBFC, NBFC-IFC, NOFHC and some others are excluded from this clause); non-bank prepaid instrument issuers and credit information companies are covered
Step 1Complain to the entity; it has 30 days to reply
Step 2Complain to the Ombudsman within 90 days of the reply window or last communication (2021 scheme: one year)
Entity's replyWritten response with documents within 15 days of receiving the complaint from the Ombudsman
AwardComplainant accepts within 30 days in writing
CompensationUp to ₹30 lakh for loss; up to ₹3 lakh more for time, expenses and mental anguish; no cap on the amount in dispute
AppealExecutive Director in charge of the Consumer Education and Protection Department (CEPD)
Memory tricks

90 days to file, 30 days to reply, 30 days to accept: "90 – 30 – 30".

Compensation 30 + 3 (loss + anguish), up from 20 + 1 in 2021.

★ NBFC layers, capital and loan rules (2025 Directions)

RBI sorts NBFCs into layers by size and activity; the higher the layer, the tighter the capital and rules.

ItemRule
What is an NBFCFinancial assets more than 50% of total assets (net of intangibles) and income from them more than 50% of gross income
Middle Layer (ML)All deposit-taking NBFCs; non-deposit-taking with assets ₹1,000 crore and above; plus SPD, IDF-NBFC, CIC, HFC and NBFC-IFC. NBFC-AA is not in the Middle Layer
Upper Layer (UL)Assets ₹1 lakh crore and above; threshold reviewed every three years
CRARML and above: 15%; UL also needs CET1 of at least 9%
Net owned fundP2P, AA, and NBFCs with no public funds and no customer interface: ₹2 crore; ICC, MFI, Factor: ₹10 crore by 31 Mar 2027; HFC: ₹20 crore

Layers climb with money: 1,000 crore (Middle) → 1 lakh crore (Upper).

Capital ladder 2 – 10 – 20 – 300 crore: P2P/AA → MFI/ICC/Factor → HFC → ARC.

★ Bank capital, exposure and co-operative bank tiers (2025 Directions)

Minimum capital and exposure limits that decide how big a loan or risk a bank may carry.

ItemRule
CRARCommercial banks 9%; SFBs and payments banks 15%; RRBs, LABs and RCBs 9%
Leverage ratioD-SIBs 4%; other banks 3.5%; AIFIs 4%
Large Exposure10% or more of eligible capital base for one borrower or group
Property valuationTwo independent valuation reports for property of ₹50 crore or more
REIT lendingOnly if listed and SEBI-regulated, and at least 80% of its assets give positive cash flow for a year or more
UCB Tier 1Unit and salary-earner UCBs, and others with deposits up to ₹100 crore
UCB Tier 2Deposits above ₹100 crore up to ₹1,000 crore
UCB Tier 3Above ₹1,000 crore up to ₹10,000 crore
UCB Tier 4Above ₹10,000 crore
UCB net worthTier 1 in a single district ₹2 crore; all others ₹5 crore
UCB small-value loansAt least 50% of loans of ₹25 lakh or 0.4% of Tier-I capital (higher), ceiling ₹3 crore per borrower
PSB elected directorAge 35 to 67; at least a graduate; special knowledge in an area such as agriculture, banking, co-operation, finance, law, IT
Housing, ornaments and sharesIPO/FPO/ESOP loans to individuals up to ₹25 lakh at the banking-system level; gold ornaments pledged up to 1 kg and silver 10 kg; gold coins 50 g and silver coins 500 g per borrower
Memory tricks

UCB tiers by deposits: 100 – 1,000 – 10,000 crore (×10 each step).

Leverage 4 for big (D-SIB), 3.5 for the rest.

Why it exists

  • Unchecked bank financing of equity purchases can fuel asset price bubbles and destabilise lenders during sudden market crashes.
  • Strict exposure limits and conservative loan-to-value caps insulate the banking system from stock market volatility.

How it works

  • The regulator defines aggregate and individual ceilings on bank advances secured against capital market instruments.
  • Lending institutions enforce loan-to-value margins and maintain statutory haircuts against pledged securities.
  • Exposures exceeding regulatory thresholds or loan covenants must be cured within prescribed working days.

More fixed facts

  • Aggregate CME ≤ 40% of eligible capital base (Tier 1 + Tier 2); direct investment CME ≤ 20%; acquisition finance ≤ 20%; separate intra-day limits
  • Caps: ₹1 crore per individual; secondary-market purchase ₹25 lakh; IPO/FPO/ESOP ₹25 lakh (max 75% funding, 25% margin, lien on shares)

How it changed

  • March 2026: Concentration Risk Management Amendment Directions 2026 (commercial banks and SFBs); limits on solo and consolidated basis

Traps

  • Aggregate exposure is calculated against total eligible regulatory capital rather than bank asset size alone.
  • Loans against equity shares are subject to conservative regulatory margins rather than discretionary bank limits.

In the news

  • January–March 2026: RBI: loans against eligible securities (from 1 Apr 2026)
  • January–March 2026: RBI: CME ceilings and IPC capital treatment

Money market minimums all ₹5 lakh (CD and CP).

Why it exists

Central bank balance sheet profits from foreign exchange operations and domestic assets belong to the sovereign owner after retaining prudent contingency risk buffers against shocks.

How it works

Under the economic capital framework recommendations, the central bank maintains risk buffers within prescribed bands of its balance sheet, transferring the residual income to the national budget.

More fixed facts

  • 623rd meeting, Mumbai, 22 May 2026; chaired by Governor Sanjay Malhotra
  • Surplus to Central Government: ₹2,86,588.46 crore, highest ever (previous year ₹2.68 lakh crore)
  • Balance sheet +20.61% to ₹91,97,121.08 crore (31 March 2026); gross income +26.42%; expenditure before risk provisions +27.60%

Traps

The surplus transfer is derived from net operational income rather than money creation, and depends strictly on maintaining the contingent risk buffer within approved regulatory bands.

In the news

  • RBI surplus transfer for 2025-26 (623rd Central Board meeting)

★ RBI strategy, Payments Vision and digital payments index

The RBI publishes multi-year plans for itself and for digital payments; this card lists the current ones and their main targets.

Six pillarsRobust Regulations; Customer Centricity and Inclusive Finance; Competitive Markets; Effective Technology; Future-ready Organisation; Global India

How many strategy pillars does Utkarsh 2029 have, and what is the base of the RBI Digital Payments Index?

Memory tricks

Utkarsh 2029: 6 pillars × 49 deliverables. DPI base: March 2018.

Why it exists

Policymakers and industry stakeholders need an objective metric to track cash substitution, electronic payment adoption, and digital network expansion across urban and rural geographies.

How it works

The index computes weighted scores across multiple parameters covering payment performance, infrastructural enablers, network access, and consumer protection.

More fixed facts

  • Five parameters: Payment Enablers 25%, Infrastructure demand-side 10%, supply-side 15%, Payment Performance 45%, Consumer Centricity 5%

Traps

Do not confuse the weights of the parameters; payment performance carries the highest weight at forty-five percent while consumer centricity carries five percent.

In the news

  • RBI Digital Payments Index (RBI-DPI)
★ Ways and Means Advances
In plain words: The central bank extends short-term credit facilities to the government to bridge temporary gaps between cash receipts and expenditure.

Why it exists

  • Government revenue collections arrive unevenly during the year, while ongoing welfare and administrative commitments must be disbursed smoothly.
  • It prevents the government from borrowing impulsively from open markets at high yields during brief cash-flow deficits.

How it works

  • The central bank and the government fix advance limits for each half of the fiscal year based on projected expenditure profiles.
  • When the government draws funds within the limit, interest is charged at the benchmark repo rate.
  • If the drawdown persists beyond agreed ceilings or durations, punitive overdraft interest applies above the standard repo rate.
  • Reaching high utilization triggers formal debt issuances in sovereign bond markets.

Fixed facts

  • WMA: introduced 1 Apr 1997 under Section 17(5) RBI Act (replaced ad-hoc treasury bills); 90 days; states' Special Drawing Facility 1% below repo

Traps

  • Ways and Means Advances are not permanent budget deficit financing; they must be cleared within ninety days.
  • The facility carries interest at the repo rate rather than zero cost, with higher punitive rates for extended overdrafts.

In the news

  • September 2026: RBI fixes H2 FY27 Ways and Means Advances limit at ₹50,000 crore
★ Floating Rate Bonds
In plain words: Sovereign debt securities whose interest coupons periodically reset based on prevailing benchmark Treasury bill yields plus a predetermined spread.

Why it exists

  • Fixed-rate bonds expose investors and the sovereign issuer to interest rate risk when market rates fluctuate over extended horizons.
  • Variable coupon structures offer protection against inflation and changing yield environments, expanding debt absorption capacity.

How it works

  • The sovereign issues debt securities with coupon payments linked directly to short-term money market auctions.
  • At each scheduled reset date, the coupon is recalculated from recent auction yields plus the fixed spread.
  • Interest is paid half-yearly to bondholders until final principal redemption at maturity.

Fixed facts

  • Linked to 182-day T-Bill yield: average weighted average yield of last 3 auctions + fixed spread 0.64 pp . Reset every 6 months; interest paid half-yearly.

How it changed

  • October 2026: RBI fixed FRB 2028 rate at 6.45% for 4 Oct 2026 – 3 Apr 2027 .

Traps

  • Floating rate bond coupons fluctuate with short-term market yields, unlike conventional fixed-rate sovereign securities.
  • The spread above the benchmark remains constant throughout the bond tenure even as the base yield fluctuates.

In the news

  • October 2026: FRB 2028 coupon
★ Central Bank Digital Currency (Digital Rupee)
In plain words: Sovereign digital legal tender issued directly by the monetary authority representing a direct sovereign claim on the central bank balance sheet.

Why it exists

  • Physical currency management incurs high printing, transport, security, and distribution costs across nationwide networks.
  • A sovereign digital token combines the direct liability status of banknotes with digital settlement speed and programmable conditional execution.

How it works

  • The central bank issues digital tokens to licensed intermediary banks through wholesale and retail pilots.
  • Individuals and commercial enterprises store sovereign tokens in dedicated digital wallets without requiring commercial bank deposits.
  • Transactions settle immediately on distributed or centralised sovereign ledger infrastructure with optional programmability parameters.

Fixed facts

  • Wholesale CBDC launched 1 Nov 2022 (interbank)
  • Retail CBDC launched 1 Dec 2022 (public)

How it changed

  • September 2026: Users can schedule automatic e₹ transfers to a beneficiary’s CBDC wallet; tech partner Montran India
  • March 2026: Pilot in PDS (public distribution system): digital coupons through RBI credited as programmable e₹; redeemed at fair price shops; districts Ahmedabad, Anand, Valsad, Surat; to extend to Chandigarh, Puducherry, DNHDD; slogan "Har Dana, Har Rupiya, Har Adhikar"

Traps

  • A digital rupee is a direct liability of the central bank rather than a commercial bank deposit or private digital balance.
  • Wholesale tokens facilitate interbank settlements, whereas retail tokens serve general public transactions.

In the news

  • September 2026: Bank of India launches programmable Digital Rupee (e₹)
  • January–March 2026: CBDC-based digital food coupon pilot (Gujarat)
★ Trade Receivables Discounting System
In plain words: An institutional electronic auction platform enabling micro, small, and medium enterprises to auction trade receivables and obtain immediate financing.

Why it exists

  • Small suppliers frequently face severe cash-flow delays when large buyers defer invoice payments over extended durations.
  • Competitive bidding by multiple financiers against accepted invoices allows small enterprises to access low-cost working capital quickly.

How it works

  • The small enterprise supplier uploads accepted trade invoices onto the licensed platform.
  • The corporate or government buyer confirms and accepts the invoice details electronically.
  • Banks and financial institutions bid against the receivable, discounting the invoice and paying funds directly to the small enterprise supplier.

Fixed facts

  • TReDS Platform operator needs net worth of ₹25 crore; participants are sellers, buyers, financiers, insurers and NCGTC

How it changed

  • September 2026: Aim: timely working capital for Micro and Small Enterprises
  • September 2026: M1xchange: first TReDS platform to operationalise it; CGTMSE CEO Manish Sinha

Traps

  • The discounting is conducted on an electronic auction exchange without recourse to the small enterprise supplier.
  • Eligible sellers on the exchange are restricted to micro, small, and medium enterprises.

In the news

  • September 2026: TReDS receivables can now get CGTMSE guarantee cover
★ Self-Regulatory Organisations in financial markets
In plain words: Non-governmental industry bodies recognised by statutory authorities to establish codes of conduct, monitor market integrity, and settle disputes.

Why it exists

  • Rapidly innovating financial sectors require specialized oversight that cannot be micromanaged directly by statutory regulators.
  • Industry-led peer supervision encourages high ethical standards, consumer protection, and faster resolution of market frictions.

How it works

  • Market participants form an independent non-profit entity representing the sector under approved statutory governance norms.
  • The statutory authority grants formal recognition after verifying governance safeguards, independence, and operational capabilities.
  • The entity monitors compliance, resolves consumer grievances, and serves as an institutional bridge between industry and regulators.

Fixed facts

  • SRO = non-government body authorised by a regulator to oversee a sector
  • Role: enforce standards, ethical conduct, market integrity, dispute resolution, transparency
  • FEDAI (Foreign Exchange Dealers' Association of India): set up 1958, association of Authorised Dealer banks, incorporated under Section 25 of Companies Act 1956

How it changed

  • September 2026: First SRO-FT: FACE (Fintech Association for Consumer Empowerment)

Traps

  • A self-regulatory organisation does not replace statutory regulation; it operates under powers delegated by the central authority.
  • Industry bodies must maintain full operational independence and representative coverage to secure official recognition.

In the news

  • September 2026: RBI recognises Unified Fintech Forum as SRO-FT
  • January–March 2026: RBI recognises FEDAI as an SRO
★ Account Aggregator financial data ecosystem
In plain words: A regulated consent-based digital intermediary network that securely transfers customer financial information between institutions without storing data.

Why it exists

  • Consumers and small businesses faced severe friction obtaining and sharing physical financial records across dispersed financial institutions.
  • Standardised data flows with explicit consent enable frictionless credit appraisal, financial planning, and inclusive digital lending.

How it works

  • A financial user requests specific historical data with customer permission through an aggregator application.
  • The customer grants digital consent specifying data types, purpose, and validity periods.
  • The financial information provider encrypts and transmits the data directly to the user through the aggregator pipeline without intermediary decryption.

Fixed facts

  • AA = RBI-licensed entity moving financial data between institutions only with customer consent; it cannot read or store the data.
  • Key figures: 17 Account Aggregators; 176 FIPs.

How it changed

  • October 2026: 31 Dec 2026 Deadline: Account Aggregator network interoperability

Traps

  • Aggregators act strictly as encrypted data conduits; they are legally forbidden from storing, viewing, or monetising client data.
  • Data transfers require explicit granular consent from the customer rather than blanket institutional authorisations.

In the news

  • October 2026: Account Aggregator network must be interoperable by 31 December 2026
★ Prepaid Payment Instruments directions
In plain words: Payment instruments that facilitate purchase of goods and financial services against pre-stored monetary value maintained in cards or wallets.

Why it exists

  • Unregulated issuance of digital stored-value wallets could jeopardize user funds and compromise national payment security standards.
  • Comprehensive prudential norms establish net worth thresholds, loading limits, and customer verification safeguards.

How it works

  • Entities obtain statutory authorisation from the central payments authority after meeting net worth criteria.
  • Customers load funds into cards or digital wallets using bank accounts or approved payment methods.
  • The pre-stored balance is debited in real time as the customer makes merchant purchases or fund transfers.

Fixed facts

  • Banks (debit card issuers) can issue PPIs with prior intimation to DPSS; non-banks need RBI authorisation and ₹15 cr net worth by end of third financial year
  • General-purpose PPI: outstanding balance up to ₹2 lakh; cash loading ₹10,000 per month
  • Gift PPI up to ₹10,000; Transit PPI ₹3,000; wallets for foreign nationals/NRIs (passport + visa) for P2M payments

Traps

  • Non-bank issuers must meet strict net worth thresholds and regulatory approvals, whereas banks issue instruments under prior intimation.
  • General-purpose instruments permit merchant purchases subject to regulatory ceilings, unlike closed single-merchant vouchers.

In the news

  • April 2026: RBI draft Master Direction on Prepaid Payment Instruments 2026
★ Urban Co-operative Banks regulatory tiers
In plain words: A four-tier regulatory classification system governing capital adequacy, unsecured credit caps, housing loan tenures, and governance in urban co-operatives.

Why it exists

  • Co-operative banking institutions range from tiny single-branch societies to massive multi-state scheduled banks with thousands of depositors.
  • Graduated tier regulations ensure proportionate regulatory oversight while preventing governance concentration and unsecured exposure risks.

How it works

  • Institutions are classified into four distinct tiers based on total deposit size and operational spread.
  • Regulatory directions enforce exposure limits on unsecured lending and prescribe ceilings on residential mortgage maturities across tiers.
  • Statutory cooling-off rules prevent board members from serving indefinite uninterrupted tenures on the same institutional board.

Fixed facts

  • Unsecured loans and advances: aggregate ceiling 20% of total loans and advances (audited balance sheet of the preceding year)
  • Housing loans: Tier 1 and Tier 2 UCBs → tenure up to 20 years, including any moratorium; Tier 3 and Tier 4 → tenure and moratorium set by Board-approved policy

How it changed

  • April 2026: Amendment Directions issued 29 April 2026: Credit Facilities, Concentration Risk Management, Financial Statements (disclosures)
  • May 2026: Continuous tenure of 10 years → re-appointment on the same bank's board (election, co-option or otherwise) only after a cooling-off of 3 years
  • May 2026: RBI Governance Amendment Directions 2026 for UCBs and RCBs; immediate effect

Traps

  • Tier one and tier two urban co-operative banks face statutory housing loan tenure caps, whereas higher tiers set tenures under board policies.
  • Continuous tenure limits require mandatory cooling-off periods before re-appointment to the same institutional board.

In the news

  • May 2026: Co-operative bank directors: three-year cooling-off
  • April 2026: RBI: lending norms for urban co-operative banks (final)
★ Balance of Payments and Current Account Deficit
In plain words: The accounting record of all economic and financial transactions conducted between domestic residents and the rest of the world.

Why it exists

  • Persistent trade imbalances and excessive external deficits can deplete foreign exchange reserves and spark currency crises.
  • Monitoring cross-border trade and capital flows ensures external debt sustainability and macroeconomic financial resilience.

How it works

  • Customs and banking channels log all cross-border merchandise shipments, service transfers, and financial remittances.
  • Transactions are categorized into current accounts covering trade in goods and services, and capital accounts covering investment flows.
  • Net surpluses or deficits in external balances determine movements in sovereign foreign exchange reserve holdings.

Fixed facts

  • CAD: USD 4.2 bn = 0.5% of GDP (Q1 FY26: USD 3.4 bn = 0.4%)
  • BoP Oct–Dec 2025: CAD Apr–Dec 2025 $30.1 bn (1% of GDP) vs $36.6 bn; net FDI $3 bn vs $0.6 bn; FPI net outflow $4.3 bn (vs inflow $9.4 bn); reserves depleted $30.8 bn

How it changed

  • September 2026: CAD: USD 4.2 bn = 0.5% of GDP (Q1 FY26: USD 3.4 bn = 0.4%)
  • January 2026: BoP Oct–Dec 2025: CAD Apr–Dec 2025 $30.1 bn (1% of GDP) vs $36.6 bn; net FDI $3 bn vs $0.6 bn; FPI net outflow $4.3 bn (vs inflow $9.4 bn); reserves depleted $30.8 bn

Traps

  • The current account tracks goods, services, and unilateral transfers, whereas asset investments sit on the capital account.
  • A trade deficit can be comfortably financed by stable long-term foreign direct investment inflows.

In the news

  • Sep 2026: CAD widens to $4.2 bn in Q1 FY27
  • Jan Mar 2026: RBI Central Board 622nd meeting (Patna); BoP Q3 2025-26
★ Bilateral and regional currency swap frameworks
In plain words: Inter-central bank agreements enabling exchange of domestic currencies to provide short-term foreign exchange liquidity support.

Why it exists

  • Emerging market partner countries experience sudden external balance-of-payments pressures and foreign currency shortages during global crises.
  • Bilateral and regional swap lines reinforce regional financial stability without demanding immediate international market borrowing.

How it works

  • Two central banks establish a standing agreement fixing swap borrowing limits and eligible drawdown currencies.
  • The requesting monetary authority draws foreign currency against an equivalent deposit of domestic currency at predetermined interest benchmarks.
  • At maturity, the swap is reversed or rolled over by mutual consent, restoring original currency reserves.

Fixed facts

  • Framework on Currency Swap Arrangement for SAARC Countries (RBI + Maldives Monetary Authority): 2024–2027; first drawdown ₹30 bn on 23 Apr 2026.
  • Follows maturity of the earlier $400 mn facility (availed Oct 2024)
  • Framework operational since 15 Nov 2012 ; RBI swap support to Maldives over $1.1 bn so far

How it changed

  • October 2026: ₹ 30 bn (₹3,000 cr) swap rolled over on Maldives' request; policy: Neighbourhood First .
  • April 2026: India approved ₹30 billion first withdrawal (SAARC Currency Swap Framework 2024-27, between RBI and Maldives)

Traps

  • Currency swaps provide temporary liquidity assistance rather than non-repayable fiscal aid or permanent grants.
  • Drawdowns remain subject to repayment tenors and pre-agreed benchmark interest conditions.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: Maldives: India rolls over ₹30 bn currency swap
  • Apr 2026: India's first drawdown for Maldives under SAARC Currency Swap Framework
  • Jan Mar 2026: India–Japan swap renewed; new Seed and Pesticide Acts
★ Green bonds and blue sustainable finance bonds
In plain words: Fixed-income debt instruments dedicated exclusively to financing environmentally positive and maritime development projects.

Why it exists

  • Accelerating ecological transitions and ocean economy development demands dedicated long-term institutional capital.
  • Earmarked sustainable finance instruments provide ring-fenced funding while assuring investors of strict environmental compliance.

How it works

  • Issuers structure bond frameworks in alignment with recognized green and sustainable finance principles.
  • Proceeds raised from investors are placed in dedicated project accounts restricted to certified ecological and maritime initiatives.
  • Independent verifiers audit deployment records and publish environmental impact metrics throughout the bond tenor.

Fixed facts

  • SMFCL (Sagarmala Finance Corporation Limited): target ₹600 crore + green shoe ₹500 crore; tenor 10 years
  • Rating AA+ (ICRA and CARE); arranger SBI Capital Markets
  • Use: maritime lending, greenfield ports, coastal roads
  • Exact total ₹15,99,506 cr; H2 plan ₹7,86,000 cr (incl. ₹15,000 cr Sovereign Green Bonds) via 23 weekly auctions; tenors 3, 5, 7, 10, 15, 30, 40, 50 yrs; greenshoe ₹2,000 cr; H2 WMA limit ₹50,000 cr

How it changed

  • September 2026: SMFCL (Sagarmala Finance Corporation Limited): target ₹600 crore + green shoe ₹500 crore; tenor 10 years

Traps

  • Sustainable debt instruments restrict fund utilization exclusively to certified eligible project categories.
  • Blue finance frameworks focus on sustainable ocean and maritime ventures rather than inland projects.

In the news

  • Sep 2026: Sagarmala Finance Corporation launches India’s first blue bond
★ Digital Public Infrastructure (India Stack)
In plain words: An interconnected set of foundational digital public platforms providing identity verification, instant payments, and verifiable data exchange.

Why it exists

  • Proprietary closed digital systems limit financial inclusion, stifle technological innovation, and create commercial gatekeeper monopolies.
  • Open, interoperable public digital architectures democratize access to financial services and public welfare delivery.

How it works

  • Public agencies develop open standard software protocols and regulatory rules governing foundational identity and payment layers.
  • Private and public sector intermediaries build user-facing commercial applications connecting to shared public digital rails.
  • Consumers execute instant authenticated transactions, digital record verification, and paperless credential transfers.

Fixed facts

  • DPI cooperation agreements with 24 countries; JAM trinity = Jan Dhan, Aadhaar, Mobile
  • UPI: ≈49% of global real-time payment volume (ACI Worldwide 2024); 81% of India's retail payment volume; 691 banks live; live in 8 countries (UAE, Singapore, Bhutan, Nepal, Sri Lanka, France, Mauritius, Qatar); IMF (Jun 2025) – world's largest retail fast payment system
  • Others: ONDC (2022); DigiLocker (2015); UMANG (2017); CoWIN (16 Jan 2021); eSanjeevani (Nov 2019); PM GatiShakti (13 Oct 2021); e-Courts Phase III ₹7,210 crore (2023–27); India Stack Global

How it changed

  • January 2026: DPI cooperation agreements with 24 countries; JAM trinity = Jan Dhan, Aadhaar, Mobile

Traps

  • Digital public platforms operate as open foundational rails rather than closed commercial applications.
  • User transaction data is managed through federated consent architecture without central data harvesting.

In the news

  • Jan Mar 2026: India's Digital Public Infrastructure snapshot
★ Enhanced Access and Service Excellence banking reforms

In plain words: Public sector banks adopt structured annual reform agendas to enhance institutional risk governance, operational technology adoption, customer service delivery, and digital inclusion.

Why it exists

Public sector banking institutions require coordinated reform frameworks to modernise digital delivery channels, build resilience against financial stresses, and strengthen governance standards.

How it works

Reform cycles set measurable themes and operational milestones evaluated across participating banks through structured performance indexes and collaborative benchmarks.

Fixed facts

  • Four themes: Risk and Resilience, Innovation, Socio-economic Impact, Excellence; GenAI and agentic AI adoption, inclusive banking

Traps

Do not confuse public sector reform themes with statutory central bank prudential mandates, as these agendas represent operational transformation benchmarks for public lenders.

In the news

  • EASE 8.0 for public sector banks
★ Legal Entity Identifier and Unique Transaction Identifier

In plain words: Standardised alphanumeric codes uniquely identify legal entities participating in financial transactions and track individual derivative contracts across global trade repositories.

Why it exists

Financial supervisors need transparent identification of legal counterparties and individual derivative contracts to measure systemic risk exposure and prevent financial fraud.

How it works

Accredited local operating units assign standard codes to financial market participants and derivative deals for centralized reporting to trade repositories.

Fixed facts

  • Master Direction 2026: Section A LEI (Legal Entity Identifier, 20 characters) immediate; Section B UTI (Unique Transaction Identifier) from 1 Jan 2027
  • UTI: max 52 characters (LEI of generator + unique code), same for whole life of derivative; generated by CCP → clearing member/ETP → agreed entity → CCIL-TR; interim UTI to be corrected within 5 Mumbai business days; CPMI-IOSCO guidance 2017; RBI Act Sections 45W and 45U

Traps

Note the structural difference between the twenty character legal entity code for corporate entities and the fifty-two character transaction identifier for individual derivative contracts.

In the news

  • RBI: Unique Identifiers in Financial Markets (LEI and UTI)
★ Bulk deposit definition and differential interest rates

In plain words: Large single deposits exceeding regulatory thresholds are classified separately by banks, allowing differential interest rate schedules based on liquidity run-off characteristics.

Why it exists

Commercial banks face heightened liquidity risk from wholesale deposits that can exit rapidly during market stress, necessitating transparent daily rate disclosures and differential pricing.

How it works

Regulated lenders must publish daily interest rates for large wholesale balances by designated morning timelines, aligning differential returns with liquidity coverage requirements.

Fixed facts

  • Banks must disclose deposit rates, including bulk deposits, in advance on their website
  • Daily disclosure by 10:00 AM each business day; grace of 10 minutes → latest 10:10 AM

Traps

Do not assume wholesale deposits follow retail deposit interest rules; banks must publish transparent daily schedules and can differentiate rates based on liquidity run-off parameters.

In the news

  • RBI: bulk deposit rate disclosure from 1 October 2026
★ Mule account monitoring and digital transaction fraud controls

In plain words: Regulatory standard operating procedures require financial institutions to place temporary debit holds on bank accounts suspected of laundering cyber-fraud proceeds.

Why it exists

Cyber criminals launder illicit proceeds across intermediary mule bank accounts, requiring prompt transaction halts and structured account verification to recover defrauded funds.

How it works

Suspected transactions trigger temporary debit holds for a specified period, giving account holders designated time to provide legitimate evidence before funds are repatriated.

Fixed facts

  • Bank places a Temporary Debit Hold on a suspected money-mule transaction (or entire account) → max 60 days (unless law enforcement says otherwise)
  • Suspected transaction threshold ₹1,000+; holder gets 20 days to explain; bank decides within 10 days of explanation, or 30 days from hold if none

Traps

Remember that temporary debit holds restrict outgoing debits while allowing incoming credits, and unexplained funds are repatriated rather than confiscated by the commercial bank.

In the news

  • RBI draft SOP: temporary debit hold on money-mule and cyber-fraud accounts
  • RBI draft: delay on high-value digital transfers (fraud control)
★ Investment Fluctuation Reserve and investment classification

In plain words: A specialized statutory reserve previously maintained by commercial banks to cushion mark-to-market losses on government bond portfolios held in the available for sale category.

Why it exists

Fluctuating interest rates cause capital losses on fixed-income securities held by banks, historically requiring a dedicated capital buffer to protect solvency.

How it works

Banks maintained dedicated reserves as a percentage of their investment portfolio, which regulations have now discontinued to align with modern international accounting classifications.

Fixed facts

  • Commercial banks and LABs: IFR requirement discontinued from 18 May 2026; balance as on 17 May 2026 moved "below the line" to Statutory Reserve, General Reserve or balance of Profit and Loss Account
  • IFR: buffer against depreciation in the value of investments

Traps

The investment fluctuation reserve was specifically designed as a buffer against depreciation in security investments rather than general loan defaults.

In the news

  • IFR: ended for commercial banks
★ Cross-border inward remittances and settlement directions

In plain words: Regulatory directions mandate fast processing timelines and rapid customer crediting for inbound cross-border personal and commercial money transfers received through banking channels.

Why it exists

International inward remittances form vital foreign exchange inflows and personal income, requiring fast credit delivery and real-time correspondent account reconciliation.

How it works

Receiving banks must notify beneficiaries immediately upon inward payment message arrival, reconcile nostro accounts near real-time, and credit customer accounts within defined business hours.

Fixed facts

  • Under Section 10(2) r/w Section 18, Payment and Settlement Systems Act 2007; effective six months from the circular
  • Inform customer immediately on receipt of the inward message (after-hours: start of next business day)
  • Credit within the same business day if received in forex market hours, else next business day; straight-through crediting for resident individuals

Traps

Inward messages received outside foreign exchange market hours are processed at the opening of the next business day rather than rejected.

In the news

  • Cross-border inward payments: faster credit (RBI guidelines)
★ Bank ownership: prior approval for major shareholding, voting caps, foreign bank subsidiaries

In plain words: Regulatory directions mandate that any entity acquiring significant voting equity in commercial banking institutions must secure prior central bank approval to maintain diversified and fit ownership.

Why it exists

Concentrated shareholding in financial institutions risks governance capture and connected lending, requiring strict ownership thresholds and promoter dilution schedules.

How it works

Central bank directions prescribe major shareholding caps, fit and proper criteria, and strict regulatory approvals before foreign entities or promoters acquire controlling equity stakes.

Fixed facts

  • Preferential allotment of up to 959 million shares at ₹280 each → nearly 60% of post-issue capital

Traps

Acquiring major voting control in commercial banks requires statutory central bank clearance regardless of whether the transaction occurs via secondary purchase or preferential allotment.

In the news

  • Emirates NBD gets all approvals for RBL Bank
★ Fair practices in lending and loan recovery (recovery agents, conduct rules)

In plain words: Lending conduct guidelines regulate how financial institutions and recovery agents interact with delinquent borrowers, banning intimidation and restricting intrusive collection practices.

Why it exists

Aggressive recovery tactics, borrower harassment, and arbitrary asset seizures undermine public trust and violate basic consumer protection rights during debt distress.

How it works

Regulatory directions establish structured advance notice timelines, reasonable contact hours, grievance redressal options, and strict rules regarding collateral enforcement.

Fixed facts

  • Lender may restrict functions of a mobile phone only when the loan financed that device, and only after the account is 90 days past due
  • First notice when the loan is 60 days overdue, giving at least 21 days to pay; second notice of 7 days before any restriction

Traps

Lenders cannot block borrower communication devices unless the credit facility directly financed that specific handset, and mandatory cure notices must be served first.

In the news

  • RBI draft: loan recovery conduct and locking of financed phones
★ Bank finance for acquisitions

In plain words: Prudential norms establish conditions and exposure ceilings under which commercial banks can provide credit facilities to domestic corporations acquiring target businesses.

Why it exists

Uncontrolled bank lending for corporate buyouts risks over-leveraging acquirers and concentrating bank balance sheet risks in speculative takeovers.

How it works

Prudential rules cap bank funding percentages, enforce debt-to-equity ceilings on consolidated balance sheets, and require short-term bridge financing to be replaced by permanent equity.

Fixed facts

  • Overseas syndicated acquisition finance: Indian banks capped at 20% of total funding (draft 10%)
  • Bank financing at most 75% of acquisition value; post-deal consolidated debt-to-equity at most 3:1
  • Bridge finance to be replaced by equity within 12 months; effective 1 Jul 2026 (RBI deferred from 1 Apr in Mar 2026)

Traps

Bank acquisition financing is capped at seventy-five percent of the acquisition value, ensuring the borrowing company contributes significant equity capital.

In the news

  • RBI final acquisition finance framework for banks

3. GST, Taxation and Budget

Basics for beginners: how to read this section

GST is a single tax on supply of goods and services, replacing many older taxes; the Centre, States and the GST Council share it.

Direct taxes (income tax) are paid by the person taxed; indirect taxes (GST, customs) are collected through the price.

The Budget lists the Government's receipts and spending for the year; a deficit means spending is more than receipts.

Reading order: GST → direct and indirect tax → Income-tax Act → Budget → deficits and the FRBM Act.

3.1 GST journey

★ GST timeline

GST took about two decades to arrive, from first talks in 2000 to launch in 2017; the timeline shows each step on the way.

YearEvent
2000Vajpayee government begins GST discussions; Asim Dasgupta-led Empowered Committee of state finance ministers
2006-07Budget speech (P Chidambaram) announces GST from 1 Apr 2010
2009Empowered Committee's First Discussion Paper on GST (Nov 2009); 13th Finance Commission Task Force on GST (chair Arbind Modi)
2011115th Constitution Amendment Bill (lapsed)
2013GSTN (Goods and Services Tax Network) set up as a Section 8 company
Dec 2014122nd Amendment Bill introduced (Arun Jaitley); Lok Sabha passed May 2015; Rajya Sabha Aug 2016
Sep 2016101st Amendment Act (assent 8 Sep) → GST Council constituted 12 Sep; first meeting 22–23 Sep 2016
1 Jul 2017GST launched at midnight session in Central Hall of Parliament (July 1 = GST Day)
Jul 2017J&K joins (8 Jul 2017) → "One Nation, One Tax, One Market"
2018E-way bill (1 Apr 2018)
3 Sep 202556th GST Council → two-slab structure, effective 22 Sep 2025 ("GST 2.0")

Which Amendment, which Article, which date?

Memory tricks

Idea 2000 → announced 2006 → Bill 2014 → Amendment 2016 → Launch 2017 → 2.0 in 2025. "Sixteen to Seventeen": Act 2016, launch 2017.

★ GST structure and Acts

GST is one tax on goods and services that replaced many separate taxes; it is split into central, state and inter-state parts depending on where the sale happens.

TaxLevied onGoes to
CGST (Central GST)Intra-state supplyCentre
SGST (State GST)Intra-state supplyState
IGST (Integrated GST)Inter-state supply and importsCollected by Centre, apportioned to the destination state
UTGST (Union Territory GST)Intra-UT supply (UTs without legislature)UT
Compensation CessSin and luxury goodsCompensation Fund (from 22 Sep 2025 merged into the 40% rate, except pan masala, gutkha and tobacco products)
  • Dual GST → Centre and states both levy | destination-based | input tax credit (ITC) chain
  • Acts of 2017 → CGST, IGST, UTGST, GST (Compensation to States) Act (5 years of compensation; 14% annual growth over the 2015-16 base)
  • Constitutional hooks → 246A (power), 269A (inter-state levy), 279A (Council), 366(12A) (definition)
  • Outside GST at launch → crude oil, diesel, petrol, natural gas, aviation turbine fuel; alcohol for human consumption; electricity (not subsumed)
Memory tricks

"CSI-U": CGST, SGST, IGST, UTGST. IGST = Inter-state, Import. Intra = CGST + SGST together (equal split).

3.2 GST Council

★ GST Council (Art 279A)

The GST Council is the joint forum where the Centre and States decide GST rates and rules together.

  • Inserted by 101st Amendment 2016; President constitutes it | joint forum of Centre and states | office in New Delhi
  • Chair → Union Finance Minister | members → Union MoS (Finance/Revenue) + state finance/taxation ministers (one among them elected Vice-Chair)
  • Secretary → Union Revenue Secretary (ex-officio) | CBIC Chairperson is a permanent invitee (no vote)
  • Voting → Centre 1/3 weight, all states together 2/3 | decision needs 3/4 of weighted votes of members present and voting
  • Recommends → taxes subsumed, exemptions, rates (including floor rates), model laws, IGST apportionment, special provisions for hill and north-east states, compensation
  • Supreme Court (2022, Mohit Minerals) → Council recommendations are persuasive, not binding
Memory tricks

1/3 + 2/3 = 100% of weight; 3/4 needed. Neither side can pass a decision alone (33% and 67% are both below 75%). The Centre alone can block, since its 33% is above the 25% blocking minority. Remember "1/3, 2/3, 3/4".

Why it exists

  • Multiple cascading state and central indirect taxes created internal economic barriers and excessive compliance burdens.
  • A destination-based shared tax structure standardises nationwide tariffs and eliminates tax cascading across interstate value chains.

How it works

  • A joint constitutional body comprising federal and state finance ministers deliberates on tax slabs, exemptions, and compliance rules.
  • Transactions attract split central and state levies or an integrated levy on interstate commerce with seamless input credit transfers.
  • Electronic declarations and automated invoice tracking verify supply chain transactions and credit settlement across jurisdictions.

More fixed facts

  • GST Council: created by Constitution (101st Amendment) Act, 2016; chaired by Union Finance Minister
  • GST Council voting? Show answer Centre 1/3, States 2/3; decisions need 3/4 of weighted votes.

How it changed

  • October 2026: Arrest powers withdrawn: omit Section 69, CGST Act 2017. Prosecution threshold ₹1 cr → ₹5 cr; Section 132 narrowed (clause c only for fake-ITC without goods/services/invoice). Max general penalty (Section 125) ₹25,000 → ₹10,000.

Traps

  • Council decisions require a three-fourths weighted majority giving neither Centre nor States unilateral veto power alone.
  • Taxation of petroleum crude and select fuels remains deferred until recommended by the federal council.

In the news

  • Nov 2026: GST collections, October 2026: ₹2.31 lakh crore gross
  • Oct 2026: IGST on gold, silver, platinum imports
  • Oct 2026: 57th GST Council meeting: rate rationalisation decisions
  • Sep 2026: 56th GST Council meeting decisions
  • Jan Mar 2026: GST collections FY26 reach record high
★ GST 2.0: slab overhaul of 2025

In 2025 the GST rate structure was simplified into fewer slabs; this card gives the date and the new rates.

  • 56th Council → 3 Sep 2025 (chair: Nirmala Sitharaman) → unanimous | effective 22 Sep 2025 (tobacco from 1 Feb 2026: 40% GST on cigarettes, pan masala and tobacco, 18% on bidis; the compensation cess replaced by excise and a health and national security cess)
  • Four slabs 5 / 12 / 18 / 28 → two slabs 5% (merit) and 18% (standard), plus special 40% (demerit/luxury)
BeforeAfterExamples
28%18%ACs, TVs, dishwashers, small cars, cement
12% / 18%5%Toothpaste, umbrellas, sewing machines, bicycles, FMCG sachets
18%NilIndividual life and health insurance premiums
28%40%Luxury cars and SUVs, tobacco products, pan masala
Cess items40% (cess merged)Aerated drinks, casinos, online gaming, race clubs
  • 12% and 28% slabs abolished | estimated revenue loss over ₹47,000 crore
Memory tricks

"5-18-40": merit, standard, sin. Four slabs became two plus one special.

☆ Registration, composition and GSTN
  • Registration threshold → ₹40 lakh (goods), ₹20 lakh (services); lower for special category states
  • Composition scheme → turnover up to ₹1.5 crore (goods)
  • GSTN → IT backbone; became 100% government-owned in 2019
  • CBIC (Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs) administers GST and customs; renamed from CBEC in 2017

These thresholds changed over the years; keep as indicative until verified.

3.3 Taxation basics

★ Direct vs indirect taxes

Direct taxes are paid by the person on whom they are levied; indirect taxes are collected from a buyer through the price of what is bought.

DirectIndirect
BurdenCannot be shiftedShifted to consumer
ExamplesIncome tax, corporate tax, capital gains tax, STT (Securities Transaction Tax, 2004)GST, customs duty, central excise (on petroleum and a few items)
BodyCBDT (Central Board of Direct Taxes)CBIC
NatureProgressive (rate rises with income)Regressive in effect (same rate for all)
  • Income tax first introduced in India in 1860 by James Wilson | Income Tax Day → 24 July
  • Wealth tax abolished from Budget 2015-16 (replaced by a surcharge on the super-rich)
  • Cess and surcharge are not shared with states (outside the divisible pool)
Memory tricks

Direct = DIrect hit on the taxpayer (CBDT). Indirect = INcluded in price (CBIC).

★ Income-tax Act, 2025

India has replaced its 1961 income-tax law with a new Act from 1 April 2026; this card gives the dates and key facts.

  • Replaced the Income-tax Act, 1961 (Act 43 of 1961) from 1 Apr 2026
  • Act 30 of 2025 | Presidential assent 21 Aug 2025 | first Income-tax Bill introduced 13 Feb 2025, withdrawn 8 Aug 2025, revised Bill passed
  • Sections 819 → 536 | Chapters 47 → 23 | Schedules → 16
  • "Previous Year" and "Assessment Year" replaced by one "Tax Year" (Section 3) → income of 2026-27 = Tax Year 2026-27
  • Policy-neutral: no new taxes, no change in rates, slabs or regimes
  • Transition → FY 2025-26 (AY 2026-27) returns still under the 1961 Act
  • TDS compressed into 3 sections (392 to 394)

When did the new Act come into force and what replaced the assessment-year concept?

Memory tricks

819 → 536 (about a third cut) | 47 → 23 chapters | 1961 → 2025, in force 2026. "Tax Year" = one year replaces two (previous and assessment).

☆ Capital gains: LTCG and STCG
AssetLong-term if heldLTCG rateShort-term
Listed shares, equity mutual funds, business trust unitsMore than 12 months12.5% above ₹1.25 lakh a year20% (STCG on listed equity)
Property, gold, unlisted shares, other assetsMore than 24 months12.5%, no indexationSlab rates
Debt mutual funds (since Apr 2023)–Slab rates, any holding periodSlab rates
  • Budget July 2024: LTCG on listed equity 10% → 12.5%; exemption ₹1 lakh → ₹1.25 lakh; STCG 15% → 20%; indexation removed from 23 Jul 2024
  • Land or building bought before 23 Jul 2024: resident individual / HUF may choose 12.5% without indexation or 20% with indexation
  • Old Act sections: 111A (STCG on listed equity), 112A (LTCG on listed equity); the Income-tax Act 2025 (in force 1 Apr 2026) renumbers sections
  • STT (Securities Transaction Tax) is separate from capital gains tax

Equity: 12 months, 12.5%, ₹1.25 lakh exempt. Short-term: 20%.

☆ Customs duty and import levies
DutyLegal basisWhat it does
BCD (Basic Customs Duty)Sec 12, Customs Act 1962Main import duty on assessable value (Sec 14)
SWS (Social Welfare Surcharge)Sec 110, Finance Act 2018Surcharge on top of BCD
IGST on importsSec 5, IGST Act 2017 with Sec 3(7), Customs Tariff ActReplaces the old additional duties for GST goods
CVD (Countervailing Duty)Sec 9, Customs Tariff Act 1975Offsets a foreign subsidy
Safeguard dutySec 8B, Customs Tariff ActTemporary duty when an import surge hurts domestic industry
ADD (Anti-Dumping Duty)Sec 9A, Customs Tariff ActOffsets dumping (export below normal value), up to the margin of dumping
AIDC (Additional Duties of Customs)Sec 3, Customs Tariff ActMatches excise or local levies on a like domestic article
  • CBIC (Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs) administers customs and GST
  • DGTR (Directorate General of Trade Remedies) recommends anti-dumping and safeguard duties; the Finance Ministry notifies them
Memory tricks

Section numbers: 8B – 9 – 9A = Safeguard (surge), CVD (subsidy), ADD (dumping).

3.4 Budget basics

★ Budget in the Constitution

The Constitution sets out how the Budget is presented and passed; each Article covers one step.

ArtSubject
112Annual Financial Statement (Budget)
113Procedure in Parliament (demands for grants)
114Appropriation Bill
115Supplementary, additional, excess grants
116Vote on account, vote of credit, exceptional grants
117Financial Bills
265No tax without law
266Consolidated Fund (1) and Public Account (2)
267Contingency Fund (President's advance)
  • Budget presented on 1 Feb (from 2017; earlier last working day of February) | Rail Budget merged with Union Budget from 2017-18
  • Plan / non-plan classification abolished 2017-18 → now revenue / capital
  • Two Bills follow the Budget → Appropriation Bill authorises withdrawal from the Consolidated Fund; Finance Bill carries the tax proposals
Memory tricks

112-113-114-115-116-117 reads "Budget, Demand, Appropriation, Supplementary, Vote on account, Financial Bills". 266 = fundS (Consolidated + Public), 267 = emergency fund.

Why it exists

  • Democratic accountability requires legislative authorization before the executive can levy taxes or withdraw public revenues.
  • A comprehensive pre-budget survey provides an analytical evaluation of macroeconomic fundamentals to inform parliamentary scrutiny.

How it works

  • The finance ministry collects spending estimates from administrative departments and coordinates economic forecasting.
  • The chief economic adviser prepares an economic report evaluating macroeconomic performance across domestic sectors.
  • The finance minister presents the annual financial statement in parliament for general debate, department scrutiny, and vote on taxes.

More fixed facts

  • First Economic Survey 1950-51; prepared by the Economic Division, Department of Economic Affairs (Ministry of Finance) under the Chief Economic Adviser (V. Anantha Nageswaran since 28 Jan 2022)
  • Tabled in Parliament by the Finance Minister one day before the Union Budget
  • Separated from the Budget speech in 1964; since 2022-23 one consolidated volume plus a Statistical Appendix (earlier two volumes)

How it changed

  • January 2026: Tabled by FM Nirmala Sitharaman in Lok Sabha; prepared under CEA V. Anantha Nageswaran
  • January 2026: Real GDP growth projection FY27: 6.8–7.2%

Traps

  • The Economic Survey is presented by convention and is not mandated by the Constitution.
  • Appropriation and finance bills follow distinct parliamentary passage and voting procedures.

In the news

  • Jan Mar 2026: Economic Survey 2025-26 tabled in Parliament
  • Jan Mar 2026: Union Budget 2026-27 presented
★ Receipts, expenditure and deficits

Receipts are money the Government gets, expenditure is what it spends, and a deficit measures how much it falls short; the formulas show how each deficit is calculated.

ItemFormula / meaning
Revenue receiptsTax revenue + non-tax revenue (no liability created, no asset reduced)
Capital receiptsBorrowings, recovery of loans, disinvestment (create liability or reduce asset)
Revenue deficitRevenue expenditure − Revenue receipts
Effective revenue deficitRevenue deficit − grants for creation of capital assets
Fiscal deficitTotal expenditure − (Revenue receipts + non-debt capital receipts) = borrowing need
Primary deficitFiscal deficit − Interest payments
Budgetary deficitOld measure; abolished from 1997-98
Memory tricks

"R → F → P": Revenue deficit, Fiscal deficit, Primary deficit. Primary = Fiscal minus interest. Fiscal deficit = how much the government must borrow.

Why it exists

  • Public investment and welfare expenditures frequently exceed current tax revenues, requiring prudential sovereign debt financing.
  • A predictable and transparent issuance calendar prevents market disruption and ensures orderly sovereign yield curve formation.

How it works

  • The union government determines its net borrowing requirement during the annual budget presentation.
  • The central bank issues dated securities and treasury bills through regular competitive electronic auctions.
  • Institutional investors purchase bonds to fund infrastructure outlays while the sovereign manages repayment tenors.

More fixed facts

  • Gross market borrowing BE ₹17.20 lakh crore → ₹16.09 lakh crore after G-Sec switches
  • FY27 fiscal deficit target 4.3% of GDP

How it changed

  • September 2026: Exact total ₹15,99,506 cr; H2 plan ₹7,86,000 cr (incl. ₹15,000 cr Sovereign Green Bonds) via 23 weekly auctions; tenors 3, 5, 7, 10, 15, 30, 40, 50 yrs; greenshoe ₹2,000 cr; H2 WMA limit ₹50,000 cr
  • March 2026: Gross market borrowing BE ₹17.20 lakh crore → ₹16.09 lakh crore after G-Sec switches

Traps

  • The borrowing programme is financed through dated securities and treasury bills rather than direct central bank deficit financing.
  • Fiscal deficit figures include net market borrowing alongside other internal liabilities.

In the news

  • Sep 2026: Centre releases H2 FY27 borrowing calendar
  • Mar 2026: Centre releases H1 FY27 borrowing calendar
  • Jan Mar 2026: Union Budget 2026-27: fiscal deficit roadmap
☆ Budget passage and cut motions
  • Stages → presentation → general discussion → scrutiny by departmentally related standing committees → voting on demands for grants (Lok Sabha only; guillotine closes undiscussed demands) → Appropriation Bill → Finance Bill
  • Cut motions → policy cut (₹1 symbolic; disapproves policy), economy cut (specified amount), token cut (₹100; grievance)
  • Rajya Sabha only discusses; does not vote on demands for grants
  • Vote on account → temporary spending cover before the Budget passes (Art 116) | interim budget in election years
★ FRBM Act and the N K Singh Committee

The FRBM Act sets limits on the Government's borrowing and deficits; the N K Singh Committee reviewed it and suggested new targets.

  • FRBM Act 2003 (Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management) | rules 2004 | fiscal deficit target 3% of GDP
  • N K Singh Committee → set up May 2016, report Jan 2017 (five members)
  • Recommendations → debt-to-GDP 60% (Centre 40% + states 20%) by 2023 | fiscal deficit 3% for 2017-20, then 2.5% by 2022-23 | escape clause up to 0.5% of GDP | three-member independent Fiscal Council | replace FRBM with a Debt and Fiscal Responsibility Act
  • Government accepted the debt anchor and escape clause in part; the Fiscal Council was not set up as recommended
Memory tricks

"60 = 40 + 20", "3 → 2.5", "0.5 escape".

☆ Economic Survey
  • First Economic Survey 1950-51; prepared by the Economic Division, Department of Economic Affairs (Ministry of Finance) under the Chief Economic Adviser (V. Anantha Nageswaran since 28 Jan 2022)
  • Tabled in Parliament by the Finance Minister one day before the Union Budget
  • No constitutional or statutory mandate: a convention
  • Separated from the Budget speech in 1964; since 2022-23 one consolidated volume plus a Statistical Appendix (earlier two volumes)

Survey = convention, not law. Budget = Art 112 (Annual Financial Statement).

★ Viability Gap Funding mechanism
In plain words: A targeted capital grant mechanism supporting commercially unviable infrastructure projects that yield substantial public economic benefits.

Why it exists

  • Critical public infrastructure projects often face high capital expenditure and low commercial tariffs, rendering them unviable for private investment.
  • Public grant support bridges upfront capital deficits, enabling private sector execution of essential public utilities.

How it works

  • Government departments identify economically justified public infrastructure initiatives requiring private investment.
  • Developers bid competitively for projects based on the lowest capital grant assistance requested from the state.
  • Sanctioned viability grants are disbursed across construction milestones to ensure timely physical commissioning.

Fixed facts

  • BESS → minimum 40% domestic content requirement; alternative-technology projects → public and private entities
  • Earlier (Sept 2023): VGF for BESS → 4,000 MWh by 2030-31; outlay ₹9,400 crore; budget support ₹3,760 crore; VGF up to 40% of capital cost

How it changed

  • November 2026: BESS → minimum 40% domestic content requirement; alternative-technology projects → public and private entities

Traps

  • Capital viability grants provide upfront construction assistance rather than indefinite operational revenue subsidies.
  • Projects are awarded through competitive bidding to minimize the public financial burden.

In the news

  • Nov 2026: Cabinet: ₹14,500 crore Viability Gap Funding for grid-scale energy storage

4. Financial Regulators and Institutions

Basics for beginners: how to read this section

A regulator is a body with legal power to set rules for one part of finance and to supervise it.

RBI: banks and payments. SEBI: shares and mutual funds. IRDAI: insurance. PFRDA: pensions. Each card gives the set-up date, HQ and law.

All India Financial Institutions (NABARD, EXIM, SIDBI and similar) are not regulators but lenders for one sector each.

4.1 Who regulates whom

★ Regulator map

Each segment of the financial system has its own regulator; this map shows which regulator watches which part.

RegulatorRegulates
RBIBanks, NBFCs, housing finance companies (since 2019), payment systems, credit bureaus, cooperative banks (with NABARD supervising RRBs and rural cooperatives), forex
SEBIStock exchanges, brokers, mutual funds, depositories, AIFs, credit rating agencies, commodity derivatives (FMC merged into SEBI in 2015)
IRDAIInsurers, reinsurers, insurance intermediaries
PFRDANPS, pension funds, Atal Pension Yojana
IFSCAFinancial services in IFSC (GIFT City)
IBBIInsolvency professionals and agencies under IBC 2016
FSDCCoordination of all regulators (chair: Finance Minister; set up 2010)
Memory tricks

"B-M-I-P": Banks → RBI, Markets → SEBI, Insurance → IRDAI, Pensions → PFRDA.

☆ How regulator heads are chosen (FSRASC)
  • FSRASC (Financial Sector Regulatory Appointments Search Committee) → set up from the FSLRC (Justice B N Srikrishna Commission) recommendation; recommends heads and members of SEBI, IRDAI, PFRDA, and RBI Governor and Deputy Governors
  • Composition → Cabinet Secretary (chair); Additional Principal Secretary to PM; Secretary DEA or DFS; chair of the concerned regulator; three outside experts
  • Final approval → Appointments Committee of the Cabinet (ACC)
  • For PSB and financial institution heads → FSIB (Financial Services Institutions Bureau)

Search committee recommends; ACC (chaired by PM) approves.

4.2 SEBI

★ SEBI at a glance

SEBI regulates the securities market, meaning shares, bonds and mutual funds; it began in 1988 and got legal powers in 1992.

  • Set up 12 Apr 1988 (non-statutory, under Finance Ministry) → statutory under the SEBI Act 1992 (statutory powers from 30 Jan 1992, after the Harshad Mehta scam) | HQ Mumbai (BKC)
  • Replaced the Controller of Capital Issues (Capital Issues (Control) Act 1947): merit-based control → disclosure-based regulation
  • Board (s.4) → 9 members = Chairperson + 2 Finance Ministry officials + 1 RBI nominee + 5 other members (at least 3 whole-time)
  • Functions → s.11 | appeals → SAT (Securities Appellate Tribunal, set up 1997; working from 1 Nov 1997) | then Supreme Court
  • First woman Chairperson → Madhabi Puri Buch (2022 to Feb 2025, also first from private sector)
  • Current Chairperson → Tuhin Kanta Pandey, 11th Chairperson, took charge 1 Mar 2025 for 3 years (appointed by ACC on 27 Feb 2025)
  • Chairperson's term → up to 5 years or age 65 as appointed

Which Act gave SEBI statutory powers? Which body did it replace?

Memory tricks

88 born, 92 sworn: "Born 88, Statutory 92".

★ Securities Markets Code, 2025 (pending Bill)

This is a proposed Code to bring securities-market laws together; it is still a Bill, so it is not yet law.

  • Introduced in Lok Sabha on 18 Dec 2025 (Bill No. 200 of 2025) → referred to the Parliamentary Standing Committee on Finance (report presented 23 Jul 2026)
  • Would repeal and replace → SCRA 1956, SEBI Act 1992 and Depositories Act 1996
  • Key proposals → SEBI Board enlarged from 9 to up to 15; separation of investigation and adjudication; mandatory registration of MIIs (Market Infrastructure Institutions: exchanges, depositories, clearing corporations); definition of market abuse; regulatory sandbox; conflict-of-interest disclosure by board members
  • Budget 2021 had announced the plan to consolidate the three Acts

Status: Bill, not yet an Act as of the last check (Oct 2026). Update this entry if it becomes law.

Memory tricks

Three old Acts → One Code: 1956 + 1992 + 1996 → 2025.

CategoryKey rule

Large Cap / Mid Cap / Small Cap / Flexi Cap80% / 65% / 65% / 65% in the named segment

Debt (Macaulay duration)Overnight 1 day; Liquid up to 91 days; Ultra Short 3–6 months; Low-to-short 6–12 months; Short 1–3 yrs; Medium 3–4 yrs; Medium to Long 4–7 yrs; Long above 7 yrs
