Memory tricks

Multi Cap: 25 – 25 – 25 = 75.

4.3 IRDAI and insurance

★ IRDAI at a glance

IRDAI regulates the insurance sector; this card gives how it began, its law and its HQ.

  • Recommended by the Malhotra Committee (R N Malhotra, 1993) → IRDA Act 1999 → IRDAI statutory body formed in 2000 | HQ Hyderabad
  • Insurance Act 1938 | LIC (life insurance nationalised 1956; LIC Act 1956, formed 1 Sep 1956) | GIC and four subsidiaries (1972-73)
  • Current Chairman → Ajay Seth (former DEA Secretary), took charge 1 Sep 2025 for 3 years or age 65; succeeded Debasish Panda
FDI limit in insuranceYear
26%2000 (private entry)
49%2015
74%2021
100%2025 (Sabka Bima Sabki Raksha Act)
Memory tricks

"38, 56, 72, 99": Insurance Act, LIC nationalisation, general insurance nationalisation, IRDA Act.

26 → 49 → 74 → 100: the ladder climbs about every 6 years.

★ Sabka Bima Sabki Raksha (Amendment of Insurance Laws) Act, 2025

This Act amends three insurance laws and raises the limit on foreign ownership of insurers to 100%.

  • Introduced in Lok Sabha 16 Dec 2025 → passed → now an Act
  • Amends three laws → Insurance Act 1938, LIC Act 1956, IRDA Act 1999
  • FDI 74% → 100% | conditions via the Indian Insurance Companies (Foreign Investment) Amendment Rules, notified 30 Dec 2025
  • Penalty cap for non-compliance → ₹1 crore → ₹10 crore
  • Notified 21 Dec 2025; in force 5 Feb 2026 | IRDAI approval needed to transfer shares: threshold 1% → 5% | foreign reinsurer's net owned fund ₹5,000 crore → ₹1,000 crore | one-time registration of intermediaries
  • IRDAI powers expanded → order recovery of wrongful gains, supersede an insurer's board and appoint an administrator, inspect intermediaries
  • Creates a Policyholders' Education and Protection Fund | Insurance laws aligned with the Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023
  • Vision tag → "Insurance for All by 2047"

Which three Acts does it amend, and what is the new FDI limit?

★ IRDAI: Ind AS, D-SIIs and health insurance rules

IRDAI has issued new accounting and health-insurance rules; the table gives the effective dates and the key points.

ItemKey facts
Ind AS for insurersFrom 1 Apr 2026; IRDAI (Actuarial, Finance and Investment Functions of Insurers) (Amendment) Regulations 2026; applies to Life, General, Stand-Alone Health insurers and reinsurers (earlier IGAAP); parallel reporting for 2 years; one-year forbearance if an insurer cannot comply; Ind AS is converged with IFRS and implemented by the Ministry of Corporate Affairs
D-SIIs (Domestic Systemically Important Insurers)FY2025-26: LIC, GIC Re, New India Assurance retain status (too big to fail by size, importance and interconnectedness)
Health insurance rulesMoratorium 8 → 5 years (60 months); 30-day free look; grace period 15 days (monthly) or 30 days (other modes); pro-rata refund on mid-term cancellation
GSTIndividual life and health policies (incl. family floater) and reinsurance exempt from 22 Sep 2025 (was 18%)
Cyber security normsInformation Security Risk Management Committee meets quarterly (earlier twice a year); CISO independent of IT function

D-SIIs = LIC + GIC Re (state-owned reinsurer) + New India. D-SIBs = SBI, HDFC Bank, ICICI Bank.

Name the three D-SIIs. Since when must insurers follow Ind AS?

Why it exists

  • Insurance contracts involve deferred promises requiring strict financial solvency standards to guarantee claim settlement capabilities.
  • Statutory product norms, underwriting disclosures, and capital reserve rules protect policyholders from arbitrary claim repudiation.

How it works

  • Insurers and intermediaries obtain operating licences and maintain statutory solvency margins determined by actuarial calculations.
  • Underwriters evaluate risks, collect policyholder premiums, and maintain dedicated technical reserves in approved asset classes.
  • Claims are adjudicated under mandatory ombudsman and consumer protection regulations enforcing prompt fair settlement.

More fixed facts

  • Health rules: moratorium 8 → 5 years (60 months) (2024); 30-day free look; grace period 15 days (monthly) or 30 days (other modes); pro-rata refund on mid-term cancellation
  • IRDAI D-SIIs for FY 2025-26: LIC, New India Assurance and GIC Re (unchanged)

How it changed

  • May 2026: Finance Ministry notification: 100% FDI in insurance under automatic route; insurance intermediaries 100%
  • April 2026: IRDAI (Actuarial, Finance and Investment Functions of Insurers) (Amendment) Regulations 2026, approved at its 135th meeting

Traps

  • Systemically important domestic insurers maintain additional capital standards due to distress contagion risks.
  • Policyholder grievance redressal follows mandatory response timelines enforced across insurance companies.

In the news

  • Nov 2026: IRDAI master circular: share-transfer fee cut 80% (2 Nov)
  • Oct 2026: IRDAI proposes overhaul of insurance distribution framework
  • Sep 2026: BMIP
  • May 2026: 100% FDI in insurance through automatic route notified
  • Apr 2026: Domestic Systemically Important Insurers FY2025-26
  • Apr 2026: IRDAI: Ind AS implementation for insurers
  • Jan Mar 2026: IRDAI (Protection of Policyholders' Interests and Allied Matters) Regulations 2026
  • Jan Mar 2026: IRDAI cyber security guidelines updated
  • Jan Mar 2026: IRDAI: revised surrender value norms for life insurance

4.4 PFRDA

★ PFRDA and pensions

PFRDA regulates pensions, including the National Pension System, which was started for government employees and then opened to all.

  • Interim authority set up 2003 by government resolution → statutory under PFRDA Act 2013 (in force Feb 2014) | HQ New Delhi
  • NPS (National Pension System) → central government employees from 2004; all citizens from 2009
  • APY (Atal Pension Yojana) → 2015 (unorganised sector)
  • NPS Vatsalya → announced in Budget 2024-25, launched 18 Sep 2024; for minors (under 18, incl. NRI/OCI) through a guardian; minimum ₹250 a year, no maximum; partial withdrawal after 3 years up to 25% of own contribution; at 18 fresh KYC and choice (by 21) to continue, move to NPS Tier I or exit (80% lump sum, 20% annuity; full withdrawal if corpus up to ₹8 lakh)
  • UPS (Unified Pension Scheme) → implemented 1 Apr 2025
  • Current Chairperson → Sivasubramanian Ramann, took charge 20 Jun 2025 for 5 years or age 65; earlier CMD of SIDBI (2021-24), Deputy CAG; succeeded Deepak Mohanty
Memory tricks

PFRDA: "03 interim, 13 Act". NPS 04 for government, 09 for all.

Why it exists

  • Demographic ageing and rising fiscal pension liabilities necessitated a transition from unfunded defined benefits to funded defined contributions.
  • Professional asset management under statutory oversight provides transparent wealth accumulation for old-age financial security.

How it works

  • Subscribers enroll through points of presence and receive a unique permanent retirement account number.
  • Contributions are allocated across regulated pension fund managers investing in equity, government debt, and corporate bonds.
  • Upon reaching retirement age, a portion of the accumulated corpus is converted into regular annuity payouts.

More fixed facts

  • PFRDA: PoP onboarding charge ₹200 per new subscriber (₹100 if fully digital); annual 0.20% of AUM; NPS minimum ₹250 first contribution, ₹10 later

How it changed

  • September 2026: Voluntary contributory pension scheme for medical expenses; pilot (Proof of Concept) in PFRDA's Regulatory Sandbox
  • January 2026: PFRDA panel on assured payouts under NPS: chair Dr M. S. Sahoo (ex-IBBI chair), 15 members

Traps

  • Retirement contributions accumulate in defined individual accounts rather than pooled defined benefit commitments.
  • Subscribers select asset allocation patterns across equity, corporate bonds, and government debt securities.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: BoB enters pension fund business
  • Oct 2026: NPS Tatkal
  • Oct 2026: NPS Central platform for subscribers
  • Sep 2026: PFRDA reclassifies NPS Multiple Scheme Framework by equity share
  • Sep 2026: NPS Swasthya Pension Scheme, 2026: PFRDA operational guidelines
  • May 2026: PFRDA: NPS investment, retirement income and annuity exit
  • May 2026: PFRDA: NPS Sanchay and insider-trading rules
  • Apr 2026: PFRDA and NPS rules
  • Apr 2026: NPS Swasthya Pension Scheme (sandbox)
  • Jan Mar 2026: PFRDA: NPS Swasthya Pension Scheme
  • Jan Mar 2026: PFRDA: banks may set up pension funds; NPS fee reset (Jan 2026)
  • Jan Mar 2026: NPS Vatsalya Scheme Guidelines 2025 (PFRDA)

All India Financial Institutions are national bodies that give long-term finance to one sector, such as agriculture, exports, housing or small business.

Twins of 82 (NABARD, EXIM), twins of 88 (NHB, SEBI), SIDBI 90, NaBFID 21.

NSDL = NSE's depository (1996); CDSL = BSE's (1999).

☆ IDBI Bank: from development finance institution to bank
YearIDBI milestone
1 Jul 1964IDBI (Industrial Development Bank of India) set up under the IDBI Act 1964 as an RBI subsidiary; development finance institution
1976Ownership moved to the Government; apex industrial finance body

☆ FIU-IND and the anti-money-laundering framework
  • FIU-IND (Financial Intelligence Unit–India): set up 2004; central agency to receive, analyse and share suspicious-transaction information
  • Works under PMLA 2002 (Prevention of Money Laundering Act); the Enforcement Directorate investigates and prosecutes
  • Enforces AML/CFT (anti-money-laundering / countering financing of terrorism) norms, including for virtual digital asset (VDA) providers

Why it exists

  • Illicit financial transactions and tax evasion networks undermine economic stability and fund criminal enterprises.
  • A dedicated intelligence processing agency detects illicit financial patterns and empowers investigative agencies with actionable intelligence.

How it works

  • Reporting entities including banks, intermediaries, and digital asset exchanges file statutory cash and suspicious transaction reports.
  • The intelligence unit analyses financial flows using cross-institutional databases and pattern recognition methodologies.
  • Actionable intelligence dossiers are disseminated to law enforcement and international intelligence counterparts for enforcement.

More fixed facts

  • FIU-IND Nov 2004 — Not statutory; reports to the Economic Intelligence Council headed by the Finance Minister; works under the PMLA, 2002
  • Reports to the Economic Intelligence Council (headed by Finance Minister); under Department of Revenue

How it changed

  • September 2026: Reports to the Economic Intelligence Council (headed by Finance Minister); under Department of Revenue

Traps

  • The intelligence unit acts as an analytical coordinating agency rather than conducting physical policing or arrests.
  • Obligated entities must transmit transaction alerts automatically based on objective monetary and risk thresholds.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: FIU-IND issues compliance notices to offshore crypto exchanges
  • May 2026: FIU-IND releases updated AML/CFT guidelines for reporting entities
☆ Minimum public shareholding and public offer (SCRR 1957)
  • SCRR (Securities Contracts (Regulation) Rules, 1957), Rule 19(2)(b): a listed company must keep 25% public shareholding (MPS, minimum public shareholding); the minimum public offer (MPO) at IPO depends on post-issue market capitalisation
  • Amended by the SCRR Amendment Rules 2026 (notified 13 Mar 2026): 25% MPS benchmark unchanged; big issuers get longer timelines
Post-issue market capMinimum public offer (MPO)Time to reach 25% MPS
Up to ₹1,600 crore25%3 years
₹1,600 – 4,000 crore₹400 crore3 years
₹4,000 – 50,000 crore10%3 years
₹50,000 crore – ₹1 lakh crore₹1,000 crore and at least 8%5 years
₹1 lakh crore – ₹5 lakh crore₹6,250 crore and at least 2.75%5 years (10 years if public holding under 15% at listing; 15% due in 5)
Above ₹5 lakh crore₹15,000 crore and at least 1% (at least 2.5% of post-issue capital offered)Same as the row above
  • IFSC-listed companies: MPS reduced from 25% to 10%
  • Superior-voting-rights shares must be listed on the same exchange as ordinary shares

25% MPS never changed; only the timeline and offer size for very large issuers did.

4.7 SEBI, insurance and pension: recent rules (2026)

★ SEBI: recent rules

Fresh SEBI circular numbers and limits that exams pick from.

Memory tricks

Numbers by theme: 3 nominees, 21 days, 10 years, 30 days, 10,000 clients.

Why it exists

  • Foreign capital inflows deepen market liquidity but create risks of sudden capital flights during international crises.
  • Categorized registration and aggregate macro-prudential ceilings allow foreign capital participation while preserving domestic financial stability.

How it works

  • Overseas entities register through designated depository participants under regulatory categories matching their institutional structure.
  • Investors purchase listed equities, corporate bonds, and sovereign securities within established broad market ceilings.
  • Custodians monitor overall foreign investment caps and verify periodic customer identity compliance.

More fixed facts

  • SWAGAT-FI (Single Window Automatic and Generalized Access for Trusted Foreign Investors): unified registration for FPIs and FVCIs from 1 Jun 2026
  • FPIs may trade non-agricultural index derivatives and non-cash-settled non-agri commodity derivatives

How it changed

  • October 2026: KYC review periodicity: 1 year → 2 years; 3 years → 8 years (Category I FPIs and regulated Category II FPIs from non-high-risk jurisdictions get 8 years)
  • June 2026: SWAGAT-FI (Single Window Automatic and Generalized Access for Trusted Foreign Investors): unified registration for FPIs and FVCIs from 1 Jun 2026

Traps

  • Foreign investors must register under designated categories rather than operating without intermediary supervision.
  • Review periods vary depending on regulatory category and jurisdiction risk classification.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: SEBI eases KYC review periodicity for foreign portfolio investors
  • May 2026: SEBI: closing auction session (CAS) and FPI reforms
  • Jan Mar 2026: RBI: VRR folded into General Route
☆ Insurance and pension regulators: recent moves
  • IRDAI D-SIIs for FY 2025-26: LIC, New India Assurance and GIC Re (unchanged)
  • IRDAI KMP pay norms (from 2027): 50% of variable pay linked to customer-centric measures such as claims responsiveness, grievance redressal and removal of dark patterns
  • PFRDA Pension Sahayak: AI-enabled grievance platform for NPS, Unified Pension Scheme and APY subscribers
  • PFRDA PRIDE-DISHA: tool to compare pension fund managers on actual contribution patterns
★ Real Estate and Infrastructure Investment Trusts
In plain words: Pooled investment trusts that own income-generating real estate or infrastructure assets and issue tradeable units to investors.

Why it exists

  • Direct acquisition of commercial real estate or infrastructure projects requires enormous capital beyond individual retail reach.
  • Trust structures aggregate distributed savings into professional grade operating assets while providing regular cash distributions.

How it works

  • A corporate sponsor transfers operational commercial property or infrastructure assets into an investment trust.
  • The trust raises capital by issuing units traded freely on stock exchanges to institutional and retail unitholders.
  • Operational earnings from property rentals or infrastructure user tolls are passed through to unitholders as periodic dividends.

Fixed facts

  • DR = foreign-currency instrument issued abroad against Indian securities held with a domestic custodian → lets overseas investors trade Indian units
  • Permitted exchanges: India INX and NSE IX (both IFSC, GIFT City)
  • DRs held only by non-residents
  • Units behind DRs cannot be priced below the price offered to Indian investors for a similar offering
  • Disclosures made on the international exchange → also filed with Indian exchanges within 24 hours; manager appoints a merchant banker
  • Privately placed InvITs: fresh DRs subscribed only by FIIs and corporate bodies

How it changed

  • November 2026: Fresh DR issue → approval of at least 75% of votes cast by unitholders; not needed for a simultaneous initial offer in India + DR listing abroad
  • April 2026: InvITs with net borrowings above 49% of asset value: fresh borrowings allowed for capex to enhance asset performance or capacity, major maintenance of road projects, and refinancing of principal only (regulations amended 17 April 2026)

Traps

  • Depository receipts backed by units can only be held by non-resident investors.
  • The pricing of underlying units cannot fall below the valuation offered to domestic Indian unitholders.

In the news

  • Nov 2026: SEBI permits depository receipts (DRs) for REITs and InvITs
  • Apr 2026: SEBI amends InvIT borrowing and SPV rules
  • Jan Mar 2026: RIIT (NHAI InvIT) listed on BSE
★ Equity and Commodity Derivatives risk framework
In plain words: A market risk containment framework comprising standardised risk indicators, auction sessions, position limits, and progressive penalty slabs.

Why it exists

  • Excessive speculative leverage and concentrated positions threaten market stability and retail investor capital safety.
  • Structured closing mechanisms and standardised disclosure meters ensure transparent risk assessment and orderly contract settlement.

How it works

  • The regulator defines objective categorisation criteria and prudential position caps for participating market entities.
  • Exchanges enforce price bands and designated auction intervals during market closing to prevent artificial price manipulation.
  • Surveillance systems compute daily position breaches and impose statutory penalties credited to investor protection funds.

Fixed facts

  • CAS in equity cash market: 20 minutes, 3:15–3:35 pm; starts with stocks having derivatives; price band ±3% of reference price; implemented from 3 Aug 2026; revised pre-open auction from 7 Sep 2026
  • Penalty per day: violation over 2% of limit → 2% of (excess × closing price) or ₹2,00,000, whichever lower; up to 2% → ₹10,000 cap; proceeds go to exchange Investor Protection Fund; >3 instances in a month → one-day square-off mode / double penalty
  • Client-level limits for agri commodities doubled: Broad 2%, Narrow 1%, Sensitive 0.5% of deliverable supply

How it changed

  • August 2026: CAS in equity cash market: 20 minutes, 3:15–3:35 pm; starts with stocks having derivatives; price band ±3% of reference price; implemented from 3 Aug 2026; revised pre-open auction from 7 Sep 2026
  • September 2026: Penalty per day: violation over 2% of limit → 2% of (excess × closing price) or ₹2,00,000, whichever lower; up to 2% → ₹10,000 cap; proceeds go to exchange Investor Protection Fund; >3 instances in a month → one-day square-off mode / double penalty

Traps

  • Closing auction sessions apply to designated cash equity scrips with active derivative contracts.
  • Position limit violation penalties are subject to progressive daily caps and enforcement measures.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: Credit Risk-o-Meter (colour-coded) mandatory
  • Oct 2026: CAS revised
  • Sep 2026: SEBI revises commodity derivatives position limits and penalties
  • May 2026: SEBI: closing auction session (CAS) and FPI reforms
  • Jan Mar 2026: SEBI circular: Credit Risk-o-Meter for mutual funds
★ Social Stock Exchange segment
In plain words: A specialized stock exchange platform allowing registered non-profit and social enterprises to raise capital for public welfare activities.

Why it exists

  • Non-profit social organisations frequently struggle to secure sustained funding through conventional charity channels or bank lending.
  • An exchange-traded transparent mechanism connects social impact organisations with retail and institutional philanthropic funders.

How it works

  • Eligible social welfare organisations register on the exchange segment after satisfying governance criteria.
  • Entities issue specialised social financing instruments without commercial dividend or repayment obligations to raise contributions.
  • Annual impact reporting verifies social outcomes achieved with the raised philanthropic funds.

Fixed facts

  • Social Impact Fund Minimum investment by individuals cut from ₹2 lakh to ₹1,000
  • MCA: CSR can now go to Zero Coupon Zero Principal instruments on the Social Stock Exchange (new item xiii, Schedule VII, Companies Act 2013)

How it changed

  • October 2026: Social Impact Fund Minimum investment by individuals cut from ₹2 lakh to ₹1,000
  • January 2026: MCA: CSR can now go to Zero Coupon Zero Principal instruments on the Social Stock Exchange (new item xiii, Schedule VII, Companies Act 2013)

Traps

  • Instruments on this exchange segment carry zero interest coupons and return no principal at maturity.
  • Corporate CSR allocations can be deployed into eligible registered social impact issues.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: SEBI cuts minimum investment in Social Impact Funds to ₹1,000
  • Jan Mar 2026: Social Stock Exchange: NABARD subscribes to first ZCZP issue
  • Jan Mar 2026: MCA: CSR contributions eligible for Social Stock Exchange ZCZP instruments
★ Portfolio Management Services regulations
In plain words: A discretionary and non-discretionary investment framework where licensed professionals manage customized portfolios for high net worth clients.

Why it exists

  • Sophisticated high net worth investors require tailored investment strategies beyond standard retail pooled fund offerings.
  • Statutory manager qualification rules and minimum client investment thresholds ensure investor protection and fiduciary accountability.

How it works

  • A licensed manager enters into a formal fiduciary agreement with an eligible client possessing the statutory minimum corpus.
  • The manager invests client funds directly in approved securities, primary debt, or specialised funds matching the agreed risk profile.
  • Portfolio holdings remain directly owned in the client individual demat account and receive regular statutory valuation reports.

Fixed facts

  • SEBI (Portfolio Managers) Regulations, 2026 replace the 2020 rules; effective 1 Apr 2027.
  • Managers may invest in IPOs, debt primary issues, listed securities, money market instruments, mutual funds; also foreign securities, unlisted securities, exchange-traded derivatives.

How it changed

  • October 2026: SEBI (Portfolio Managers) Regulations, 2026 replace the 2020 rules; effective 1 Apr 2027.
  • October 2026: Managers may invest in IPOs, debt primary issues, listed securities, money market instruments, mutual funds; also foreign securities, unlisted securities, exchange-traded derivatives.

Traps

  • Discretionary managers execute portfolio adjustments on behalf of the client without prior trade confirmation.
  • Client accounts remain distinct individual segregated portfolios rather than collective pooled investment vehicles.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: Portfolio Managers Regulations, 2026 + PRIM route
  • Oct 2026: SEBI eases co-investment PMS norms for AIF managers
★ International Financial Services Centres Authority
In plain words: A unified statutory regulator governing financial institutions, financial products, and securities transactions within special international offshore financial zones.

Why it exists

  • Cross-border financial transactions conducted through domestic banking channels faced complex fragmented domestic regulatory overlapping.
  • A single offshore authority provides competitive global financial regulations to repatriate financial services onshore.

How it works

  • The authority exercises consolidated regulatory powers of domestic regulators over entities located within the designated international zone.
  • Global institutions establish offshore banking units, international exchanges, and insurance offices operating in freely convertible foreign currencies.
  • Cross-border asset trades and international debt listings take place under competitive tax and regulatory regimes.

Fixed facts

  • IFSCA IFSCA Act 2019; set up 2020; HQ GIFT City, Gandhinagar
  • IFSC-listed companies: MPS reduced from 25% to 10%
  • Permitted exchanges: India INX and NSE IX (both IFSC, GIFT City)

How it changed

  • May 2026: IFSC-listed companies: MPS reduced from 25% to 10%

Traps

  • Entities operating inside the financial centre are treated as non-resident offshore units under currency regulations.
  • Regulatory oversight is unified under the dedicated authority rather than dispersed across domestic agencies.

In the news

  • Nov 2026: SEBI permits depository receipts (DRs) for REITs and InvITs
  • May 2026: IFSCA: MPS for listed companies cut to 10%
★ Index of Eight Core Industries
In plain words: A monthly production index tracking the physical output volume of foundational infrastructure and energy industries.

Why it exists

  • Core infrastructure sectors drive downstream manufacturing activity and signal industrial output trends early.
  • Frequent volume tracking of heavy industries provides essential leading data for business cycle surveillance.

How it works

  • Designated industrial authorities collect physical output volumes from major producers across core utility and mineral sectors.
  • Sectoral production metrics are indexed against base year volumes and weighted by capital intensity and industrial share.
  • Statistical agencies release the composite index monthly ahead of broad industrial production aggregates.

Fixed facts

  • New base 2022-23 (old 2011-12); core industries 8 → 9 with Iron Ore added
  • Base year 2022-23; covers 9 industries: coal, crude oil, natural gas, refinery products, fertilizers, steel, cement, electricity, iron ore. Biggest weights: electricity (30.932), refinery products (22.572), steel (17.584).

How it changed

  • September 2026: Provisional ICI growth 7.9% year-on-year, vs revised 5.9% in August (revised from 4.8%); index 118.3 (109.6 a year earlier); cumulative April–September growth 5.1% (2.5% a year earlier).
  • September 2026: Weights: Electricity 30.932% > Refinery products 22.572% > Steel 17.584% > Crude 7.430% > Coal 5.596% > Iron ore 4.905% > Cement 4.410% > Natural gas 3.841% > Fertilisers 2.731%

Traps

  • The composite index tracks physical output volume rather than financial turnover or corporate profitability.
  • Electricity and refinery products carry heavier statistical weightings than basic mineral extraction.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: ICI, September 2026
  • Sep 2026: ICI (new base 2022-23)
  • Apr 2026: Index of Eight Core Industries, March 2026
  • Jan Mar 2026: Eight Core Industries Feb 2026 and other data
★ Retail and wholesale inflation metrics
In plain words: Macroeconomic price indices measuring headline changes in consumer retail living costs and producer wholesale transactions.

Why it exists

  • Price stability is essential for preserving household purchasing power and guiding central bank monetary policy adjustments.
  • Separate retail and wholesale baskets distinguish end-consumer price pressures from upstream farm and factory price movements.

How it works

  • Field enumerators collect retail prices across urban and rural markets while trade offices track wholesale mandi and factory rates.
  • Price changes are aggregated using statistical baskets reflecting nationwide household consumption and wholesale trade expenditure.
  • Authorities compute monthly consumer and wholesale price indices to evaluate cost trends across commodity groups.

Fixed facts

  • CPI (2024=100) by MoSPI/NSO: 358 weighted items (299 earlier; 308 goods + 50 services), 12 divisions
  • Top weights: Food & beverages 36.75%; Housing, water, electricity, gas & fuels 17.67%; Transport 8.80%; Clothing 6.38%; Health 6.10%

How it changed

  • September 2026: WPI inflation: 9.92% (Aug) → 11.39% (Sep 2026); retail (CPI) inflation 4.83% → 5.54%.
  • September 2026: Revised WPI series introduced June 2026: base year 2022–23; basket 697 → 957 items; now includes solar, wind and nuclear electricity.

Traps

  • Monetary policy targets headline retail consumer prices rather than upstream wholesale trade indices.
  • Food and beverage expenditure carries a substantial weighting in consumer price measurement.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: WPI inflation, September 2026
  • Sep 2026: CPI inflation August 2026: 4.82% (new base 2024=100)
  • Sep 2026: WPI inflation August 2026: 9.92%
  • May 2026: Price, output and jobs data released in May 2026
  • Apr 2026: Inflation, March 2026
  • Jan Mar 2026: CPI inflation Dec 2025 and base-year revision
  • Jan Mar 2026: Price and output data: January 2026
  • Jan Mar 2026: Monthly numbers: Feb 2026 data (released Mar)
  • Jan Mar 2026: Trade and inflation data, Feb 2026
  • Jan Mar 2026: December 2025 data snapshot: UPI, WPI, trade, PMI
★ National Accounts and Gross Domestic Product
In plain words: The comprehensive macroeconomic accounting framework quantifying aggregate economic output, value addition, and national income.

Why it exists

  • Policymakers require systematic standardized national accounting to assess economic growth, productivity, and sectoral performance.
  • Distinguishing nominal price expansion from real volume output allows accurate evaluation of living standard improvements.

How it works

  • Statistical authorities collect enterprise survey data, tax filings, and agricultural yields across economic sectors.
  • Gross output is adjusted for intermediate consumption to compute sectoral gross value added across primary, secondary, and tertiary activities.
  • National economic aggregates are deflated against base year prices to estimate real output growth.

Fixed facts

  • Real GDP ₹81.36 lakh crore ( 7.8% ); nominal GDP ₹88.27 lakh crore (10.3%)
  • Real GVA ₹73.82 lakh crore ( 8.2% ); nominal GVA ₹80.53 lakh crore (11.5%)
  • Actual Q1 FY27 (Apr–Jun 2026) growth: 7.8% (National Statistical Office, NSO estimate).

How it changed

  • September 2026: Real GDP ₹81.36 lakh crore ( 7.8% ); nominal GDP ₹88.27 lakh crore (10.3%)
  • September 2026: 7.8% = highest Q1 growth during 2023-24 to 2026-27 (manufacturing, services, domestic demand)

Traps

  • Real output estimates isolate volume changes by stripping away price inflation effects.
  • Gross value added measures economic production before accounting for net indirect product taxes.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: India GDP growth: who projected what
  • Sep 2026: GDP Q1 FY2026-27: real growth 7.8%
  • Apr 2026: Base year 2022-23: GSVA and DDP guidelines
  • Jan Mar 2026: GDP First Advance Estimates FY2025-26 (Jan 2026)
  • Jan Mar 2026: National Accounts base year revision (GDP new series)
★ World Gold Council demand and reserve tracking

In plain words: The World Gold Council tracks international bullion movements, central bank sovereign reserve accumulations, and jewellery consumption trends across major economies.

Why it exists

Central banks and institutional investors need systematic global surveillance of bullion reserves and physical gold demand to monitor monetary stability and macroeconomic liquidity.

How it works

Quarterly demand surveys synthesize physical buying, bar and coin accumulation, exchange-traded fund activity, and central bank reserve acquisitions across sovereign jurisdictions.

Fixed facts

  • India demand Q3 2026: 162.5 tonnes (down 29% on year); value ₹2.42 lakh crore (up 3%)
  • Bars and coins = India's largest gold investment channel; India 2nd-largest bar and coin market after China, about 21% of global demand
  • Central banks (Q3): China 63 t (largest disclosed buyer); Poland 41 t (2nd); Russia sold 19 t (only major seller)

Traps

Do not confuse value metrics with tonnage, as elevated global gold prices can increase total expenditure even when physical volume demand drops sharply.

In the news

  • World Gold Council: India gold demand Q3 2026; central bank buying
★ SEBI settlement mechanisms and consent proceedings

In plain words: Legal mechanisms allow entities facing regulatory enforcement actions to settle alleged securities law violations by paying monetary amounts and accepting behavioral undertakings without admitting guilt.

Why it exists

The securities regulator needs an efficient administrative procedure to resolve enforcement proceedings swiftly without enduring protracted and costly judicial litigation.

How it works

Settlement amounts are calculated using structured formulas based on base penalty minimums, legal costs, and gravity factors, alongside fast-track routes for smaller monetary claims.

Fixed facts

  • Who: SEBI. Affects: entities settling enforcement cases.
  • Fast-track settlement for amounts up to ₹10 lakh: two types → violation-based and monetary-threshold-based.

Traps

Settlement does not constitute an admission or denial of guilt, and ill-gotten gains subject to disgorgement cannot be rolled into the basic settlement fee.

In the news

  • SEBI (Settlement Proceedings) Regulations, 2018 (amended)
★ Nidhi Companies regulatory framework

In plain words: Non-banking mutual benefit financial entities operate exclusively among their members to cultivate thrift, accepting deposits and advancing small loans secured against physical collateral.

Why it exists

Traditional mutual benefit societies enable grassroots financial cooperation among local members, but require strict statutory oversight to protect member savings from unauthorized commercial expansion.

How it works

Regulated under company law, these entities cannot solicit public deposits, transact with non-members, or issue unsecured loans, and must maintain unencumbered reserves with commercial banks.

Fixed facts

  • Nidhi = mutual-benefit company: deposits/loans only from members; Section 406, Companies Act 2013, Nidhi Rules 2014
  • Central Govt declaration (Form NDH-4) mandatory again from 15 Aug 2019; 395 declared Nidhis; deposits not DICGC-insured

Traps

Deposits placed with Nidhi companies are strictly excluded from statutory deposit insurance coverage, distinguishing them from commercial and cooperative bank deposits.

In the news

  • Nidhi Companies: regulatory framework and compliance
★ Share buybacks: tender offer vs open-market route

In plain words: Listed corporate entities repurchase their own outstanding equity shares from existing investors either through structured tender offers or directly via open-market exchange mechanisms.

Why it exists

Companies utilize surplus cash reserves to optimize capital structure, return capital to shareholders, and signal intrinsic corporate valuation without distributing taxable dividends.

How it works

Capital market regulations govern buyback methods, setting maximum duration limits, escrow requirements, minimum promoter lock-in standards, and percentage volume limits.

Fixed facts

  • Buyback through stock exchanges (additional proposals after PMAC consultation): complete within 66 working days of offer opening; at least 40% of buyback size to be used in the first half of the offer period

Traps

Do not confuse the tender offer route where all shareholders tender shares pro-rata with open-market exchange buybacks where shares are repurchased over designated trading sessions.

In the news

  • SEBI: buyback and IPO norms
  • SEBI proposals: GARUDA for AIFs and open-market buybacks
★ Market indices and their regulation

In plain words: Statutory regulations govern administrators and benchmark providers that calculate financial market indices used for benchmarking investment funds and settling derivative products.

Why it exists

Systemic financial products and exchange-traded funds rely on benchmark indices, requiring transparent governance, conflict-of-interest controls, and reliable computation methodologies.

How it works

The securities regulator requires providers of significant market indices to obtain formal registration, maintain governance oversight committees, and submit to periodic compliance audits.

Fixed facts

  • Under SEBI (Index Providers) Regulations 2024; applies to indices of securities listed on Indian exchanges
  • Significant index: daily average cumulative AUM of mutual fund schemes tracking it above ₹20,000 crore for each of the past six months, ending 30 June and 31 December

Traps

Index provider regulations apply exclusively to benchmarks of securities listed on domestic exchanges; commodities and foreign instruments are excluded from this framework.

In the news

  • SEBI: "Significant Indices" and registration of index providers
★ Market intermediaries: merchant bankers, brokers, registration

In plain words: Registered market participants, including stock brokers, merchant bankers, and portfolio managers, facilitate securities issuances and client trading under statutory capital and conduct rules.

Why it exists

Orderly functioning of capital markets requires reliable intermediaries with adequate financial standing, mandatory client disclosures, and robust investor protection mechanisms.

How it works

Intermediaries must meet net worth thresholds, display risk disclosures, separate proprietary funds from client monies, and maintain mandatory registrations with market authorities.

Fixed facts

  • Merchant banker Category 1: Capital adequacy ₹50 crore by 2 Jan 2028
  • 5–31 Oct 2026 (World Investor Week): websites must show awareness messages + risk disclosures; trading apps voluntary (if shown, risk disclosure optional).

Traps

Risk disclosures and investor awareness notices are mandatory on broker platforms, differentiating regular commercial advertisements from statutory warning messages.

In the news

  • SEBI: non-individual PMS managers; revised timeline for Merchant Banker norms
  • Investor awareness messages by brokers (Project Jagrook)

5. Government Committees, Commissions and Reports

Basics for beginners: how to read this section

A committee or commission is set up to study one problem and make recommendations; it has no power to force the Government to accept them.

The Finance Commission is the exception: it is a constitutional body (Art 280) that decides how tax money is shared between Centre and States.

Banking, fiscal and tax committees led to many of the reforms in earlier sections; each row gives the year and the result.

5.1 Finance Commissions

★ Finance Commission basics

The Finance Commission is the body set up every five years to decide how tax money is shared between the Centre and the States.

  • Art 280 → President constitutes it every 5 years | chairman + 4 members | conditions of service → Finance Commission (Miscellaneous Provisions) Act 1951
  • Art 281 → President lays its report before Parliament with an explanatory memorandum (action taken)
  • Recommends → vertical devolution (Centre → states share of net tax proceeds), horizontal devolution (among states), grants-in-aid (Art 275), local body grants, and disaster funding
  • Recommendations are advisory, but conventionally accepted
  • Art 270 → taxes shared between Centre and states | cess and surcharge stay outside the divisible pool
Memory tricks

280 = Constitutes, 281 = Reports to Parliament (28 + 0/1: "zero starts, one lays it before").

★ RBI Economic Capital Framework (Bimal Jalan, 2018-19)

This committee looked at how much capital the RBI should keep as a cushion and how much of its surplus it can pass to the Government.

  • Committee set up by RBI in Nov 2018 under Bimal Jalan (former Governor) | accepted in Aug 2019
  • Two components of economic capital → realised equity (the Contingent Risk Buffer, CRB) and revaluation balances
  • CRB range → 5.5% to 6.5% of RBI's balance sheet | RBI set it at 5.5% in 2019 (was 6.5% as at 31 Mar 2024)
  • Framework to be reviewed every 5 years | an RBI internal committee is reviewing it (Governor Malhotra, Feb 2025)
  • RBI's accounting year July–June aligned with April–March from 2020-21; interim dividends only in extraordinary conditions
Surplus transfer to CentreAmount
FY19 (Aug 2019)₹1.76 lakh crore = ₹1.23 lakh crore surplus + ₹52,637 crore excess provisions
FY20 / FY21 / FY22 / FY23₹57,128 crore / ₹99,122 crore / ₹30,307 crore / ₹87,416 crore
FY24₹2.1 lakh crore (highest ever at the time)

World Population ProspectsUN DESA

"Doing Business" (World Bank) was discontinued in 2021 after data irregularities.

Why it exists

  • Traditional income poverty measures overlook simultaneous deprivations in nutrition, sanitation, clean energy, and schooling.
  • Multidimensional evaluation provides detailed granular indicators to guide targeted social welfare interventions.

How it works

  • Household demographic surveys collect micro-data spanning ten indicators across health, education, and living standards.
  • Deprivation profiles are constructed for every surveyed individual by weighting household deprivations equally across dimensions.
  • Statistical agencies compute the composite index by combining poverty incidence headcounts with deprivation intensity ratios.

More fixed facts

  • Three dimensions: Health, Education, Standard of Living; scale 0–1 (higher = poorer).
  • Coverage: 108 countries, 6.3 bn people; 1.2 bn in acute multidimensional poverty (18.9%); 617 mn are children; 83% rural.
  • First rise in numbers since MPI began in 2010. 33 countries halved MPI in under 15 years.
  • Highest: Chad 0.517 (incidence 84.2%), then Niger, CAR, Burundi, Madagascar. Lowest: Serbia 0.000, Azerbaijan, Armenia, Kazakhstan, Turkmenistan.
  • India: MPI 0.283 → 0.069 (2005/06 → 2019/21, 14.5 years); incidence 55.1% → 16.4%.

How it changed

  • October 2026: Coverage: 108 countries, 6.3 bn people; 1.2 bn in acute multidimensional poverty (18.9%); 617 mn are children; 83% rural.
  • October 2026: First rise in numbers since MPI began in 2010. 33 countries halved MPI in under 15 years.

Traps

  • The index evaluates non-income deprivations across living standards rather than monetary wage levels alone.
  • A higher numerical score denotes greater poverty intensity and deprivation across population groups.

In the news

  • Oct 2026: Global MPI 2026 (UNDP + OPHI)

  • Report on Trend and Progress of Banking in India: statutory under Section 36(2) of the BR Act, 1949
  • Pairs to remember: HDI = UNDP; Gender Gap = WEF; Press Freedom = RSF; CPI = Transparency International; Peace and Terrorism = IEP
Memory tricks

"G-P-T" IEP gives Peace (127) and Terrorism (13). WEF gives Gender Gap. UNDP gives HDI.

★ Periodic Labour Force Survey
In plain words: A nationwide statistical survey providing frequent estimates of labour market indicators including employment, unemployment, and participation rates.

Why it exists

  • Earlier quinquennial employment surveys failed to track rapid labour market shifts and emerging employment trends.
  • Frequent quarterly urban and annual rural surveys provide reliable timely data for evidence-based macroeconomic policymaking.

How it works

  • The national statistical office conducts probability sampling of rural and urban households across all administrative units.
  • Field enumerators record activity statuses, hours worked, earnings, and industry classifications using standardised questionnaires.
  • Data processing compiles participation rates, worker population ratios, and open unemployment rates.

Fixed facts

  • Periodic Labour Force Survey (NSO, MoSPI); usual status (ps+ss), age 15+
  • LFPR 59.3% (male 79.1%, female 40.0%) | WPR 57.4% | UR 3.1% (male 3.3% → 3.1%; female 3.1%)
  • Youth (15–29) UR 10.3% → 9.9%; rural youth 8.3%; urban youth 13.6%; urban UR 4.8%; rural UR 2.4%

How it changed

  • November 2026: Rural unemployment 4.2% (from 4.6%); rural female unemployment 3.9%, the lowest recorded in 2026; rural LFPR 58%; rural female LFPR 38.9%; urban female LFPR 25.9%.
  • September 2026: Unemployment rate, overall 5.1% (was 5.4% previous quarter)

Traps

  • Usual status measures activity over a longer reference year rather than current weekly status.
  • The survey measures both rural annual households and quarterly rotational urban samples.

In the news

  • Nov 2026: PLFS quarterly bulletin (July–September 2026)
  • Oct 2026: PLFS monthly bulletin, September 2026
  • Apr 2026: PLFS annual report 2024-25
  • Jan Mar 2026: PLFS quarterly bulletin (October–December 2025)
★ Annual Survey of Industries framework
In plain words: The principal statistical survey capturing detailed economic parameters, capital structure, and employment in the registered manufacturing sector.

Why it exists

  • Macroeconomic national accounting requires reliable empirical data on industrial output, value addition, and factory employment.
  • A comprehensive census of registered industrial units provides foundational data for industrial policy formulation and economic planning.

How it works

  • Statistical authorities enumerate registered factories meeting threshold employment criteria across nationwide industrial zones.
  • Enterprises furnish audited records detailing fixed capital investments, fuel consumption, raw material inputs, and wages paid.
  • Aggregated survey outputs compute manufacturing gross value added, capital formation, and employment growth across industrial categories.

Fixed facts

  • Top 5 states by GVA (over 54%): Maharashtra, Gujarat, Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Uttar Pradesh
  • Top 5 industries by employment (over 41%): food products, textiles, basic metals, motor vehicles, wearing apparel
  • Gross value of plant and machinery (registered manufacturing): up 7.25%

How it changed

  • November 2026: Top 5 states by GVA (over 54%): Maharashtra, Gujarat, Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Uttar Pradesh
  • November 2026: Top 5 industries by employment (over 41%): food products, textiles, basic metals, motor vehicles, wearing apparel

Traps

  • The survey covers formal registered factories under the Factories Act rather than the unorganised informal manufacturing sector.
  • Gross value added reflects industrial output net of intermediate manufacturing consumption.

In the news

  • Nov 2026: ASI 2024-25 results
  • Oct 2026: ASI 2024-25

6. Schemes and Acts with Launch Year and Ministry

Basics for beginners: how to read this section

A scheme is a Government programme run by a ministry for a target group; the key facts are launch year, ministry and target group.

An Act is a law passed by Parliament; the Acts table lists the year and the ministry responsible.

Check the "Launch, Ministry, Target group" columns first; those are the most common questions.

6.1 Financial inclusion and social security

★ Scheme-at-a-glance: inclusion and Jan Suraksha

These are the main schemes for financial inclusion and for insurance and pension cover, with the launch date, ministry and target group.

SchemeLaunchMinistryTarget groupKey feature
PMJDY (Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana)28 Aug 2014Finance (Dept of Financial Services)Unbanked householdsZero-balance account, RuPay debit card with accident cover, overdraft facility, DBT
PMJJBY (Jeevan Jyoti Bima)9 May 2015Finance (DFS)Age 18–50₹2 lakh life cover (death by any cause); renewable yearly
PMSBY (Suraksha Bima)9 May 2015Finance (DFS)Age 18–70₹2 lakh accidental death or total disability; ₹1 lakh partial; ₹20 a year
APY (Atal Pension Yojana)9 May 2015 (Kolkata)Finance (DFS), run by PFRDAAge 18–40, unorganised sectorGuaranteed pension from age 60; extended by Cabinet to FY 2030-31 (21 Jan 2026)

How it works → Open PMJDY account → get RuPay card → enrol in PMJJBY / PMSBY / APY through the same bank (auto-debit) → claim or pension paid to account → DBT of subsidies lands in the same account.

JAM Trinity = Jan Dhan + Aadhaar + Mobile. PMJDY launched 28 Aug 2014 (its anniversary is 28 Aug).

Memory tricks

Age ceilings "50-70-40": PMJJBY 50 (Jeevan = Jee-Vita 50), PMSBY 70, APY 40. All three launched on one day, 9 May 2015, from Kolkata.

★ Credit schemes for small business

These schemes help small businesses get loans, with the launch year, ministry and who they are meant for.

SchemeLaunchMinistryTarget groupKey feature
PMMY (Pradhan Mantri MUDRA Yojana)8 Apr 2015Finance (DFS)Non-corporate, non-farm micro and small enterprisesCollateral-free loans up to ₹20 lakh through Member Lending Institutions
Stand-Up India5 Apr 2016Finance (DFS)SC/ST and women entrepreneursBank loan of ₹10 lakh to ₹1 crore for greenfield enterprises; extended only for 2020-25, scheme period ended March 2025
PM SVANidhi1 Jun 2020Housing and Urban AffairsStreet vendorsGraduated collateral-free working-capital loans
PMMY categoryLoan size
Shishuup to ₹50,000
Kishore₹50,000 to ₹5 lakh
Tarun₹5 lakh to ₹10 lakh
Tarun Plus₹10 lakh to ₹20 lakh (for those who repaid Tarun loans; effective 24 Oct 2024, Budget 2024-25)

How it works → Entrepreneur applies at bank, NBFC or MFI → collateral-free loan → MUDRA card for working capital → repayment → eligible for next category.

Memory tricks

Loan ladder "50k – 5L – 10L – 20L": child (Shishu), youth (Kishore), grown (Tarun), grown plus (Tarun Plus).

Why it exists

  • Small micro-enterprises and informal vendors lack collateral assets, cutting them off from formal institutional banking credit.
  • Refinancing banks and providing institutional credit guarantees expands micro-enterprise self-employment and grassroots entrepreneurship.

How it works

  • Borrowers apply to participating commercial banks, cooperative lenders, or microfinance institutions for micro-enterprise credit.
  • Loans are sanctioned across graded categories matching the borrower business development stage without physical collateral requirements.
  • The specialized refinancing agency provides institutional refinancing and credit guarantee protection to lending institutions.

More fixed facts

  • Launched 8 April 2015; loans 57 crore+, ₹40.07 lakh cr (27 Mar 2026); collateral-free up to ₹20 lakh; MUDRA = Micro Units Development and Refinance Agency; guarantee via CGFMU (NCGTC)
  • Women: 59.81% of accounts, 37.45% of amount; new entrepreneurs 21%; SC/ST/OBC 45.52% of accounts
  • Top states by disbursal: UP ₹58,111 cr > Bihar ₹54,064 cr > Maharashtra ₹50,762 cr; MUDRA Ltd. record profit ₹827 cr+ (FY25); JanSamarth: 14 schemes, 7 loan categories, 200+ lenders

How it changed

  • April 2026: Launched 8 April 2015; loans 57 crore+, ₹40.07 lakh cr (27 Mar 2026); collateral-free up to ₹20 lakh; MUDRA = Micro Units Development and Refinance Agency; guarantee via CGFMU (NCGTC)
  • April 2026: Top states by disbursal: UP ₹58,111 cr > Bihar ₹54,064 cr > Maharashtra ₹50,762 cr; MUDRA Ltd. record profit ₹827 cr+ (FY25); JanSamarth: 14 schemes, 7 loan categories, 200+ lenders

Traps

  • Micro loans under the scheme require zero collateral security from eligible non-farm micro borrowers.
  • Credit is extended directly by commercial and cooperative lending institutions backed by apex refinance.

In the news

  • Apr 2026: PM MUDRA Yojana: 11 years
★ PM SVANidhi (restructured 2025)

PM SVANidhi gives small working-capital loans to street vendors; this card gives its restructuring in 2025.

  • Full form → Pradhan Mantri Street Vendor's AtmaNirbhar Nidhi | Central sector scheme of the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs | launched 2020 (Covid relief)
  • Cabinet approved restructuring on 27 Aug 2025 → lending period extended to 31 Mar 2030 (earlier ended 31 Dec 2024) | outlay ₹7,332 crore | target 1.15 crore vendors, including 50 lakh new
  • Loan ladder (enhanced): ₹15,000 (was ₹10,000, 12 months) → ₹25,000 (was ₹20,000, 18 months) → ₹50,000 (unchanged, 36 months)
  • Extras → 7% interest subvention on timely repayment | UPI-linked RuPay credit card after repaying the second loan | digital cashback up to ₹1,600 | coverage widened to census towns and peri-urban areas
Memory tricks

"15-25-50" replaces "10-20-50": only the first two tranches moved.

☆ Sovereign Gold Bonds (SGB)
  • Launched 5 Nov 2015 alongside the Gold Monetisation Scheme and Indian Gold Coin; notified 14 Jan 2016 under the Government Securities Act 2006
  • Issued by RBI on behalf of the Government of India; denominated in grams (1 gram and multiples)
  • Interest 2.5% a year, paid half-yearly; tenor 8 years, premature exit after the 5th year, on interest dates
  • Eligible: resident individuals, HUFs, trusts, universities, charitable institutions; limit up to 4 kg per financial year
  • Capital gains on redemption exempt for individuals (from tax year 2026-27, only for bonds bought at original issue and held to maturity); interest taxed at slab rates
  • Last tranche: Series 2023-24 IV (21 Feb 2024); Government confirmed in Feb 2025 that no further tranches will be issued; existing bonds continue
Memory tricks

2.5 – 8 – 5 = interest %, years to maturity, year of exit.

6.2 Employment, labour and rural livelihoods

★ VB-G RAM G Act, 2025 (replaced MGNREGA)

This Act replaced the MGNREGA rural job law with a new guaranteed-employment law.

  • Full form → Viksit Bharat – Guarantee for Rozgar and Ajeevika Mission (Gramin) Act, 2025 | Ministry of Rural Development
  • Bill introduced in Lok Sabha 16 Dec 2025 (Shivraj Singh Chouhan) → passed → President's assent Dec 2025
  • Repeals MGNREGA 2005 | in force from 1 Jul 2026 (MGNREGA provisions continued during the interim)
  • Guarantee raised 100 → 125 days of wage employment per rural household per financial year
  • Focus on durable infrastructure and digital governance; existing e-KYC job cards stay valid until new cards are issued

How it works → Rural household registers → demand for work → employment within the guarantee of 125 days → wages paid → assets created (infrastructure) → payment in lieu if work is not provided.

Memory tricks

"100 to 125", "2005 to 2025", "1 July 2026".

★ PM Viksit Bharat Rozgar Yojana (PM-VBRY)

This scheme gives incentives linked to new jobs created, for both employers and first-time workers.

  • Earlier called ELI (Employment Linked Incentive) | Cabinet approval 1 Jul 2025 | effective 1 Aug 2025 | announced by PM on 15 Aug 2025 | Ministry of Labour and Employment, via EPFO
  • Outlay ₹99,446 crore | aim → 3.5 crore jobs (1.92 crore first-timers) | jobs created from 1 Aug 2025 to 31 Jul 2027
  • First-time employees (salary up to ₹1 lakh) → one month's EPF wage, up to ₹15,000, in two instalments after 6 and 12 months (second needs a financial literacy programme)
  • Employers → up to ₹3,000 a month per additional employee; extra bonus for manufacturing
Memory tricks

"99,446 crore, 3.5 crore jobs, 15,000 first pay".

★ Four Labour Codes (in force 21 Nov 2025)

The four Labour Codes merge many older labour laws into four, covering wages, industrial relations, social security and workplace safety.

CodeLaws merged
Code on Wages, 20194 (Payment of Wages, Minimum Wages, Payment of Bonus, Equal Remuneration)
Industrial Relations Code, 20203 (Industrial Disputes, Trade Unions, Standing Orders)
Code on Social Security, 20209 (including EPF Act, ESI Act, Maternity Benefit, Gratuity)
OSH Code, 2020 (Occupational Safety, Health and Working Conditions)13 (including Factories Act, Mines Act)
  • 4 + 3 + 9 + 13 = 29 central labour laws replaced | Ministry of Labour and Employment | all four notified in force on 21 Nov 2025, some provisions to be phased in
  • Coverage extended to unorganised, gig and platform workers
Memory tricks

"4-3-9-13 = 29": Wages 4, IR 3, Social Security 9, OSH 13.

☆ EPFO and the wage ceiling
  • EPFO (Employees' Provident Fund Organisation): under the Ministry of Labour and Employment; Employees' Provident Funds and Miscellaneous Provisions Act 1952
  • Three schemes: EPF (provident fund), EPS (Employees' Pension Scheme, 1995), EDLI (Employees' Deposit Linked Insurance, 1976)
  • Sept 2026: Cabinet raised the mandatory-coverage wage ceiling ₹15,000 → ₹25,000 a month (₹15,000 since Sept 2014); about 51 lakh more employees covered; annual Government outgo about ₹11,339 crore; effective 17 Sep 2026

What is the new EPFO wage ceiling for mandatory coverage?

Why it exists

Formal and informal workers require statutory retirement savings, family survivor protection, and social safety nets to protect household livelihoods against disability and old age.

How it works

Central boards administer mandatory employer-employee contributions across provident and pension accounts, setting annual interest declarations and operating digital claim settlement portals.

More fixed facts

  • EPFO 239th CBT (chair Mansukh Mandaviya): 8.25% interest recommended for FY 2025-26; new EPF Scheme 2026, EPS 2026 and EDLI Scheme 2026; inoperative account = no contribution for 3 years after age 55; pilot auto-settlement for balances up to ₹1,000
  • EPFO VISHWAS 2026: one-time settlement of PF damages; till 28 Dec 2026; penalty 0.25%/0.5%/1% per month vs up to 37% p.a.
  • EPF withdrawals directly via UPI to bank accounts from 1 Apr 2026; auto-settlement limit ₹1 lakh → ₹5 lakh; settlement within 3 days

Traps

Do not confuse the Employees Provident Fund statutory retirement scheme with state-level welfare cess mechanisms designed for platform and transport gig workers.

In the news

  • EPFO 239th CBT meeting: 8.25% interest for FY 2025-26; new EPF Scheme 2026 draft
  • EPFO VISHWAS 2026 (one-time settlement of damages)
  • EPFO moves to direct benefit transfer via UPI for claim settlements

☆ Operation Flood and the White Revolution
  • Operation Flood launched 13 Jan 1970 by NDDB (National Dairy Development Board, Anand, Gujarat); architect Verghese Kurien, "Milkman of India"
  • Phases: I 1970–80, II 1981–85, III 1985–96
  • Funded by sale of skimmed milk powder and butter oil gifted by the EEC (European Economic Community) through the World Food Programme; Phase II also had a World Bank loan
  • Model: Amul (Kaira District Cooperative Milk Producers' Union, Anand; Tribhuvandas Patel) → a national milk grid
  • India became the world's largest milk producer in 1998, passing the US

How it works → farmer stores produce in a WDRA-accredited warehouse → gets an e-NWR (electronic negotiable warehouse receipt, Warehousing Act 2007) → trades on e-NAM → can pledge the receipt for finance.

In PLI schemes the Government pays firms an incentive on extra sales they make in India, to encourage manufacturing.

★ Cooperatives: International Year, policy and reforms

OtherWhite Revolution 2.0 (milk procurement +50% in 5 years); Sahakar Taxi (first cooperative mobility initiative, capital ₹300 crore)
Tax reliefSurcharge on ₹1–10 crore income 12% → 7%; MAT 18.5% → 15%; PACS cash limit per member ₹20,000 → ₹2 lakh
  • NCDC (National Cooperative Development Corporation, set up 1963) is the nodal financier of cooperatives; the Ministry of Cooperation was created in 2021

Which body is the nodal financier of cooperatives, and when was the National Cooperation Policy launched?

★ Beti Bachao Beti Padhao (BBBP)

Beti Bachao Beti Padhao is a campaign to improve the child sex ratio and to promote girls' education.

ItemKey facts
Launch22 Jan 2015
MinistryWomen and Child Development; Health (data) and Education are partners
AimRaise the child sex ratio; change mindsets about the girl child; girls\' education and empowerment
FundsAllocated to districts by sex-ratio-at-birth (SRB) status, using 2020-21 HMIS data
2021Merged into Mission Shakti (now a component of its SAMBAL vertical)
15th Finance Commission periodExtended to all districts; activities focused on behaviour change, so beneficiaries cannot be counted
SRB (HMIS)918 (2014-15) → 929 (2024-25)
Girls\' secondary GER (UDISE)75.51% (2014-15) → 83.4% (2025-26)
  • Related: PM Matru Vandana Yojana (since 1 Jan 2017; ₹5,000 for the first child via DBT, ₹6,000 for a second girl child); ₹20,446 crore paid to about 4.35 crore women up to 15 Jul 2026
  • Criticism: 54% of spending went to media and advocacy; only 18% of the ₹222 crore allocated for 2022-23 was used by Dec 2022; a July 2024 paper found no significant impact on enrolment; budget hard to track after the Mission Shakti merger
  • Sukanya Samriddhi Yojana was launched with it on the same date (see the savings-schemes card)

When was BBBP launched, and under which umbrella scheme does it now sit?

Memory tricks

B-B-B-P = 2015 → 2021 → 2024-25: launch (2015) → merged into Mission Shakti (2021) → SRB 929 (2024-25).

918 → 929: "9-1-8 to 9-2-9" (SRB rose by 11 points).

Memory tricks

"Old four": 1881 NI, 1934 RBI, 1938 Insurance, 1949 Banking. Turn of millennium: 1999 FEMA + IRDA; 2002 SARFAESI + PMLA + Competition. 2013: Companies + PFRDA.

Why it exists

  • Fragmented debt recovery laws enabled extensive promoter delays and severe corporate asset value destruction during distress.
  • A consolidated insolvency architecture preserves distressed business enterprise value through rapid market-based resolution.

How it works

  • Operational or financial creditors file an insolvency application before the designated company law tribunal upon default.
  • The tribunal admits the application, imposes an immediate moratorium, and vests management powers in an insolvency professional.
  • A committee of creditors evaluates commercial resolution plans, approving restructuring or directing liquidation within statutory timelines.

More fixed facts

  • IBC 2016 Insolvency and bankruptcy
  • Admission within 14 days; resolution plan approval 30 days; CIRP revival before liquidation once within 120 days; CoC can replace liquidator (66% vote)
  • IBBI Insolvency professionals and agencies under IBC 2016

How it changed

  • January 2026: Admission within 14 days; resolution plan approval 30 days; CIRP revival before liquidation once within 120 days; CoC can replace liquidator (66% vote)

Traps

  • Management control shifts from the defaulting promoter to creditors immediately upon insolvency admission.
  • Liquidation is pursued strictly as a last resort when viable revival proposals fail within statutory deadlines.

In the news

  • Jan Mar 2026: Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (Amendment) Bill, 2025 (Lok Sabha passed)
☆ Small savings schemes
SchemeYearKey feature
PPF (Public Provident Fund)196815-year tenure; ₹500 to ₹1.5 lakh a year
KVP (Kisan Vikas Patra)1988Money doubles in fixed period
SCSS (Senior Citizens' Savings Scheme)2004Age 60+; 5-year tenure
SSY (Sukanya Samriddhi Yojana)22 Jan 2015Under Beti Bachao Beti Padhao; account till the girl is 10; minimum ₹250, maximum ₹1.5 lakh a year; deposits for 15 years; matures after 21 years; 50% education withdrawal at 18 or Class 10; maximum two girls per family (twins/triplets exception)

Interest rates change every quarter and belong in the monthly notes.

★ Startup India ecosystem framework

In plain words: A national initiative fosters entrepreneurship, provides tax exemptions, streamlines statutory compliance, and facilitates early-stage venture funding for innovative commercial enterprises.

Why it exists

Early-stage innovation ventures face high capital hurdles and regulatory compliance burdens, requiring government ecosystem support to scale high-technology enterprises and create jobs.

How it works

Eligible innovative entities obtain formal department recognition, accessing dedicated fund of funds allocations, credit guarantees, patent facilitation, and startup award platforms.

Fixed facts

  • 16 Jan = National Startup Day; Startup India launched 2016. DPIIT-recognised startups above 2.07 lakh (under 500 in 2014); unicorns 4 → nearly 125; India = 3rd largest startup ecosystem
  • About 50% of startups from Tier II/III cities

Traps

Department recognition provides statutory tax and funding benefits, distinguishing formal innovation startups from standard uncertified small commercial businesses.

In the news

  • Startup India: record recognitions and Fund of Funds 2.0
  • Startup India: 10 years and National Startup Awards 5.0
★ Digital Life Certificate (Jeevan Pramaan)

In plain words: A biometric digital identification facility allows pensioners to submit annual life certificates electronically through facial authentication or fingerprint verification without visiting offices.

Why it exists

Elderly and infirm pensioners historically faced physical travel hurdles and bureaucratic delays when visiting bank branches or post offices annually to verify survival.

How it works

Using smartphone cameras or biometric scanners connected to national identity databases, pensioners complete instant facial authentication to generate digitally confirmed life certificates.

Fixed facts

  • By DoPPW (Ministry of Personnel); 2,000 cities and districts; biggest so far; "Utsav" mode with health check-ups, Aadhaar updates, doorstep service, mega camps
  • Target 2.30 crore DLCs, of which 1.50 crore face-authenticated (MeitY and UIDAI support; Android and iOS); 4th campaign → 1.54 crore DLCs (91 lakh+ face-authenticated)

Traps

Digital life certification eliminates physical attendance requirements at disbursement counters, but physical branch submission remains an available fallback.

In the news

  • 5th nationwide Digital Life Certificate campaign, 1–30 November 2026
★ Viksit Bharat - G RAM G rural employment framework

In plain words: A rural employment mission framework guarantees statutory wage employment for rural households, providing dedicated days of work on community water security and infrastructure projects.

Why it exists

Rural agricultural households face seasonal unemployment and income volatility, necessitating legally guaranteed wage work to sustain rural livelihoods and create productive assets.

How it works

Financed jointly by union and state governments, the program guarantees designated days of manual wage employment per household, focusing on water conservation and rural connectivity assets.

Fixed facts

  • Centrally sponsored, normative funding: Centre–state 60:40; 90:10 for NE and Himalayan states; 100% Centre for UTs without legislature; annual need ₹1,51,282 crore (Centre ₹95,692.31 crore)
  • States can notify up to 60 no-work days a year (sowing, harvest); unemployment allowance after 15 days, state liability; wages weekly, max fortnight

Traps

The framework expands guaranteed rural employment to one hundred twenty-five days annually, up from the historical statutory guarantee of one hundred days.

In the news

  • Viksit Bharat - G RAM G bill replacing MGNREGA
★ Critical and Strategic Minerals framework

In plain words: A comprehensive policy and statutory architecture governs the exploration, mining concessions, processing subsidies, and strategic reserves of minerals essential for national energy transitions.

Why it exists

Clean energy technologies and advanced manufacturing depend on secure supplies of rare earths and critical minerals, which face severe geopolitical supply disruptions.

How it works

The framework amends mining statutes to empower union auctions of offshore and strategic minerals, funds processing infrastructure, and designates specialized industrial processing corridors.

Fixed facts

  • Notified under Section 11C, MMDR Act 1957: "Coal" now reads "Coal, including Coking Coal"; coking coal added to Part D (critical and strategic minerals)
  • Scheme for Sintered Rare Earth Permanent Magnets: ₹7,280 crore, 6,000 MTPA (Cabinet Nov 2025); National Critical Mineral Mission approved 29 Jan 2025; Budget 2026-27: Rare Earth Corridors in Odisha, Kerala, Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu

Traps

Statutory authority to auction critical and strategic mineral concessions rests directly with the union government rather than state mining departments.

In the news

  • Coking coal notified as Critical and Strategic Mineral
  • Solar capacity and rare earths
★ Prime Minister Employment Generation Programme

In plain words: A flagship credit-linked subsidy program finances micro-enterprises across manufacturing and service sectors to generate self-employment opportunities in rural and urban areas.

Why it exists

Unemployed youth and traditional rural artisans lack capital and margin money to establish small enterprises, requiring subsidized bank-financed credit support.

How it works

Entrepreneurs contribute small equity margins to secure bank loans, receiving government margin money subsidies credited directly after successful project setup.

Fixed facts

  • Since FY 2014-15: 8.38 lakh+ units; loans over ₹70,000 crore; subsidy over ₹25,193 crore; about 75 lakh jobs; about 80% rural, 50% SC/ST/OBC, 40% women-owned
  • Released by Jitan Ram Manjhi, Kartavya Bhawan-3, New Delhi → bank loans about ₹3,260 crore; over 1.02 lakh jobs expected

Traps

The subsidy under this credit program operates as margin money deposited in an escrow account, converting to a permanent grant upon satisfactory business operation.

In the news

  • PMEGP: ₹1,000 crore margin money subsidy for 11,333 new micro-enterprises
★ Regional Connectivity Scheme (UDAN)

In plain words: A regional connectivity initiative subsidizes scheduled passenger airline routes to unserved and underserved airports, capping domestic regional airfares through viability gap funding.

Why it exists

Tier two and tier three towns lack scheduled commercial flights due to low initial passenger density, requiring route subsidies and airport infrastructure revitalization.

How it works

Aviation authorities auction underserved regional air routes, providing viability gap funding subsidies financed through fuel levies alongside airport fee waivers from state governments.

Fixed facts

  • Ministry of Civil Aviation to sign MoUs with 22 states for next phase of Regional Connectivity Scheme UDAN (Ude Desh ka Aam Nagrik; original launch Oct 2016)
  • Cabinet approved; FY2026-27 to FY2035-36 (10 years); outlay ₹28,840 crore (Ministry of Civil Aviation)

Traps

Viability gap funding on regional routes is co-funded through regional connectivity levies and state concessions, rather than open-ended airline operational grants.

In the news

  • Modified UDAN scheme: MoUs with 22 states
  • Regional Connectivity Scheme – Modified UDAN
★ Shipbuilding Financial Assistance Scheme

In plain words: A financial support policy provides capital assistance subsidies to domestic shipyards constructing commercial vessels to encourage indigenous maritime manufacturing.

Why it exists

Domestic shipyards face severe cost disadvantages against subsidized foreign competitors, necessitating production subsidies to promote domestic maritime fabrication.

How it works

Shipbuilding firms receive graded financial assistance percentages on the contract value of vessels delivered, encouraging domestic procurement and advanced green ship construction.

Fixed facts

  • Extended up to 2036 with revised outlay ₹24,736 crore to support domestic shipbuilding

Traps

Assistance is disbursed directly to domestic yards upon confirmed construction milestones, rather than providing operational subsidies to shipping lines.

In the news

  • SBFAS 2.0

Headquarters are easier to remember in groups: for example Washington DC (IMF, World Bank) and Geneva (WTO, WHO, ILO).

Memory tricks bars hold shortcuts for the longer lists.

"Banks in Asia": ADB = Manila (A for Asia, M for Money), AIIB = Beijing, NDB = Shanghai.

★ OPEC, OPEC+ and the UAE exit

OPEC is a group of oil-producing countries that coordinate how much oil they produce; the UAE left it in 2026.

  • UAE exit → announced 28 Apr 2026; effective 1 May 2026 from OPEC and OPEC+ after a review of its production policy and capacity; the UAE had joined OPEC in 1967
  • OPEC (Organization of the Petroleum Exporting Countries) → founded at Baghdad in 1960; founders Iran, Iraq, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, Venezuela; HQ Vienna; had 12 members including the UAE; publishes the World Oil Outlook
  • OPEC+ (Vienna Group, 2016) → OPEC plus 10 non-OPEC states: Azerbaijan, Bahrain, Brunei, Kazakhstan, Malaysia, Mexico, Oman, Russia, South Sudan, Sudan

Which country left OPEC and OPEC+ on 1 May 2026?

Memory tricks

Founders = I-I-K-S-V (Iran, Iraq, Kuwait, Saudi, Venezuela). OPEC+ born 2016.

★ United Nations: principal organs and the Security Council

The UN works through six principal organs, and the Security Council is the one that can make binding decisions; the table gives each.

Principal organDetail
General AssemblyAll 193 members; one vote each; elects non-permanent UNSC members
Security Council (UNSC)15 members: 5 permanent with veto (China, France, Russia, UK, US) + 10 non-permanent
ECOSOCEconomic and Social Council
Trusteeship CouncilSuspended operations in 1994
International Court of JusticeThe Hague, Netherlands; 15 judges
SecretariatHeaded by the Secretary-General; HQ New York
  • UN Charter in force 24 Oct 1945 (UN Day)
  • Non-permanent members: staggered 2-year terms; five elected every year by the General Assembly (secret ballot, two-thirds majority); seats by region: Africa 3, Asia-Pacific 2, Latin America and Caribbean 2, Western Europe and Others 2, Eastern Europe 1
  • 2026–27 members: Bahrain, Colombia, DR Congo, Latvia, Liberia (Latvia first time); 2025–26: Denmark, Greece, Pakistan, Panama, Somalia
Memory tricks

UNSC = 5 + 10 = 15; veto only for the P5 (C-F-R-U-U: China, France, Russia, UK, US).

7.4 Environment conventions

☆ Ramsar Convention and India's wetland sites
  • Ramsar Convention: adopted 1971 at Ramsar, Iran; in force 1975; India joined 1982
  • India has 103 Ramsar sites across 28 States and UTs (as of 15 Oct 2026): latest additions Sukhna Wetland (Chandigarh) and Sauj Jheel (Uttar Pradesh); Glaw Lake became Arunachal Pradesh's first (3 Aug 2026)
  • Most sites: Tamil Nadu 20, then Uttar Pradesh 14; largest site: Sundarban Wetland (West Bengal), about 4,230 sq km

The count changes every few months; always check the latest figure in the monthly notes.

Memory tricks

Ramsar 71 → 75 → 82: adopted, in force, India joins.

7.5 Climate agreements, bodies and India's framework

★ UNFCCC, Kyoto and Paris: the climate treaty ladder

Climate action runs through a series of treaties, each building on the last; this ladder gives the year, the treaty and what it decided.

YearEventKey facts
1992UNFCCC adoptedUnited Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change; signed at the Rio Earth Summit; in force 21 Mar 1994; 197 Parties; secretariat Geneva → Bonn (Aug 1996)
1995COP1Berlin
1997Kyoto ProtocolAdopted at COP3, Kyoto; in force 16 Feb 2005
2002COP8New Delhi (India's only COP); Delhi Declaration on developing countries' needs and technology transfer
2007COP13Bali Road Map and Bali Action Plan
2009COP15Copenhagen Accord; up to $30 billion fast-start finance for 2010–12
2010COP16Cancun: Green Climate Fund, Technology Mechanism, Cancun Adaptation Framework
2013COP19Warsaw International Mechanism for Loss and Damage
12 Dec 2015COP21 ParisParis Agreement adopted by 196 Parties; in force 4 Nov 2016; "well below 2°C", pursue 1.5°C; NDCs on a 5-year cycle
2021COP26Glasgow: India announces net-zero by 2070
2023COP28Dubai: first Global Stocktake (every 5 years)
2025COP30Belem (Nov 2025); India joined the TFFF as an observer
2026COP31Antalya, Türkiye, 9–20 Nov 2026; President Murat Kurum, Negotiations President Chris Bowen; India withdrew its bid for COP33 (2028)

At which COP was the Paris Agreement adopted, and when did it enter into force?

Memory tricks

92 – 97 – 15: UNFCCC, Kyoto, Paris. COP numbers: 1 Berlin, 3 Kyoto, 8 Delhi, 13 Bali, 15 Copenhagen, 16 Cancun, 19 Warsaw, 21 Paris, 26 Glasgow, 28 Dubai, 30 Belem, 31 Antalya.

★ Climate bodies and funds: IPCC, UNFCCC, GCF, ISA

Several global bodies and funds work on climate; this card gives the set-up and purpose of each.

BodyKey facts
IPCC (Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change)Set up 1988 by WMO (World Meteorological Organization) and UNEP (UN Environment Programme); secretariat Geneva; Nobel Peace Prize 2007 (shared with Al Gore); seventh assessment cycle began 2023
IPCC assessment reportsAR1 1990; AR2 1995; AR3 2001; AR4 2007; AR5 2013–14; AR6 2021–23 (Synthesis Report Mar 2023)
UNFCCC secretariatBonn, Germany
Green Climate FundCreated at COP16 Cancun (2010) to channel climate finance; NaBFID has been approved as a GCF accredited agency
WMO "State of the Climate 2025"Released 23 Mar 2026: 2025 about 1.43°C above 1850–1900; oceans hold 91% of heat; Earth's energy imbalance at a 65-year high
ISA (International Solar Alliance)HQ Gurugram; conceived at COP21 Paris 2015; "Towards 1000": $1,000 bn by 2030, 1,000 million people, 1,000 GW; the US withdrew in 2026
Memory tricks

IPCC reports: 90, 95, 01, 07, 13, 21. IPCC = Geneva; UNFCCC = Bonn.

★ India's climate framework: NAPCC and NDCs

India's climate plan has a national action plan and pledges (NDCs) made under the Paris Agreement; the table gives each.

ItemKey facts
NAPCC (National Action Plan on Climate Change)Launched 30 Jun 2008 under the Prime Minister's Council on Climate Change; MoEFCC coordinates; 8 original missions + Climate Change and Human Health (9 now): Solar; Enhanced Energy Efficiency; Sustainable Habitat; Water; Sustaining the Himalayan Ecosystem; Green India; Sustainable Agriculture; Strategic Knowledge for Climate Change
INDC / first NDC (2015)Emission intensity of GDP down 33–35% by 2030 (vs 2005); about 40% non-fossil power capacity by 2030; extra carbon sink 2.5–3 bn t CO₂e
Updated NDC (2022)Emission intensity −45% by 2030; LT-LEDS 2022 → net-zero 2070 (announced at COP26, 2021)
NDC 2031–35 (Cabinet approved; submitted to UNFCCC)Emission intensity −47% by 2035; 60% non-fossil installed capacity; carbon sink 3.5–4.0 bn t CO₂e; basis: first Global Stocktake and CBDR-RC (Common but Differentiated Responsibilities and Respective Capabilities)
ProgressIntensity cut 36% (2005–2020); non-fossil share 52.57% (Feb 2026); 2030 targets of 33–35% intensity and 40% non-fossil already met
OtherLiFE; Ek Ped Maa Ke Nam; Green Hydrogen Mission (2023; 5 MMT by 2030); Net Zero Portal and NAPCC portal launched Sept 2026
Kigali AmendmentIndia ratified Sept 2021 (in force 26 Dec 2021); HFC phase-down from 2032: 10% → 20% (2037) → 30% (2042) → 85% (2047); World Ozone Day 16 Sep

What are India's NDC targets for 2035, and when was the NAPCC launched?

Memory tricks

Intensity cut: 35 (2015) → 45 (2022) → 47 (2035). Non-fossil capacity: 40 (2030, already met) → 60 (2035). Sink: 2.5–3 → 3.5–4 bn t. Net-zero 2070.

☆ Carbon markets and trade: CCTS, PACM, CBAM
  • CCTS (Carbon Credit Trading Scheme) notified 2023; GEI (Greenhouse Gas Emission Intensity) targets are legally binding per unit of product
  • First notification (Oct 2025) → 282 entities in aluminium, cement, chlor-alkali, pulp and paper; 13 Jan 2026 → 208 more (petroleum refineries, petrochemicals, textiles, secondary aluminium) → about 490 entities
  • Indian Carbon Market Portal launched Mar 2026 at Prakriti 2026 (BEE, Bureau of Energy Efficiency, conference); 9 notified methodologies
  • PACM (Paris Agreement Crediting Mechanism, Article 6.4) → first credits issued (a clean-cooking project in Myanmar)
  • EU CBAM (Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism) → carbon tax on steel, cement, aluminium, fertilisers, electricity and hydrogen imports; transitional phase from 2023; fully in force 1 Jan 2026
  • UK CBAM → recognises India's CCTS for carbon-tax relief (Sept 2026)
  • MISHTI (Mangrove Initiative for Shoreline Habitats and Tangible Incomes) → launched 5 Jun 2023; 540 sq km; 2023-28; funded about 70% from CAMPA plus MGNREGS

What is CBAM, and which sectors does it cover?
